# 🤖 database, proofs that something good eventually happens
(authored by agents unless marked 🧑)

226 sources, best-read first, then newest first. Generated from `sources.jsonl`; edit the reviews' notes, not this page.

- APR safety/liveness framework (2026)
    - title: Abstract Framework for All-Path Reachability Analysis toward Safety and Liveness Verification (Full Version)
    - what: Reformulates all-path reachability (APR) proof rules for abstract reduction systems so they correspond one-to-one with LCTRS rules, adapts cyclic APR proofs, reduces safety properties to partial APR validity, and introduces total validity that quantifies over finite and infinite execution paths for liveness. Defines DVP+ and a complete disproof rule DVP-bottom, proves equivalence with the original DVP, and formalizes finite APR pre-proofs, bud/companion links and proof graphs. Safety APR predicates characterize non-reachability of error states, illustrated with Peterson mutual exclusion. For liveness, Theorem 6.3 proves that an APR proof graph is acyclic iff the APR predicate is totally valid; the Peterson starvation-freedom example obtains an acyclic proof. Appendices establish decidability under finite reachable closure and detail limitations of reduction and cyclic generalization.
    - number: DVP and DVP+ define the same partially valid predicates
    - number: partial validity considers finite maximal paths; total validity considers finite and infinite paths
    - number: Theorem 6.3: acyclic proof graph iff total validity for a partially valid APR proof
    - number: APR proof/disproof decidability when reachable closure finite and side conditions decidable
    - limits: ARS reductions may be nondeterministic; partial validity alone ignores infinite paths and is insufficient for liveness. Total-validity criterion assumes a partially valid APR proof and its finite proof graph; decidability additionally needs finite reachable closure and decidable emptiness/intersection/runnability. Current cyclic buds must match companions exactly, the full cut rule is omitted, and process fairness/generalized companions are future work. Safety reduction uses error sets and normal-form conditions.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `apr-safety-liveness-framework-2026`

    - title: Lawyer: Modular Obligations-Based Liveness Reasoning in Higher-Order Impredicative Concurrent Separation Logic
    - what: Lawyer proves fair termination (every fairly scheduled run finishes) in Rocq/Iris. It uses a logical transition system of obligations. - LIFT loop-bound termination verification (2026)
    - title: LLM-Guided Loop Bound Generation for Program Termination Verification
    - what: Introduces LIFT, a guess-and-check termination framework in which LLMs generate conjunctive or lexicographic loop bounds and a formal validator proves them or refutes them. A candidate bound b(V) is converted to a safety query by adding a counter initialized with b and asserting cnt>0 at each loop head. Constant bounds use bounded unrolling; symbolic bounds use ICE-DT or LLM invariant learners plus Boogie/SMT. If invariant proving fails, bounded violation analysis searches for a reachable counterexample state and feeds it back, while timeout and syntax diagnostics steer later prompts. Progressive generation starts with simple conjunctive bounds and escalates to lexicographic tuples; constant-offset correction and k-induction are included.
    - number: 171 benchmarks
    - number: 152 solved with Gemini
    - number: 139 solved with DeepSeek
    - number: 19 unsolved
    - limits: A valid over-approximate loop bound implies termination, but bounded violation analysis can miss violations after depth m and then treats the witness as potentially spurious. The framework is presented for one loop and says it generalizes to multiple loops. It relies on LLM inference, ICE-DT invariant quality, Boogie/SMT arithmetic, fixed iteration/time budgets, and syntactic restrictions excluding division/nonlinear operators. Nineteen of 171 benchmarks remain unsolved: ten lacked precise bounds and nine exceeded validator capability.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `lift-loop-bound-termination-verification-2026`

- Pischke-Yoshida top-down bottom-up (2026)
    - title: Top-down = Bottom-up: Sound and Complete Characterisations of Liveness by Multiparty Global Protocols
    - what: Develops a principal global-type synthesis algorithm from safe-and-live local typing contexts and proves that top-down global-type projection and bottom-up local verification characterize exactly the same live multiparty sessions (Ptop = Pbot) under precise synchronous subtyping, balanced global types, and coinductive full-merging projection. Beyond the headline equivalence, the paper gives operational semantics for processes, local contexts, and global types; a deterministic fair round-robin scheduler that linearizes independent interactions; termination and balancedness proofs for synthesis; a combined projection/subtyping relation establishing trace inclusion and principality; and a complexity bound O(n^2 b |Delta|^n) for graph synthesis. The Monte Carlo MapReduce example shows why subsumption removes an unused crash branch. The implementation includes four projection algorithms, process/local inference, safety/liveness checking, and global synthesis, and benchmarks literature protocols including MapReduce, independent workers, rings, wallets, e-voting, and binary counters.
    - number: Ptop = Pbot
    - number: PSPACE-complete synchronously
    - number: undecidable in the asynchronous semantics
    - number: O(n^2 b |Δ|^n) graph synthesis bound
    - limits: Assumes synchronous session semantics, finite guarded recursive local types, finite labels, safe-and-live input contexts, fair scheduling, precise synchronous subtyping, and balanced global types. Process-level liveness is undecidable, so bottom-up checks context properties that soundly imply it; synchronous context checking is PSPACE-complete and asynchronous liveness is undecidable. Synthesis can incur exponential syntactic expansion because recursive syntax cannot share branch subterms; benchmarks show stack overflow/timeouts on largest coinductive-full cases. Tool results cover representative examples rather than production deployments.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- REAL Ramsey elimination (2026)
    - title: Fast Ramsey Quantifier Elimination in LIRA (with applications to liveness checking)
    - what: Presents REAL (Ramsey Elimination for Arithmetic Logic), a solver-agnostic implementation eliminating Ramsey quantifiers from existential LIA, LRA, and LIRA formulas, with SMT-LIB extension and Alchemist translation from FASTer/Armoise reachability relations. The resulting existential formulas can be solved by standard SMT solvers to check liveness/termination. The paper defines the infinite-clique semantics and gives a worked arithmetic elimination argument using arithmetic progression witnesses. REAL adds SMT-LIB parsing/export, formula sharing and normalization, and compact Boolean/theory encodings. The FASTer pipeline verifies McCarthy91 termination and checks liveness of sliding-window, Lamport bakery, and cache-coherence protocols (SYNAPSE, Berkeley, Dragon, futurbus, MOESI). On eight pipeline benchmarks, REAL elimination/solving times and formula sizes are reported; parameterized integer/real families scale near-linearly in elimination, with solving dominating, and comparison to the prototype shows orders-of-magnitude speedups.
    - number: eight liveness benchmarks
    - number: dimensions 10, 100, 1000
    - number: McCarthy91 REAL 7.834s elimination and 853.53ms solving
    - number: futurbus FAST 125.95s and REAL 28.570s
    - limits: Ramsey elimination applies to existential linear arithmetic; liveness claims depend on FASTer computing a sound reachability relation and on the encoded fairness/model assumptions. The Lamport bakery starvation check fails without fairness assumptions. Mixed LIRA has substantial overhead because integer and real variables are separated; large benchmark solving times reach hundreds of seconds. The paper does not provide completeness beyond the supported arithmetic fragment or direct comparison on identical prototype benchmarks.

- Robot supervisor synthesis audit (2026)
    - title: Realizability Is Not Enough: Encoding, Liveness, and Auditing of Synthesized Robot Supervisors
    - what: Builds an open-source ROS2/FlexBE pipeline that generates capability-based GR(1) specifications, performs well-separation analysis, synthesizes with slugs, audits explicit Mealy strategies, merges equivalent states with a proof, and emits executable HFSM supervisors. Four domains compare one-hot versus enumerated capability encodings and Fair-Outcome versus System-Goal liveness. Formalizes activation/outcome protocols and pending memory, explains how Fair-Outcome assumptions can be falsified by system-controlled retry bookkeeping, and introduces a post-synthesis explicit-state auditor. The auditor is sound and complete for protocol violations, deadlocks, bounded-failure violations and goal-unreachable SCC traps under completed exploration. A reducer is proven to preserve core capability output traces while explicitly documenting that pending propositions and full GR(1) winning status need not be preserved. Experiments expose ordering sensitivity and the gap between realizability, structural progress and executable deployment.
    - number: Two-Rivers one-hot raw 37 states and reduced 23; enumerated raw 26 and reduced 23
    - number: P0 2FP/3FP: all 44 trials flagged GOAL_UNREACHABLE_TRAP
    - number: P1 one-hot System-Goal 25,841.977 s versus enumerated 235.193 s in reported fixed ordering
    - number: Crazyflie System-Goal: one-hot 19.730 s and 76.7 reduced states; enumerated 9.275 s and 106 reduced states
    - limits: Results are for the GR(1) fragment, slugs/CUDD backend, sequential one-active-capability protocol, finite explicit strategies, and specified goal labels. Well-separation diagnoses game-level assumption falsification but is not a deployment certificate; the auditor is not a general fairness/liveness verifier and leaves SCCs with optional exits unflagged. Reduction preserves capability-level traces, not all auxiliary propositions. Physical correctness and indefinitely failing capabilities remain outside guarantees.

- Rocq MPST liveness (2026)
    - title: Formally Verified Liveness with Multiparty Session Types in Rocq
    - what: Reports Rocq mechanization of safety, deadlock freedom and liveness for a synchronous multiparty session calculus. Uses coinductive local/global type trees, process-oriented subtyping, plain-merge projection and association of local environments to balanced, well-formed, projectable global types. Operational correspondence plus induction on finite grafting contexts proves associated environments live on fair paths; session typing transfers guarantees to processes. Authors report about14K added Rocq lines, with public/archived source and classical choice/excluded-middle assumptions explicitly used in path construction. Records exact balancedness, well-formedness and projectability standing assumptions; asymmetric label matching under subtyping; environment versus session liveness quantifiers; mixed fixed-point path encoding; graft-height proof and its constructive_indefinite_description use; fair-session-path construction with excluded_middle_informative; stuck-recursion definition repair; precise output safety and synchronous reactive-semantics limits. Earlier record omitted axioms and conflated environment path guarantees with the existential session definition.
    - number: About 14K lines of Rocq
    - limits: Simple synchronous, rendezvous communication calculus, not asynchronous network queues. Guarded recursion, finite nonempty labeled branch sets, conditionals, sends/receives and inactive processes; values/expressions include bool/int/nat and nondeterministic choice. No crashes, Byzantine behavior, loss, timeout or network implementation are modeled. Subsorting is reflexive with nat≤int. Communication payloads are typed but do not encode arbitrary dependent data properties. Processes/sessions are inductive syntax, recursive variables encoded with natural indices; local/global types are possibly infinite coinductive trees corresponding to guarded recursive syntax. Continuations are lists of optional branches: label index k exists only for Some, not None/out-of-bounds. Raw Rocq constructors allow lists without valid branches; environment well-formedness tctx_wf imposes nonempty well-formed local trees. Participant maps use natural-number keys, disjoint domains for composition, and MMaps extensional equality M.Equal. Standing assumptions after Remark3.8: global types satisfy wfgC, encompassing inherited well-formedness/balancedness, and projectableA. Balancedness: for every subtree there is a finite depth k by which each participant in that subtree appears on every path long enough or ending. This is a structural protocol requirement, not scheduler fairness. Theorem5.8/6.9 concise statements suppress these standing conditions; arbitrary global trees or any collection of locally typed processes do not receive the theorem. Projection is coinductive plain merge. A participant at a root send/receive gets corresponding local branch; an uninvolved participant must project all continuations to the same local type. Absent participants project end. Stronger full merge is future work; projectability is assumed rather than proved for every global syntax. Process-oriented subtype may receive more labels and wider input sorts, or send fewer labels and narrower output sorts; continuations subtype coinductively. Association Γ⊑G requires every participant of G in Γ with Γ(p) subtype of its projection; any extra environment participant is absent or end. Local environments have individual send/receive readiness and synchronized reductions requiring compatible sorts. Global reduction permits root branch selection and disjoint communication in all branch continuations; printed GR-Ctx also requires both root participants in every continuation. Association is more than matching syntax, and must be preserved under reduction. Theorem4.8 soundness: if G can communicate between p/q under labelℓ, Γ can communicate between the same participants with possibly different labelℓprime, and both resulting objects remain associated. It does not simulate every exact global choice. Theorem4.9 completeness: each Γ synchronized step has the same-label G step preserving association. Counterexample in Remark4.10 has a sender restricted toℓ0 while the global type allowsℓ0/ℓ1. Calling this bidirectional same-label bisimulation would be inaccurate. Environment safety coinductively requires every mutually ready sender/receiver to synchronize using the sender label and compatible payload sorts, preserved under all synchronized reductions. It does not require a counterpart ready for every unilateral request; safety is distinct from progress. Example5.2 int sender/nat receiver is unsafe because int is not a subtype of nat. Associated environments satisfy Theorem5.3. Paths may be finite or infinite. Fairness Definition5.4: whenever synchronized pair(p,q) is enabled at positionn, some later communication of that same ordered pair occurs, possibly with a different label. It is pair fairness, not fairness of every branch label. Live paths require every unilateral send or receive request eventually gets its corresponding pair communication. An environment is live if every fair path from every reachable reduct is live. The universal condition is over fair paths only; unfair starvation schedules are excluded. Paco encodes coinductive predicates as greatest fixed points of parameterized inductive relations. Paths are cosequences with conil/cocons; eventual is inductive least fixed point, alwaysCG coinductive greatest fixed point. This avoids explicit index bookkeeping and Rocq native cofix guardedness limitations. These are proof representations, not a runtime scheduler or finite model checker. Balancedness supplies a finite p-grafting context containing no p, with holes beginning at p-related communication or end. Theorem5.8 first proves liveness for direct projections (actual Rocq uses equivalent global enabledness predicate), using height of p-grafting versus q-grafting as a decreasing induction measure until the pair becomes enabled, then fairness forces communication. Proof transfers to subtyped Γ by associating paths pointwise and preserving transition labels. Detailed grafting/projection definitions and inherited results are referred to [16]/[20]. Theorem5.8 path lifting extracts witnesses of Theorem4.9 from Prop to construct a CoFixpoint cosequence, explicitly using constructive_indefinite_description. Theorem6.9 additionally constructs a fair session path using constructive_indefinite_description and excluded_middle_informative. Thus reported machine checking has these logical assumptions; do not describe the whole development as axiom-free or constructive merely because it uses coinduction. Full artifact assumption audit/build was not performed. Reactive semantics folds finite internal conditional evaluation/recursion unfolding into communication transitions using an unfolding relation; no explicit τ-only LTS transitions. Structural congruence rearranges parallel composition/unit. This lets subject reduction match each session communication with exactly one same-label environment step, rather than a weak simulation with silent stuttering. Does not establish equivalence to arbitrary calculi with internal divergence or a concrete distributed runtime. Table2 session rule requires each component process be typed by an environment already associated with a suitable global type. Lemma6.1 preserves typing through unfolding. Theorem6.2 sends every session step to a same-label type step; Theorem6.3 permits a type step to be matched by a same-participant session/type step with potentially different label. Theorem6.5 explicitly guarantees the sender label belongs to the waiting receiver branch set in every reachable unfolded typed session. Theorem6.6 says a typed session either can communicate or every unfolding can further reach all-inactive processes, not global termination. Session liveness Definition6.7 is an existential continuation property at every reachable unfolded send/receive: from that unfolded state there exists a finite reduction sequence reaching the corresponding continuation. It is not syntactically the environment definition quantifying every fair path. Authors identify it as Padovani lock-freedom/lock-freedom under full fairness; Theorem6.9 proves it by choosing a fair session path, lifting it to a fair live environment path, then extracting a finite communication prefix. No bounded latency or unconditional all-schedules termination theorem is claimed. Remark6.8 repairs a prior liveness formulation that started its required continuation at the pre-unfolded state. In stuck sender µX.q!ℓ(0).X with q inactive, the original state coincides with the apparent post-send recursion, allowing zero steps to fake progress. Their definition starts at the unfolded pending-send state, excluding that example. This is a semantic definition correction, not a discovered bug in their checked theorem. Printed Table1 R-comm has p receiving from q but labels the communication(p,q), whereas Definition4.2 and environment/global send rules use(p,q) for p sending to q. Read literally this reverses the sender/receiver order and conflicts with same-label subject reduction for a one-way session. Likely presentation convention/typo; this reading has not checked artifact definitions and does not establish a Rocq inconsistency. Endpoint order should be checked before transcribing the rule into another verifier. Examples4.4/4.7/5.6 use G=p→q:{ℓ0(int).G,ℓ1(int).q→r:ℓ2(int).end}. The infiniteℓ0 path is fair under pair fairness, since p/q always communicate, but r waits forever. G is projectable and associated yet unbalanced. This is an explicit negative example outside standing balancedness, demonstrating why association alone is insufficient for liveness. The finiteℓ0,ℓ1,ℓ2 path terminates and is fair/live. Reported contribution around14K lines is added code on top of prior subject-reduction development [16], not necessarily whole artifact size. Proofs of subject reduction/deadlock freedom were rewritten for environment-based typing; operational correspondence, environment safety/liveness new. No checking-time, memory, theorem counts, admitted-proof count, dependency audit, industrial protocol benchmark or verified endpoint extraction measured here. Paper links GitHub omerskeskin/mpstlive and an SWH archive; reader has not built or audited artifact. First mechanization claims are author-qualified to their knowledge and not independently surveyed here. No completeness theorem saying every live session typable is provided. Fair termination is explicitly stronger; stronger liveness under instruction fairness or justness may require different global types/projection. Full merge, other calculi and weak simulation support are future work, not delivered extensions. Crash-stop/asynchronous association results mentioned are other work. Explicit declaration says no AI-generated text or code is present. This is authors stated provenance, not an independent audit. No Verus, Rust or TLA+ implementation delivered. For the human goal the value is a checked metatheory template with explicit fairness, logical-choice, model and correspondence assumptions; connecting it to verified Rust/network execution requires additional refinement and runtime proofs. Entire supplied21-page conference PDF read; no separate arXiv2605.23633 or cited background paper read in this batch. Existing multi-source record replaced, preserving aliases and archived original rather than treating prior abstract/incomplete text as a fresh whole read. Confidence about mechanized results is based on paper and reported source availability, not a fresh Rocq build.

- SoK consensus (2026)
    - title: Systematization of Knowledge: Formal Verification of Consensus Protocols
    - what: A survey that sorts consensus verification into model checking, deductive proofs, code proofs and hybrids.
    - number: over 20 verified consensus protocols
    - number: only about 30% of surveyed works address liveness
    - number: Matrix covers 20+ protocols in abstract and over 30 in conclusion
    - number: Only ≈30% of surveyed works address liveness (§5)

- Structural Liveness of Conservative Petri Nets (2026)
    - what: Proves EXPSPACE-completeness of structural liveness for conservative and structurally bounded Petri nets, with doubly-exponential bounds on minimal live markings. The paper models Petri nets as action vectors and develops a proof chain: reversible virtual reachability is represented by bounded linear systems; conservative, structurally bounded and live-bottom-SCC properties are related; dead markings are represented by disjunctions of bounded restricted markings; quasi-dead markings and extractor cases reduce non-liveness to linear-system constraints. Small-solution theorems then yield a 2-exp bound on a live marking, enabling an exponential-space nondeterministic algorithm. A strengthened EXPSPACE lower bound completes hardness for conservative nets and transfers it to structurally bounded nets. The proof extends integer-solution bounds with divisibility constraints.
    - number: The complexity result is theorem-based; no runtime data or benchmark suite.
    - number: Linear-system dimension in the construction is d·2^d; solution norms are bounded by (2+||A||)^{2^{poly(d)}}.
    - number: The upper-bound algorithm guesses a binary-encoded live marking and checks liveness in polynomial space in that encoding.
    - limits: Results concern standard finite-dimensional Petri nets with nonnegative markings and conservative/structurally bounded subclasses; general structural liveness complexity remains open. Bounds are doubly exponential and not practical algorithms. Several technical ingredients are cited from prior work (Rackoff/Leroux), and the proof uses virtual reachability, reversibility and large-component extractors. No implementation or experimental evaluation is reported.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `structural-liveness-of-conservative-2026`
- SysMoBench (2026)
    - title: SysMoBench: Evaluating AI on Formally Modeling Complex Real-World Systems
    - what: A benchmark of eleven concurrent and distributed systems. An AI writes a TLA+ model of each. The model is scored on syntax, execution, behavioral match to the code, and expected properties.
    - number: source sizes from 175 lines (PGo dqueue) to 5,360 (ZooKeeper leader election)
    - number: Etcd Raft with basic Sonnet agent: 7.69% conformance, 69.23% invariant correctness, 100% syntax
    - number: liveness properties violated 41.9% vs safety 8.3% (§5, Figure 4c)
    - number: each agent run five times, best output evaluated, at most three repairs
    - limits: Covers only the finite executions and configurations supplied. A model can match buggy code and still fail the desired guarantee. The evaluator uses an LLM for some mappings.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Timers (2026)
    - title: Verifying First-Order Temporal Properties of Infinite-State Systems via Timers and Rankings
    - what: Turns a claim about time into a termination problem by adding timers that predict when formulas become true. A human supplies the model, the claim, the implicit ranking, the invariant and finite-set approximations. Z3 checks the generated conditions.
    - number: 17 tasks in Table 2, e.g. Ticket: 2 s, 20 invariant conjuncts, 256 proof tokens
    - number: Paxos: 101 s, 238 tokens; ABP: 4 s, 539 tokens; HRB-Correct: 135 s, 99 tokens; Dijkstra k-State: 64.8 s, 213 tokens
    - number: Ivy proof sizes for six tasks: 407, 521, 860, 446, 593, 748 tokens (Ticket, Paxos, ABP, HRB-Correct, HRB-Relay, Ackermann)
    - number: runtime is Z3 validation on Apple M1 Pro, Z3 4.13.3
    - limits: The guarantees cover first-order transition-system models, not deployed code. Rankings and invariants are not discovered automatically. Sizes count proof tokens, not human effort.

- TLA-Verus (2026)
    - title: TLA-Verus: Generating and Solving Temporal Proof Obligations with LLM Agents
    - what: This work translates existing TLA+ models into Verus temporal proof tasks. The translator is either an algorithm or an LLM writer plus reviewer. It builds on Anvil's temporal logic library, and agents prove the tasks. Checks freeze the task and reject new assumptions or admissions (unproved claims accepted as true).
    - number: 16,459 model/property pairs attempted
    - number: algorithmic: 5,160 usable translations, 3,094 proofs (304 liveness)
    - number: agentic: 12,352 usable translations, 492 proofs in a 500-task sample
    - number: evaluation: 40 paired tasks, ten per category, 32 models from 24 repositories
    - limits: Showing that the implementation refines the model is future work, so a model proof is not a proof about Rust behavior. An LLM reviews whether the translation is faithful, and this is not proved. The dataset and pipeline are unreleased. The runs were not repeated. A Raft-style model was safe only because it could not make progress.

- Transition Invariants Revisited: Termination Witnesses and Their Validation (2026)
    - what: Extends the standard machine-readable proof format that program verifiers hand to each other so it can carry termination proofs. The proof is a transition invariant: a relation between any earlier and later state of a run that must decrease in a well-founded way. Three checkers re-validate such proofs independently of the tool that made them.
    - number: Evaluated on all SV-Benchmarks programs expected to terminate, 900 s CPU and 15 GB per run
    - number: Witnesses validated by at least one validator: CPAchecker 366 of 422 (87%), CPAchecker-TransVer 192 of 200 (96%), UAutomizer-TransVer 375 of 389 (96%), Goblint-TransVer 176 of 286 (62%)
    - number: Validators solved about 40 programs that CPAchecker's own verification mode could not, using other tools' witnesses
    - number: Validation often faster than verification (Wilcoxon p = 2.1e-10 and 1.1e-13 for the two CPAchecker validators on ranking-function witnesses)
    - limits: Termination of sequential C programs only; the authors say "We do not consider other reasons for non-termination, such as deadlocks." One reduction route needs finite state spaces and TransVer has limited support for arrays and structs. Witnesses with many invariants (Goblint) often could not be validated within limits. Not about fairness or liveness of concurrent or distributed systems.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-32537-2_2); [notes: Transition invariants as termination witnesses (2026)](claude_readings.md)
    - record id: `transition-invariants-revisited-termination-2026`
- Verified MCP Gateway (2026)
    - title: Formal Verification of Agentic MLOps Protocols: Model Checking and Liveness Guarantees in Distributed Model Context Protocol Workflows
    - what: A gateway sits between an LLM agent and its tools (Model Context Protocol, MCP, a standard for LLM tool calls). Before each tool call it checks, against a hand-written state machine, that the call keeps three temporal-logic rules true: no deadlock, every GPU that is handed out is eventually returned, and no two agents write the same network folder at once.
    - number: 500 multi-agent training workflow traces, chain depth up to 14 tool calls
    - number: reachable states at depth 14: 196,608 unconstrained vs 672 with guards (99.66% fewer)
    - number: deadlocks: 54.2% (vanilla MCP), 33.6% (ReAct retry), 18.4% (static DAG check), 0.0% (verified gateway)
    - number: task completion 99.4% vs 38.4% for vanilla MCP; wasted GPU hours 0.2% vs 41.8%
    - limits: The state machine, its 12 tools and the three properties are written by hand for one production system; the paper does not say how the 500 traces were produced. The 'liveness' property is enforced by checking one step ahead at run time, so it is a guard, not a proof about all executions; there is no theorem or proof-assistant artifact.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22715686); [notes: Formal Verification of Agentic MLOps Protocols (2026)](claude_readings.md)
    - record id: `verified-mcp-gateway-2026`
- Verifying Graceful Degradation in a Distributed Malware-Detection System with SPIN (2026)
    - what: The authors model, in Promela, the endpoint side of a Bitdefender-style malware-detection system that falls back from a primary server to a legacy server to a weaker local scan, and check six properties written in temporal logic (five safety, one liveness) plus deadlock freedom with the SPIN model checker.
    - number: All six properties hold on the core 1 endpoint x 1 scan model: 6,168 states stored, depth 101, under 1 second (Table 2)
    - number: Streaming model (1 endpoint, 2 scans): 314,527 states; concurrent model (2 endpoints, 1 scan): 22,177,616 states (Table 4)
    - number: 2 endpoints each with 2 scans ran out of memory, so no result
    - number: 4 reachability trap claims all violated, showing the checked behaviors occur (Table 3)
    - limits: Assumes the transport is reliable (no loss or duplication), deadlines are untimed nondeterministic choices, and two detection contracts: the full servers always give the right verdict (A1) and the reduced scan never flags a benign file (A2). Finite instances only; unbounded endpoints and scans need parameterized techniques left to future work. The model is hand-abstracted from the production system and not tied to its code.

    - what: Welder proves, for each controller, that it makes progress and limits its requests. This statement is called the CORE spec. It then combines the proofs of controllers that fit together. The authors verified the ReplicaSet, Deployment, StatefulSet and RabbitMQ controllers.

- Wirehead Rocq proofs (2026)
    - title: Toward Formal Verification of a Lock-Free Distributed Double Buffer Cache
    - what: A master's thesis that proves, in the Rocq proof assistant, six properties of the swap algorithm in Wirehead, a MongoDB-based cache that lets many data generators feed one training job. The author models each MongoDB call as a plain function and the algorithm as a state machine where one process takes one atomic step at a time.
    - number: 4 Rocq files, 3,470 lines (thesis text says 3,475 in one place), 144 proofs, 2 Admitted, 3 axioms (all on the user-supplied chunk function)
    - number: Safety.v 1,339 lines; Liveness.v 1,342 lines; DB.v 513; Algorithm.v 276
    - number: about 800 lines of Safety.v are auxiliary invariants needed only for liveness
    - number: 5 of 6 properties fully checked; progress depends on 2 Admitted lemmas
    - limits: Assumes one MongoDB instance, crash-stop failures only, atomic database calls, and weak fairness of the scheduler; the match between the Rocq model and the Python code is asserted, not proved. Crash transitions and the real timeout-based lock recovery are not modeled.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.57709/138); [notes: Toward Formal Verification of a Lock-Free Distributed Double Buffer Ca](claude_readings.md)

- ChonkyBFT (2025)
    - title: ChonkyBFT: Consensus Protocol of ZKsync
    - what: Presents ChonkyBFT, a partially synchronous committee BFT protocol for ZKsync with one voting round, single-slot finality, quadratic communication, and n≥5f+1. Safety, validity, and partial-synchrony progress are proved by quorum and timeout lemmas. The Quint specification and Apalache symbolic checking found pseudocode type/definition errors and attack paths; an 18-lemma inductive invariant was checked for bounded parameters. A deployment evaluation measures failure-free throughput, latency, resource use, and replication lag.
    - limits: At most f Byzantine replicas with n≥5f+1; authenticated reliable point-to-point channels, secure signatures/hashes, and partial synchrony after GST. Liveness additionally assumes block synchrony, periodic retransmission, bounded clock drift, and eventual correct leaders. Formal Apalache checks restrict to six replicas, at most one fault, three views, bounded message/QC sets, so they are model-level evidence. Performance tests are failure-free and exclude block synchronization.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15380)
    - record id: `chonkybft-2025`

    - what: The paper defines an automaton that recognizes finite traces of a first-order temporal sentence over a background theory. It gives results on expressiveness and closure, and decision procedures that work under conditions.

- Implicit rankings (2025)
    - title: Implicit Rankings for Verifying Liveness Properties in First-Order Logic.
    - what: The method compares old and new states through formulas, instead of computing a number that must shrink (a ranking function). Constructors combine them pointwise, lexicographically, by multiset or permutation, and across a whole domain. Z3 checks the conditions that result. It verifies toy stabilization, a binary counter, token-ring non-starvation, Chang-Roberts leader election, SAT backtracking and abstract CDCL termination. It also verifies Dijkstra's k-, 4- and 3-state stabilization and Ghosh's 4-state stabilization.
    - number: all examples verified within 10 minutes (§6, p.388)
    - number: all examples verified within 10 minutes
    - number: Dijkstra 3-state DomPerm(k=4) creates 18 existential quantifiers
    - number: toy stabilization bound n^2
    - limits: The user supplies how the ranking is composed, the other proof-rule formulas, and sometimes hints for existential witnesses. Domains are finite but unbounded. It does not cover unrestricted infinite domains in full. The review notes found no person-month figures.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.13996); [notes: LVR and implicit ranking functions](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_2_liveness.md); [notes: Ivy, ranking, Coq, and Iris case index](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_2_liveness.md)
    - record id: `implicit-rankings-2025`
- LAW (2025)
    - title: The LAW theorem: Local Reads and Linearizable Asynchronous Replication
    - what: The paper proves that no replicated register can have all three of these: a local read (a read with no messages), linearizability, and a guarantee that operations finish despite async delays and one crash. It designs almost-local reads (batched read validation) in Hermes, ZAB and Raft variants.
    - number: Raft-ALR 2.6x baseline throughput at 2% writes, 1.8x at 5%; up to 156 million requests/s
    - number: ZAB-ALR within 4% of ZAB at 1% writes; Hermes-ALR within 11% at 1% writes
    - number: 5 CloudLab servers, one million keys; reads batched up to 128
    - limits: A human wrote a mathematical proof about a mathematical register (deterministic servers, one active operation each, at most one crash). The correctness of the read algorithms is argued in prose. The Raft baseline relaxes linearizability, so the performance comparison is an upper bound. There is no LLM, no code proof and no person-hours.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://www.vldb.org/pvldb/vol18/p2831-giortamis.pdf); [notes: Added related work: The LAW theorem: Local Reads and Linearizable Asyn](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_19_law_related.md)
    - record id: `law-2025`
- LiDO-DAG (2025)
    - title: LiDO-DAG: A Framework for Verifying Safety and Liveness of DAG-Based Consensus Protocols
    - what: It separates the DAGs that spread data from the ordering step. In Coq, it proves that partially synchronous Narwhal, Bullshark and Sailfish keep agreement, and that they eventually commit vertices built by synchronous processes. Verification revealed a latency improvement for Sailfish.
    - number: two persons over three months for the three protocols (§5, p.203:21)
    - number: Table 2 (spec/proof lines): model 747/1500; Narwhal 2307/9697; Bullshark 603/4343; original Sailfish 270/3479; modified Sailfish 339/3040; some reused
    - number: LiDO-DAG model: spec 747 lines, proof 1,500
    - number: Narwhal: spec 2,307, proof 9,697
    - limits: It covers protocol models only. It does not show that a Rust implementation refines them. It excludes asynchronous probabilistic variants. It needs an extra fairness assumption: each synchronous builder succeeds within a fixed number of time segments. Line counts mix reused and safety proofs with liveness proofs.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Lilo (2025)
    - title: Lilo: A Higher-Order, Relational Concurrent Separation Logic for Liveness
    - what: Lilo is a Coq logic for the liveness of shared-memory programs. It uses obligations (promises to finish) and progress credits. The authors give proofs for a spinlock, a ticket lock, Treiber and elimination stacks, and infinite message passing.

- Neural model checking with invariants (2025)
    - title: Let a Neural Network Be Your Invariant
    - what: For hardware designs, one small neural network is trained to be both an inductive invariant (a set of states that contains all reachable states and is closed under steps; this proves safety) and a ranking function (a number that drops each time something bad could recur; this proves liveness). A solver then checks the network exactly, so a yes answer is a real proof. Training uses a constraint solver (MILP) rather than gradient descent.
    - number: 634-task benchmark: 11 SystemVerilog designs; 206 safety, 219 liveness, 209 safety+liveness tasks
    - number: solved: 190/206 safety (92%), 192/219 liveness (88%), 172/209 safety+liveness (82%)
    - number: nuXmv solved 82% of liveness and 74% of safety+liveness tasks; rIC3 50% and 61%; ABC 36% and 46%; prior neural method NMC'24 79% of liveness
    - number: pure liveness: faster than the best other tool on 66% of tasks, 10x faster on 46%
    - limits: Only finite-state hardware-style systems (no arrays or strings of parametric length, no procedure calls or dynamic data); the benchmark is the authors' own, built from textbook-level designs, so results may not carry to other workloads; relies on sign-activation feed-forward networks.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://research.birmingham.ac.uk/en/publications/fe64a278-7ad4-495e-9751-663b05f077c6); [notes: Let a Neural Network Be Your Invariant (2025)](claude_readings.md)

    - what: A logic in Rocq and Iris, with later-free ghost state (proof-only state without step-indexing), for proving termination and functional correctness of core-Rust programs. The prototype is RustHalt. Examples include Ackermann variants and traversal of a mutable list.

- On Temporal Verification of Stateful P4 Programs (2025)
    - what: Defines P4LTL temporal properties over completed packet processings while retaining register state between packets. Models arbitrary input packets and changing table actions, optionally constrained by symbolic control-plane assumptions. Constructs a Büchi transaction product in which the specification automaton advances only at packet return, reducing violations to fair feasible traces. A proof sketch establishes the abstract correspondence; p4tv implements P4-to-Boogie translation, ghost instrumentation and an Ultimate Automizer extension. Evaluation checks19 temporal tasks on nine programs plus assertion checks/scaling, identifying two naturally occurring property failures and three injected defects. Records exact packet-boundary/old/free-variable semantics, register initialization and control-plane scope, product transitions/acceptance and proof-sketch qualifications, refinement algorithm and implementation trust, all Table2/3 cells, actual versus injected bug distinction, p4v approximation and assertion instrumentation, rapid scaling growth, and source specification/prose inconsistencies including vacuous liveness formula. Separates local P4xos checks from whole Paxos consensus verification.
    - limits: §3: Environment is a serial infinite loop of complete packet processings, persistent registers outside loop, nondeterministic packet fields each iteration and metadata initialized per P4-16. Table applications are nondeterministic action choices. This abstracts a single programmed switch; no proof of interleaved pipeline concurrency, simultaneous register read/modify/write races, multiple switches, queue dynamics or actual hardware refinement is supplied. Packet time is not wall-clock time. §§2/3/7/8: Register initialization is part of each verification configuration. P4NIS failing task9 leaves count unconstrained; successful invariant/assertion examples assume valid/zero initialization or add recovery logic. Persistent arbitrary initial values differ from independently re-randomizing registers every packet. Results under initialization assumptions do not show a real deployment meets those assumptions. §§3.2/4: CPI constrains table keys/actions/parameters, allows symbolic sets of entries and changes between packets. Empty CPI permits every modeled rule. Property checked is assumption implies desired property; it can hold if assumption is impossible. Authors do not report systematic assumption satisfiability/vacuity checks or prove a real controller always enforces CPI. Table2 needs CPI for tasks6,7,8,16 only. §4.1: Predicates are evaluated at packet boundaries: terms normally after processing; old(t) at ingress start after parsing; valid(h) records validity after parsing, hit/apply record occurrence during processing. old(key) and old(para) expressly arbitrary. fwd(t) is egress_spec==t syntactic sugar, so packet-output/architecture correspondence matters. Free variables describe nondeterministic values intended to cover all names; scope/stability must be preserved when translating specifications. §§4/6/7.2: Boundary-only temporal properties can miss transient within-packet violations. Assertion checks use a shadow variable initialized true per packet and capture the assertion at its program point, then check its boundary value. This is an explicit instrumentation workaround; the native P4LTL language still cannot directly observe arbitrary internal events. Correct instrumentation and all relevant visits are part of the trust boundary. §5.1: Product keeps program/specification location pairs, updates specification only on TR(s), uses assume true on other statements, and accepting locations require spec acceptance plus TE/TR boundary. Fair means Büchi acceptance (infinitely recurring accepting states), not automatic network-delivery, scheduler or adversary fairness. Liveness assumptions about infinitely many particular packets are written explicitly in the property. Infinite repeated packet processing is the base execution model. §5.1/Appendix A: Theorem5.1/A.1 is an abstract correspondence with an informal proof sketch, not a mechanized proof of P4 semantics, Boogie translation, instrumenter or executable checker. Projection deletes only true steps imposed by non-return transitions; a return transition labelled true must remain. Appendix switches the named if/only-if directions, and reverse construction retains TE(s)∨TR(s) while operational product advances spec on TR(s) only. A rigorous proof should distinguish statement/boundary indexing and establish infinitely many returns; no concrete tool-unsoundness counterexample claimed. §5.2/6: Algorithm1 reuses counterexample-guided refinement from cited fairness-modulo-theory work: select accepting trace, test feasibility, return genuine trace or remove a generalized set of infeasible traces, finish when none remain. No universal termination/runtime bound or translation correctness theorem provided. Trust includes P4/Boogie modeling, ghost instrumentation, LTL conversion, automata/refinement/feasibility engine and underlying reasoning tools. Code/proofs not run in this reading. §6: Prototype fully supports V1Model but limited TNA/PSA. Authors claim model transferable with more detailed P4 processing; future architecture support is not demonstrated full support. State explosion remains. About10000C++/JavaLOC and selected small benchmark programs do not establish production-scale coverage. §8 pp.228–229: Printed P4NIS liveness consequence is conjunction over ports i of GF(phi_sp -> fwd(i)), under GF(phi_sp). This is weaker than GF(phi_sp AND fwd(i)): infinitely many non-specific packets satisfy every implication regardless of forwarding. Alternating specific/non-specific packets with no fwd(i) satisfies the displayed formula while violating the natural-language goal. This is a semantic observation about the printed specification, not a run of artifact specifications. §4’s earlier liveness example has a different stronger shape. §8: NDN weak-until formula is activated only after a data packet was forwarded; it then forbids repeated data forwarding until a matching forwarded interest. It does not by itself forbid the first unsolicited data forward before that trigger, nor require eventual forwarding because W permits no future interest. Task6’s separate property covers interest-before-data. Universal free hash variable is not a proof of hash collision freedom or data-name authentication. §5.1 Figure5/§7.2 Table3: Figure5 prose says status becomes0 when hdr.cnt<0, while listing/automaton tests>0; record follows code when describing example. Table3 says acceptor stored round strictly less than current round, while adjoining prose uses≤ under zero initialization. Strict< would fail on equal zeros; cannot silently interchange these properties. Printed description/code discrepancies do not establish implementation bugs. §7: Dataset has nine manually selected programs and manually authored properties; artificial bugs inserted in CPI/task8 and code/tasks15,18. Natural failures tasks9,13 are author-reported findings without external maintainer confirmation/production exploit in paper. 14 verified tasks and five counterexamples are outcomes for specified models, not whole programs certified correct. §7.2: Temporal comparison with four prior tools is a support limitation discussion; measured assertion comparison runs bf4 and an approximate p4v implementation from bf4 work because original p4v unavailable. Vera limited user assertions and Aquila unavailable. Spurious counterexamples are relative to chosen persistent-register configuration, not evidence prior tools unsound for their broader nondeterministic model. p4tv is much slower but more precise for these stateful examples. §7.3: Program and specification scaling varied separately on P4NIS ports and acceptor index clauses. Curves grow rapidly; Figure8 about8600s near64ports and Figure9 about24000s near32clauses, approximate visual readings only. Easy false properties return inabout90s for CNT8/16, so falsification speed does not imply comparable proof scaling. No cross-product scalability, latency budget or large production network study. §7: Hardware i5-12400 2.50GHz/32GB; no repeat counts, confidence intervals, error bars or user specification effort measurements given. Main temporal tasks10–1260s; assertion acceptor-safe1701s, so21min maximum applies only Table2. P4xos acceptor/learner predicates are local state/order/vote checks, not an end-to-end Paxos agreement/liveness proof under crash/message-loss/reconfiguration. No Verus/Rust/TLA+ implementation theorem or LLM-agent evaluation. §7.1/Table2 task8: Printed task8 checks property6, data is forwarded only after a corresponding interest. Reported trace receives interest then drops data. A dropped data packet alone cannot violate this only-if safety condition. Preserve author-reported counterexample outcome, but it does not demonstrate violation of the printed property without more trace/specification detail. This inconsistency does not establish tool unsoundness.

- rlive (2025)
    - title: Infinite-State Liveness Checking with rlive.
    - what: rlive is a model checker for liveness of infinite-state transition systems. It searches for counterexamples, learns abstraction predicates and synthesizes ranking functions. The ranking functions rule out loops that cannot run forever.
    - number: 835 liveness verification problems
    - number: timeout 1200 s, 8 GB per instance
    - number: solves 22 more instances than alpha-L2S
    - number: average speedup 6.47x on instances solved by both

- Sound and Complete Witnesses for Template-Based Verification of LTL Properties on Polynomial Programs. (2025)
    - what: Defines existential and universal Büchi ranking functions as witnesses that a finitely branching numerical transition system has one, or only, runs visiting an acceptance set infinitely often. Gives soundness/completeness theorems and an LTL-to-product reduction, with deterministic-Büchi restrictions for direct universal verification. Synthesizes location-wise polynomial witnesses through quantified constraints, a cited Positivstellensatz reduction and SMT solving. Evaluates proof and refutation of four LTL properties on 287 linear and31 nonlinear programs; detailed results show competitive linear coverage and advantages on selected nonlinear tasks. Nonnegative witness values define a recurrent region; every chosen (existential) or enabled (universal) successor preserves this region, decreasing by at least one outside acceptance and allowing a reset at acceptance. Product automaton locations make Büchi membership constant at each synthesized location. Existential synthesis chooses one initial valuation and one suitable enabled successor; universal synthesis covers every initial valuation and successor. Reports invariant support, solver portfolio, excluded benchmark classes, true/false/unknown distinctions, and averages over different solved subsets. The paper’s broad general-LTL claims exceed the directly displayed positive universal-certificate reduction when the formula has no deterministic Büchi automaton.
    - limits: Transition systems have finitely many locations, finitely many real-valued variables and finitely many deterministic-update transitions; finite nondeterministic branching is allowed, unbounded nondeterministic assignment is not. Every state has a successor; a terminal location has an identity self-loop so all runs are infinite. Initial sets may be infinite. Synthesis further requires polynomial updates, guards and atomic constraints. There is no probabilistic transition semantics. LTL verification means all runs satisfy phi; refutation means some run satisfies not phi. Atom valuations need not be independent: x>=0 implies x+1>=0. The product with an NBW accepting not phi gives existential Büchi refutation. A direct universal-Büchi product for proving phi requires a deterministic Büchi automaton for phi; arbitrary LTL languages need not have one. Formal product definition and Lemma1 proof are deferred to [13]. The p.607 remark observes universal truth iff no accepting counterexample exists, then suggests using existential witnesses for general verification. Reader limit: absence of an EBRF is a negative semantic statement, not a positive polynomial witness that a synthesis routine has exhibited. Failure to find a degree-D witness cannot certify nonexistence of all witnesses. The displayed corollary for positive universal certificates remains restricted to deterministic Büchi formulas; deterministic programs with one run also permit an existential positive route. Büchi ranking is scalar real-valued in Definition1. On an edge from a state with f>=0: if current state is accepting, successor f must stay >=0; otherwise successor f must lie between0 and f−1. The decrement is at least1, not arbitrary strict real descent. Values at accepting states may jump upward, allowing repeated finite excursions. Negative values exempt states outside the selected invariant region. Existential EBRF requires at least one nonnegative initial state and at least one Büchi-ranked successor for every state. On negative states the implication is vacuous and total-successor assumption suffices. Choosing ranked successors from the initial nonnegative region creates an accepting run. Universal UBRF requires nonnegativity at every initial state and ranking on every transition, establishing acceptance on every run. Definition2 prints codomain R^n, but reuses scalar comparisons and the synthesis assigns scalar polynomials; reader treats that as a likely type typo, not a defined vector order. Theorems1/2 claim sound and complete witness families for existential/universal Büchi analysis of the stated finite-branching systems. Proofs are deferred to arXiv:2403.05386. Reader explanation of soundness: a nonnegative sequence cannot sustain decrements of at least1 forever outside B, hence each infinite continuation returns to B; the existential/universal successor quantifiers determine which continuations. Universal completeness depends on finite branching to bound nonaccepting delays from a fixed reachable state; the extension proof was not audited. The worked EBRF in Section3.1 assigns x0+3 at (linit,q0), x0+2 at (l1,q0), x0+1 at (l2,q0), zero at accepting (l1,q1), and zero otherwise. Reader defect: Figure2 includes nonaccepting terminal (lt,q0) with an identity self-loop, whose zero rank cannot satisfy0<=f(next)<=f(current)−1. The zero otherwise branch also fails at nonaccepting (l3,q0), whose successor has rank x0²+1. This invalidates the displayed example as written, not the general witness theorem; no one-cell full repair is claimed. Fixed maximum polynomial degree D gives one coefficient template per location over all monomials of degree<=D. Location invariants restrict entailments. The tool says it jointly generates suitable polynomial invariants using [12], and also reports ASPIC for linear benchmarks. The article does not fully specify how these two invariant sources interact in every experiment or the exact template/certificate degrees used. Existential constraints require some outgoing transition be enabled and ranked wherever the location invariant and f>=0 hold. Disjunction is rewritten equivalently as (phi and not psi1 and ... and not psi(k−1)) implies psik, then antecedent DNF/consequent CNF distribute obligations. This avoids changing an existential successor choice into a requirement to rank every successor. Universal synthesis instead generates an implication for each enabled transition. Putinar-based elimination is imported from [1] as a sound/semi-complete conversion of polynomial implications to existential quadratic constraints. The printed paper does not supply its full compactness/positivity, certificate-degree or strict-inequality treatment. Theorems3/4 and text claim that sufficiently high D finds any polynomial witness, and sub-exponential complexity at fixed D. These are author theorems with omitted derivations; this reading does not independently validate unrestricted equivalence or quantitative complexity of every algebraic step. Existential initialization adds unknown initial values t and requires theta_init(t) and f_init(t)>=0. Universal initialization instead requires theta_init implies f_init>=0 for all initial values. Reader representation detail: a degree-two term c*t² has total degree3 in unknown coefficient c and initial t, so Step4 is not literally quadratic without auxiliary-variable lifting or other encoding. Such lifting is possible, e.g. v=t² then c*v; its equations are not described in this short algorithm. This is an encoding omission, not a demonstrated unsoundness. If a QP solution is found, the corresponding coefficients/initial values give a witness and Yes. Failure returns Unknown, acknowledging a higher-degree or nonpolynomial witness may exist. No bounded-degree failure establishes the opposite LTL answer. Mathematical soundness/completeness of witness families differs from semi-completeness of polynomial synthesis and from the practical solver’s ability to finish within a timeout. Prototype Java implementation calls Z3, Barcelogic and MathSAT5 in parallel on the generated constraints. Linear benchmarks use ASPIC for invariants. Direct-quantified Z3 is an ablation that omits the specialized Positivstellensatz step, not the same workload as a QP solver. No independent exact certificate validation, proof-assistant formalization, solver proof-log checking or floating-point-versus-exact coefficient audit is reported here. Experiments use Debian11, Intel E5-2670 at2.60GHz,6GB RAM,1800s timeout. Tool versions, concurrent solver resource allocation, repeats/variance, maximum memory, exact invariant/witness degree and complete translation details are not given in the article. Implementations/models linked at https://github.com/ekgma/LTL-VerP and DOI10.5281/zenodo.12518217 were not inspected or run. Benchmark selection: 297 of335 TermComp22 C-integer programs after excluding unbounded nondeterminism and variable-free programs;287 linear and10 nonlinear. Add21 of28 SV-COMP22 nonlinear loop benchmarks, excluding7 with unsupported operators such as integer mod. Thus318 programs,31 nonlinear, each paired with four specifications. Source categories mention integers while formal semantics uses reals; the article does not spell out a semantics-preserving integer-to-real translation or full arithmetic treatment. Refuting a real relaxation need not give an integer execution. Specifications use alphabetically first variable x, so their application is uniform but not an author-provided behavioral intent for each benchmark. RA is eventual terminal and globally x>=0, with every variable initially[0,64]. OV is eventual terminal or x outside[-64,63], with initial[-64,63]. RC is infinitely often x>=0; PR is always x<−5 implies eventually x>0, with initial[-64,63]. The formal model uses mathematical reals; the arithmetic domain and translation used for each benchmark are not specified in the article. OV is presented as a bound-crossing property; machine wraparound semantics are not established. A bounded nonterminating run need not converge, despite that word in the prose. Table2 compares Ours, Ultimate, nuXmv, MuVal and direct Z3. Yes/No count successful proofs/refutations; Tot adds them, U counts uniquely solved tasks. Reader arithmetic check: linear RA nuXmv prints76 Yes,91 No,137 Tot;76+91=167, so Tot is inconsistent. Stored table retains137 as printed and flags167 as arithmetic sum. Other printed count rows sum correctly. No raw outputs were examined to resolve which cell is wrong. Ours linear totals RA255,OV246,RC274,PR265 versus Ultimate263,267,280,282 and MuVal194,252,277,277. Thus Ours does not dominate linear coverage. Nonlinear Ours27,26,26,27 versus nuXmv1,7,26,25 and MuVal19,25,24,21; Ultimate unsupported, directZ3 zero. Unique counts sum10 linear and11 nonlinear property-program tasks; without task lists these must not be asserted to be21 distinct source programs. Average times are over successful subsets with differing coverage, not a common solved intersection. Table average rows include Yes,No,Tot times even though caption describes programs proved correct. Linear Ours5.4s Yes/81.5s No/47.2s combined, Ultimate5.4/4.1/4.7. Nonlinear Ours10.7/99.1/32.3. Do not infer a uniformly faster method from equal5.4s affirmative averages or compare conditional means as controlled speed ratios. The paper contains two program/automaton illustrations and an explicit location-wise EBRF example, but no named per-benchmark case study in results beyond aggregate categories. Supporting witness/product/algebraic proofs and full worked synthesis example are deferred to [13]/[1]. These were not read in this record. Heap/nonnumerical abstraction is future work; motivation through smart contracts/CPS is not a demonstrated deployed-system verification. No Verus, verified Rust, TLA+, distributed protocol implementation or LLM-agent experiment is supplied. Relevance to such evaluation comes from exact run quantifiers, proof versus refutation certificates, nondeterminism restrictions and theory/solver/benchmark boundaries, as reader inference.

- Behavioral Programming liveness (2024)
    - title: Keeping Behavioral Programs Alive: Specifying and Executing Liveness Requirements
    - what: Adds a must-finish state idiom to Behavioral Programming so b-threads can specify liveness directly, with GBA-game and MDP-based execution mechanisms. The paper maps 18 liveness patterns among 55 Dwyer patterns to executable b-threads, proves live-run compatibility, translates b-programs to generalized Büchi automata, and uses either SCC winning strategies or reward-guided MDP policies. Evaluation covers railway-level crossing and parameterized Maskable PPO, plus Sokoban.

    - limits: The GBA mechanism assumes finite-state executable models; MDP experiments focus on one liveness b-thread and use learned policies that may become infeasible as state complexity grows. Verification assumes must-finish states eventually exit, and RL results are proof-of-concept rather than universal policy guarantees.

- Bythos (2024)
    - title: Compositional Verification of Composite Byzantine Protocols
    - what: Embeds TLA in Coq and assumes that message delivery is weakly fair (a message that stays deliverable is eventually delivered). Proves that pending packets are eventually delivered and that protocol phases finish. The method: for each phase, find a finite set of packets that is enough. Show that delivering them starts the next phase. Chain the phases with the transitivity of the leads-to relation (for example init ∧ □next ∧ WFDelivery ⊢ sent ~~> output). Covers Provable Broadcast, Reliable Broadcast, Accountable Confirmer and Accountable Reliable Broadcast.
    - number: full development about 7100 Coq lines, trusted OCaml shim about 200
    - number: liveness spec/proof lines: Bythos library 160/181; Provable Broadcast 92/67; Reliable Broadcast 144/161; Accountable Confirmer 172/200; composition 3/7; connector 48/92
    - number: knowledge lemmas: 10-20 per case, found manually
    - limits: Fairness holds only for delivery to honest processes. The set of honest members is fixed. No proof of probabilistic termination. Partial synchrony (bounded message delays that hold only after some unknown time) is left to future work. The combined proof is tiny only after humans find reusable interfaces and facts about the connectors. The paper does not state how long the authors took.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- ForEx hyperliveness verification (2024)
    - title: Automated Software Verification of Hyperliveness.
    - what: Introduces Forall-Exist Hoare Logic (FEHL) and ForEx, a constraint-based verifier for finite-program ∀k∃l relational properties (Forall-Exist Hoare Tuples). The method symbolically postpones existential nondeterministic choices as parameters, computes parametric strongest postconditions, and discharges resulting first-order constraints with SMT. FEHL provides self-composition, sequencing, conditionals, loops and consequence rules; the paper proves soundness and completeness relative to sound/complete unary Hoare and under-approximate systems. ForEx implements eager restriction satisfiability checks, a minimal C-like language with arrays/bitvectors, Z3 support for linear integer arithmetic, arrays and finite bitvectors, and guess-and-check invariant generation. Experiments compare ForEx against HyPA, PCSat and HyPro on k-safety, GNI, refinement and related benchmarks. ForEx is faster on solved instances but its counting-based loop alignment misses context-dependent alignments and it cannot prove termination of existential loops, leaving some GNI instances unsolved.
    - number: ForEx solves 6 of 8 ∀*∃* instances in the PCSat comparison
    - number: HyPA comparison includes 20 k-safety/relational benchmark rows across Tables 5a/5b
    - number: ForEx times range from 0.10 ms to 2.88 ms on listed PCSat GNI cases
    - number: HyPro comparison checks 4 GNI instances over bitwidths 1–6 with 3-minute timeout
    - limits: FEHT validity quantifies finite terminating executions; the algorithm is for verification, not refutation of invalid FEHTs (which requires existential-universal reasoning). Completeness is relative to complete unary over/under-approximate proof systems and, for loops, depends on adequate invariant guesses. The tool explores a restricted counting-based loop alignment, so state-dependent alignments are outside its scope. Experiments use small benchmark programs, an M1 Pro with 32 GB, and user-provided invariant hints for marked cases.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-57249-4_10)

- Ghost signals and completeness thresholds (2024)
    - title: Semi-Automated Modular Formal Verification of Critical Software: Liveness and Completeness Thresholds
    - what: Develops (1) ghost-signal separation logic for modular termination proofs of busy-waiting concurrent programs under fair scheduling and (2) completeness thresholds reducing unbounded memory-safety proofs for array-traversing programs to bounded checks. Ghost signals are verification-only resources with obligations to set signals, well-founded levels preventing cyclic wait dependencies, and shared-state invariants connecting signal state to arbitrary data structures. The logic is modular over threads/functions and extends to race freedom, deadlock freedom, arbitrary conditions, signal erasure, higher-order features and fine-grained concurrency; appendices prove soundness. A bounded FIFO producer-consumer example and CLH lock were verified with tool support. Completeness-threshold chapters define thresholds over input sizes, derive verification-condition based approximations, prove modular/transitive/elimination rules, and characterize simple unconditional array accesses.
    - number: Tool support verifies C versions of bounded FIFO and CLH lock
    - number: No aggregate benchmark counts or runtime table in supplied text
    - number: Threshold results are theorem-based rather than empirical
    - number: bounded FIFO producer-consumer: 160 annotation lines for 37 lines of C (435%), verifies in 0.08 s
    - limits: Liveness requires fair scheduling and proof obligations that every signal is eventually discharged; levels must be well-founded and correctly encode dependencies. Ghost signals are not runtime state, so invariants linking them to concrete conditions are mandatory. Completeness thresholds target heap programs traversing arrays without allocation/freeing and can yield sound bounded guarantees only when verification conditions are precise; broader data structures remain future work. The dissertation reports no broad benchmark runtime study.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- LiDO (2024)
    - title: LiDO: Linearizable Byzantine Distributed Objects with Refinement-Based Liveness Proofs
    - what: A pacemaker state (current round and remaining time) exposes enough timing information for Coq refinement proofs of Byzantine consensus safety and liveness under partial synchrony (the network is eventually well-behaved). Unpipelined Jolteon is extracted to runnable OCaml, with a handwritten network and timer environment. The liveness proof splits leader entry, request, votes and receipt into timed stages.
    - number: unpipelined Jolteon: safety about 4,000 Coq lines, liveness about 1,800 (§4, PDF p.19)
    - number: pipelined liveness extension about 2,000 lines (§5)
    - number: liveness Table 1 itemises 357+523+953 lines
    - number: liveness proof around 1,800 lines
    - limits: It proves the system responds infinitely often, not that it responds to every client request. The proof of the pipelined model is not a verification of a production blockchain. The review notes found no person-time. The contracts for the network and environment are assumed.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- LVR (2024)
    - title: LVR: Mostly Automated Verification of Liveness Properties for Distributed Protocols with Ranking Functions
    - what: LVR turns a progress goal into ranking obligations and safety obligations: the measure decreases, stays nonnegative, cannot leave the pending condition without reaching the good state, and no deadlock occurs. It infers integer variables, bounds and polynomial coefficients. mypyvy and P-FOL-IC3 discharge the safety obligations. Humans supply any missing variables, bounds and tiers.
    - number: 37 out of 40 (§9.2, p.35:27) automatically; the rest manually in a few hours each
    - number: each final coefficient-solving query takes less than a second
    - number: eight models: ticket lock 68 lines, hybrid reliable broadcast 137, alternating bit 127, TLB shootdown 361, Paxos 133, multi-Paxos 135, stoppable Paxos 160, sliding window 119
    - number: all eight protocols succeed: ticket lock, hybrid reliable broadcast, alternating bit, TLB shootdown, Paxos, Multi-Paxos, Stoppable Paxos, sliding window
    - limits: By default, scheduling assumes a globally bounded interval. This is a slightly stronger fairness than "infinitely often". For example, scheduling at times 1, 2, 4, 8 satisfies "infinitely often" but not any fixed bound. Prophecy variables and a three-tier ranking keep the original fairness. LVR's multi-Paxos leaves out the local scheduler and the I/O queue. So fewer lines than IronFleet does not mean equally less effort. A cheap solver query is not the whole cost.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.1145/3632877); [notes: LVR and implicit ranking functions](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_2_liveness.md)
    - record id: `lvr-2024`
- neuralmc (2024)
    - title: Neural Model Checking
    - what: NeuralMC trains neural ranking functions (measures that shrink toward the goal) from simulation samples. It targets LTL liveness of SystemVerilog hardware. It then validates the functions symbolically with SMT over all transitions.
    - number: Table 1: 194 tasks; own tool 180, ABC 57, nuXmv 117, commercial X 156
    - number: five hours per task
    - number: VGA solves 0 of 10
    - limits: It covers finite hardware models. Training failures and timeouts are separate from proved violations.

- SCTP formal analysis (2024)
    - title: A Formal Analysis of SCTP: Attack Synthesis and Patch Verification
    - what: Humans build a Promela model of SCTP from the RFCs. They choose ten LTL properties and four attacker models. SPIN verifies the properties and KORG synthesizes attacks. This shows that the RFC 9260 patch removes the modeled CVE-2021-3772 attack.
    - number: ten LTL properties; four attacker models
    - number: long Off-Path checks 123:42 and 86:10 (mm:ss) unpatched vs 7:07 and 6:48 patched, on a 16GB M1 MacBook Air
    - number: KORG changes: 80 existing and 213 new lines, plus 102 shell lines
    - number: Ten LTL properties verified without attacker; long off-path checks 123:42 and 86:10 (mm:ss) unpatched vs 7:07 and 6:48 patched
    - limits: Two peers and buffers of size one. Excludes crashes, restarts, congestion control and most data transfer. The attacks are real only against the model. No theorem that the code matches the model. Model size and modeling time are not stated.
