- Stochastic Omega-Regular Verification and Control with Supermartingales (2024)
    - what: Introduces nonnegative Streett supermartingale certificates for probability-one omega-regular properties of general discrete-time stochastic processes. Uniform expected decrease on A minus B, bounded increase on B and no increase elsewhere certify each Streett pair. For Markov models, combines deterministic Streett automata, inductive invariants and parametric policies into polynomial-real constraints. Gives template-relative synthesis reductions to quantified NRA, existential NRA, QCP or LP, and eleven small example timings. Completeness for every satisfying stochastic process is explicitly open. Uses Robbins–Siegmund to certify disjunction of finite A-visits or infinite B-visits without proving either disjunct individually almost surely. Derives product-state post-expectations and six classes of universally quantified constraints, preserving invariant and policy synthesis jointly. Explains parametric versus known guards, Farkas infeasibility disjunct, external shielding and finite-memory encoding. Complete table/control parameters support checking exact model/property consistency; reader calculations identify printed automaton boundary and controller discrepancies, not inspected tool defects.
    - limits: General theorem uses a filtered probability space, measurable/adapted processes, nonnegative real V and fixed positive epsilon,M. It certifies probability1, permitting measure-zero exceptional runs, not every possible trajectory or a quantitative failure probability/time bound. Conditional expectations must be well-defined with the stated nonnegative drift inequality. Any connection to implementation numeric/physical behavior remains outside the mathematical theorem. A Streett pair(A,B) means finitely many visits to A OR infinitely many visits to B. Drift is E[V_next|history]<=V−epsilon*1_(AminusB)+M*1_B. Uniform epsilon matters; arbitrary strictly positive but summable decreases would not exclude infinite A-visits. B may recharge the certificate, so V need not be an ordinary supermartingale globally. Reader derivation of Theorem2 from Theorem1:take U_t=epsilon*1_(AminusB),W_t=M*1_B. On finite B-visits,sumW finite; Robbins–Siegmund implies sumU finite almost surely, hence finite AminusB and finiteA. Finite conjunction of probability1 pair-events preserves probability1. The paper gives theorem statements/intuitive examples rather than a machine-checked derivation. Theorem3 restricts Markov drift conditions to a measurable supporting invariant I containing the initial state and preserved with probability1. This can exclude unreachable regions where no global certificate exists. V must remain nonnegative onI; disregarding invariant initiation/consecution would break the proof. Common epsilon,M across finitely many pairs can be obtained by min/max if separate finite constants exist. Synthesis model is time-homogeneous real-state update f(x,w;kappa),i.i.d.disturbances and known fixed policy templates. Memoryless deterministic policies are direct; finite-memory/random inputs can be encoded by extra state/disturbances with fixed memory size/template. This does not synthesize arbitrary history-dependent policies or verify adversarial nondeterministic schedulers without a separate semantics. Deterministic Streett automaton reads current state label, advances q using currentx and advances modelx synchronously. Certificate/invariant live on product(x,q); automatonq alone generally lacks Markov time-homogeneity. LTL-to-automaton correctness and measurable observation predicates are trusted. Rabin duality requires negating the property/probability appropriately, not interpreting a Rabin probability0 result as satisfaction of the same unnegated formula. Symbolic closed-form post-expectation is essential. Theory allows piecewise polynomial templates of fixed degree and polynomial post-expectations, reducing to existential parameters/universal real states with quantifier elimination. Non-polynomial dynamics, arbitrary distributions, unknown degree, non-real arithmetic and unbounded unknown memory lie outside this algorithmic decidability claim. Template completeness is relative to existence of certificate,invariant,policy satisfying the encoded constraints in supplied forms. The paper explicitly leaves completeness of Streett certificates over all satisfying stochastic processes open. SolverUNSAT can show no encoded-template solution, not generally disprove the temporal property; no claim that increasing degree/regions must eventually succeed. Eq16 requires closure for every disturbance w inW, which is sufficient but stronger than Theorem3 probability1 closure when W contains null-probability choices. Example uniform[0,1] hasP(w>0)=1but excludes point0onlyalmostsurely; robust all-wclosure of invariantx>0 would fail. Relative completeness should be understood for the stronger written constraints, not all measurable probability1invariants. Piecewise linear in state does not mean linear in unknown coefficients: controller/certificate products make existentialNRA. Farkas implication rewriting includes either nonnegative multipliers proving consequence or multipliers proving antecedent infeasible. With known guards/invariant, discard infeasible premises then use feasible Farkas form. QCP may be nonconvex; polynomial products can be encoded with fresh quadratic equations. Fixedautonomous dynamics plus fixedguards reduce toLP. Strict-guard reduction cites[20,Lemma1] without reproducing its preconditions. Simply replacing strict premises by weak ones is not equivalent for an infeasible strict antecedent whose closure becomes feasible: (y<0 AND −y<=0)=>1<=0 is vacuouslytrue, while closed version fails aty0. A correct reduction must preserve strict infeasibility or meet the cited lemma conditions. This is a missing justification/precondition issue, not an inspected solver bug. Visible Figure3 claims DSA for(x>=−1) U G(−1<=x<=1),but transitions classify x>=1asq0 and only−1<=x<1asq1. Constantx1satisfies the printed closed-interval formula yet stays inq0forever and is rejected by Streett({q0,q2},empty). Thus displayed automaton enforces the stronger eventualx<1boundary; examplecontroller kappa.5 avoids that boundary issue, but general translation claim is not exact as printed. Example3 continuous uniformnoise[-.1,.1],x0=100,kappa.5 has V=x+1inq0,0otherwise andI:q0x>=−.2,q1x∈[−.2,.9],q2false. Eq32driftε.5 atx>=1andEq33atx<1are valid onI. Eq26maps[-.2,1)to[-.2,.6),Eq29maps[-.2,.9]to[-.2,.55],so closure holds. This is a concrete worked proof, not an executed synthesis replication. Example4 Eq44 prints PostV determined solely by x for all q∈{q0,q1,q2}, although q2 is absorbing in Figure3. For q2 the actual post branch uses alpha2,beta2 regardless of x; setting the q2 invariant false makes that defect irrelevant for Example3. Eq43 has an unexplained printed fv prefix. These are presentation defects; no artifact behavior is inferred. Prototype uses one affine certificate case per automaton state, convex polyhedral invariant templates, Spot LTL translation, SymPy symbolic manipulation and Z3 QF_NRA. Eleven Table1 rows:5VIC,4VI,1VC,1V; all reported under30seconds. Table includes externally supplied-invariant VC/V cases, despite the generic paragraph saying none are required. No comparison benchmark table, failed instances, scaling study, CPU/RAM/OS/solver versions or public artifact link is identified in this paper. Benchmarks use discrete random variables for weighted enumeration of post-expectation, except countably infinite evenOrNegative whose post-expectation is manual. The continuous uniform-noise example is worked analytically; benchmark distributions are not all continuous. Authors report a single deterministic execution and negligible variance, without repeated timing data, error bars or independent replication. Temperature1 model is x_next=.99x+2.8+alpha*x+beta+noise, with fair independent noise±.1 and x0=280K. Hot means x>298, Cold x<292. Alpha−1/32,beta4787/512 yield contraction767/800, center≈294.536 and limiting extreme interval[292.111742,296.960227], consistent with eventual comfort. Observations classify292/298 as neither Hot nor Cold, while prose uses open interval(292,298); preserve the boundary distinction. Reader counterexample to printed Temperature3 controller:alpha−1/64,beta9/2 give x_next=(1559/1600)x+7.3±.1. Starting from280, interval[280,289] is invariant: smallest/largest images are280.025/288.994375. Cold is therefore always true and Hot never true; GFCold=>GFHot fails on every run. This contradicts the printed model/observations/controller/property combination. It may be a model or parameter printing mismatch, not an independently inspected tool defect. Reader Temperature4 check:below shield305, alpha−43/3200,beta4 give x_next=(125/128)x+6.8±.1. Interval[280,295] is invariant, with extreme images280.1375/294.9859375. Hot never occurs and shield never activates.32consecutive low disturbances map any state in this interval to<=290.142598<292. Independent fair Bernoulli blocks produce infinitely many such positive-probability blocks almost surely; hence GFCold holds almost surely and GFHot is false. The printed reactivity claim conflicts with these shared dynamics/thresholds/initial state; artifact uninspected. Temperature3 total28.58s includes23.65s SMT. Temperature4 Table1 total4.64s and prose QCP solve.03s measure different work. Comparison changes controller class, shield, supplied invariant and parameter bounds; the entire speedup cannot be attributed solely to solver choice. Original alpha,beta bounds[−10,10], shielded[−5,5], with305alpha+beta<5.24. FinMemoryControl has one memory bit b; parameters alpha56,l1/8,m14,p1/2,q51 give guards x<=112 in b1 and x<=102 in b0. The simultaneous x update is alpha+w in b1, w−1 in b0. The paper does not state this benchmark’s disturbance distribution, initial state or initial memory bit, so its full behavior cannot be independently reconstructed from this PDF. The other random-walk and Temperature2 rows also omit full model details. Relevance is probabilistic liveness certificates, automaton-product invariants, template synthesis and checking exact generated controller/specification agreement. No LLM writes proofs/specs, no Verus/verified Rust or TLA+ pipeline, and no specific distributed protocol/implementation is verified. Quantitative verification and neural certificate learning are future directions; priority claims are author statements.

- TLA+ model-checking education (2024)
    - title: WIP: An Engaging Undergraduate Intro to Model Checking in Software Engineering Using TLA+
    - what: Describes an undergraduate/graduate teaching approach that introduces TLA+ and TLC through puzzles, a microwave controller, concurrency, bounded counters, and buffers. Students progressively confront safety, liveness, deadlock, and thread-safety failures. Analyzes model-checking courses, supplies reproducible examples and open-source modules, and uses successive counterexamples to motivate stronger requirements. The microwave case distinguishes safety (door/radiation) from liveness (eventual shutoff), while bounded buffers and counters introduce concurrency.

    - number: No controlled learning-outcome comparison reported
    - limits: Findings are preliminary, based on one institution/course and anonymous student input; the paper explicitly calls the work in progress and plans longitudinal/multi-institution evaluation. Educational engagement does not measure professional verification competence.

- Using Dynamically Layered Definite Releases for Verifying the RefFS File System (2024)
    - what: The RefFS project page links a separate 124-page draft, Progress of Concurrent Objects with Dynamic Dependencies, authored by Mo Zou. Its PDF metadata dates it May24,2024; the project says “A separate paper” illustrates MoLi's metatheory (P1). This is a complete fresh reading of that PODD paper, not an appendix of the OSDI paper and not a new reading of the RefFS implementation. The original RefFS main-paper account remains inherited from formal509. Whether this precise draft proves every rule of the released MoLi framework is not established by the link alone.  PODD makes a thread's progress depend on definite actions, obligations that their owning threads must fulfill once enabled. Actions have state-dependent layers, and dependencies must preserve strict relative order even when numeric layers change. Layers have a total order, not necessarily a well-founded one. Finite dependency sets and decreasing local/stack metrics supply the termination argument. The LIVE-FRM rule extends action/layer context while preserving old ordering, so an acquisition proof can be reused without expanding every nested wait chain. Tokens account for unblocked loop progress and finitely many levels of delaying interference. Theorem4.2 states linearizability and deadlock-freedom; starvation-freedom additionally requires rely and guarantee actions at level0 (S12,S14,S15).  AppendixA supplies the fuller resource/ownership logic, fences, rules and ideal shared-memory semantics. AppendixB first gives judgment simulations with finite-height while stacks: arbitrary-length lexicographic lists would not be well-founded (S31). B.3 expands new while/live-frame cases. B.4 maps these to a common simulation, directly for starvation-freedom and through a lock plus nondeterministically decreasing finite-wait wrapper for deadlock-freedom (S45,S48). B.5 combines thread simulations using finite active-thread dependency sets, scheduler rounds and a lexicographic metric. LemmasB.22–B.30 connect coinductive fair traces to refinement (S54,S57,S58,S66,S69). B.6 defines progress over complete fair traces, permitting abort alternatives, and uses three most-general clients to relate finite refinement to linearizability and fair refinement to linearizability plus starvation/deadlock freedom. The deadlock equivalence wraps the atomic specification; it must not be replaced by the stronger starvation refinement (S72,S74).  AppendixC provides all printed proof outlines for nested TAS/ticket locks, ticket-lock coupling lists, optimistic/lazy TAS lists, and two-list transfers. Ticket queues support starvation-freedom; unfair TAS permits only the weaker deadlock-freedom claim. Lists use local lock obligations, node order and finite traversal metrics. Optimistic/lazy cases retain removed nodes in ghost garbage, not a proved memory-reclamation scheme. Lazy contain is explicitly omitted. Transfers use one mutex to serialize moves, list levels0/1/2 and lexicographic (listlevel,distance-to-tail) layers; their add/remove proofs are referred to earlier lists. The two-list move is a useful dynamic-dependency example, rather than a distributed network/crash model (S87,S90,S94,S102,S109,S116,S122,S124).  Evaluation consists of paper proofs and worked examples; no numerical benchmark, proof-time table or replayed mechanization is supplied here. Section6 explicitly makes mechanization and automation future work (S20). Section5.1 admits that common rely conditions and frame stability can prevent reuse across objects (S17). Assumptions include fair scheduling, an arbitrary fixed finite number of threads, ideal atomic memory actions, safe total abstract methods and the presented non-nested method-call language. No bounded response-time, weak-memory, crash/recovery or implementation-to-binary theorem follows from this reading.  Reader deductions from the literal listings must be kept separate from intended theorems. Figure40's inner ticket loop never increments o; Figure55's elems retains marked values and discards unmarked ones; Figure62's sequential MOVE removes a head before reading the new head. Details and elementary counterexamples are in reader_diagnostics.json, with exact page anchors. These are printed-source defects, not findings about unseen code or a global refutation of PODD. The page also has draft-level omissions: a duplicated strict-comparison branch in the proof on p57, an apparent dependency-set type slip in DefinitionB.12, and an unresolved Fig.?? reference plus omitted scheduler semantics on p58. Underlines/fonts were visually checked before identifying these issues.  Relevance to the requested database is manual liveness proof structure and evaluation of specification/proof agents: track actual fairness and progress strength, stable dependency ordering, finite metrics, abstract-method contracts, omitted cases and intended versus literal specifications. These are reader uses of a concurrency result. This paper does not evaluate Verus, Rust, TLA+, LLM agents or distributed consensus. RefFS/Coq performance and proof counts remain attributed to the inherited OSDI paper, rather than this draft. Records exact WHILE/OBJECT obligations, ticket and abstract-lock metric differences, dynamic-layer/TDep construction, VFS bug cycle and source typo, refcount ownership protocol, non-atomic operation abstraction, conditional theorem and unmechanized metatheory, survey denominators, all LOC cells, workloads, normalized performance limits and graph estimates. Separates checked RefFS proofs from trusted C/runtime/lock/allocator and protocol-only VFS reasoning.
    - limits: Fresh source: all124pages, every LF line and all31 rendered contact sheets read; larger renders48,51,54,57–59,66,68–69,96,111,118 inspected. Original21page RefFS main reading inherited from formal509. Separate PODD draft is not a same-title appendix or proof-assistant replay. S12,S15,S72,S74: fair scheduling and arbitrary fixed finite thread count; linearizability plus deadlock-freedom generally, starvation-freedom only under level0 rely/guarantee restriction. Abort alternatives in progress definitions do not establish memory safety by themselves. S10,S22,S23,S27: ideal shared-memory language, primitive atomic operations and specified safe/total abstract methods; presented language excludes nested method calls. No weak-memory, compiled implementation, network/crash/recovery or bounded response-time theorem. S12,S14,S31,S54,S66: total ordering of layers is distinct from well-founded local/stack metrics. Dynamic numeric ranks may change while actual strict dependency relations remain stable. Finite-height stack bound and finite active-thread sets are substantive premises. S17,S20: paper admits object-specific rely/frame obligations and leaves mechanization/automation for future work. Reading the linked draft does not identify its rules with every released MoLi implementation rule. S96,S111,S118: printed metric, lazy abstraction and MOVE inconsistencies require qualification before reuse as formal specifications; counterexamples are elementary reader deductions, without implementation inspection. S102,S109,S116: ghost garbage retention does not prove reclamation; contain and repeated add/remove proof cases are omitted/referred elsewhere. All supplied content was read; omitted derivations were not independently supplied. P1: directory depth ranks can tie. A uniquely determined numeric rank does not give a strict total order on directory identities. Strict ordering of actual lock dependencies needs its own invariant; this local wording issue alone does not refute the locking result.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi24/presentation/zou)
    - record id: `using-dynamically-layered-definite-2024`
- Verifying Randomized Consensus Protocols with Common Coins (2024)
    - what: # Whole-paper record: Verifying Randomized Consensus Protocols with Common Coins (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Song Gao, Bohua Zhan, Zhilin Wu and Lijun Zhang. DSN 2024, IEEE, pp.403–415; DOI 10.1109/DSN58291.2024.00047. Read arXiv 2409.17627v1, submitted 26 September 2024, all 13 PDF pages, §§I–VIII, all 27 references, six figures and four tables. Inspected every page image. Publication pagination comes from Crossref; this PDF has no printed page numerals (Q52). Exact extracted passages with PDF pages and source lines are in quotes.json and combined.quote-source.txt. No author code, compiler, checker, prover, solver or serialized experiment/model data was executed or loaded.  ## Contribution and claimed guarantees (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The paper extends parameterized threshold-automaton verification from independent local coins to a shared common coin. It adds a separate coin automaton and communication variables, giving an infinite-state Markov decision process with process-location counters, per-round shared variables and resilience parameters (§III,Q9–17). Byzantine processes are represented through their effect on threshold guards rather than explicit Byzantine automata (§III-A,Q8). This is a verification method for protocol models, not an implementation refinement or verified executable.  Safety and termination follow different reductions. For safety, two one-round invariants imply agreement and binary validity (Proposition 1,Q20–21). Theorem 1 states that any finite schedule can be reordered by rounds while preserving its final configuration and per-round stutter equivalence (Q22). Theorem 2 relates one-round LTL without next-time properties to a single-round system, assuming nonblocking behavior and termination of every fair single-round execution for all initial configurations (Q24). These assumptions matter; it is not an unrestricted equivalence between arbitrary multi-round properties and one bounded run.  For termination the paper distinguishes three categories (§V-B,Q26–28). Category A has no decide event and targets eventual convergence to a common final value. Category B has binary messages and explicit decisions. Category C uses a Binary Crusader Agreement stage that may output ⊥, meaning undecided. C1 requires a uniform positive probability of converging during a round. C2 preserves unanimity; C2′ instead gives a positive probability of deciding when the round starts unanimously. Proposition 3 supplies a two-round geometric argument: the probability of not deciding after 2r rounds is at most `(1−p·p′)^r` (Q29).  Binding fixes a bit at the first relevant execution prefix and excludes its opposite in every continuation. It is a property of sets of executions, beyond ordinary single-execution agreement (Definition 4,Q30). The model refinement introduces N0, N1, N⊥ locations so that five sufficient safety conditions, CB0–CB4, imply binding (Proposition 4,Q31–32). Proposition 5 combines binding and C2′ for termination (Q33). Lemma 2 states that, in the single-round setting, a uniform positive probability under every admissible adversary is equivalent to each adversary having some satisfying path (Q35). That quantifier order is stronger than merely finding one favorable schedule.  The proof record is mixed. Lemma 1 has a one-line justification. Proposition 2 calls its proof trivial after a geometric-tail argument. Propositions 3–5 provide paper proofs; Corollary 1 cites them. Proposition 1, Theorems 1–2 and Lemma 2 explicitly omit proofs for length (Q21,Q24–25,Q35). proof-inventory.json records all ten named results and their assumptions. No proof-assistant artifact was inspected, and this reading does not independently establish their soundness.  ## Assumptions and limits (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The network is asynchronous and reliable: a sent message is “eventually received” without “loss, duplication or modification,” and receivers identify senders (§I(a),Q3). Byzantine faults are bounded by the selected resilience condition. The common coin is a trusted, fault-free abstraction, and only strong coins are considered: both bits are common outputs with probability 1/2. Cryptographic implementation correctness is future work (Q2–3,Q41).  The model separates shared-message guards from coin guards. Correct processes cannot update coin variables; coin rules cannot modify shared variables. Automata are canonical: rules on cycles have zero updates (§III-B,Q10–13). The method is parameterized by the environment's admissible n,t,f values, rather than verifying only the example cluster sizes.  The termination specification is explicitly “under Round-rigid Adversaries” (§IV,Q19). Such an adversary is fair and schedules all actions of round k before those of round k+1 (§III-E,Q17). Removing this restriction is future work (§VIII,Q41). Finite safety-trace reordering does not establish probabilistic termination for arbitrary asynchronous adaptive schedulers.  No benchmark archive, model file or proof-artifact URL is identified in the read paper. The paper says it builds two one-round automaton versions per protocol, but those inputs and their transformations were not available in the inspected sources (Q36–37). This does not assert that no artifact exists elsewhere. No ByMC run was reproduced.  ## Cases and reported evaluation (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The benchmark comprises Rabin83, CC85(a), CC85(b), FMR05, KS16, MMR14, Miller18 and ABY22 (§VI,Q36; Table II,Q45). Their protocol descriptions give different Byzantine bounds: for example Rabin83 uses t<n/10, CC85(b) t<n/6, and CC85(a), KS16 and ABY22 use n>3t. These protocol descriptions should not be substituted for the undisclosed exact checked input environments.  Table II reports model locations/rules, execution-schema counts and verification time for agreement, validity and almost-sure termination. The authors “ran each experiments 10 times” and report averages, without raw trials or dispersion (§VI,Q37). The first six rows use an Intel Core i7-12650H laptop; the final two use a 216-core AMD EPYC7702 server in parallel mode. Times measure verification work, not consensus latency or throughput.  Small examples complete quickly: Rabin83 has 7 locations/17 rules and reported times 0.25s for agreement, 0.20s for validity and 0.43s for its convergence target. CC85(a) uses 342 schemas/4.93s for agreement and an average 171.5 schemas/2.70s for termination. Fractional schema counts are averages. All eight rows and all table values are retained in numeric-evidence.json.  MMR14 has 17 locations/29 rules. Its agreement and validity checks take 298.90s and 8.74s. The termination column says CE, with no time. Separately, §VI reports a CB2 violation “within 10 seconds at best,” with an example witness n=193,t=64 (Q38). A failed sufficient condition alone does not prove nontermination. The authors additionally analyze the returned execution, compare it with a known adaptive attack and describe repeating its final actions to recreate the opposite estimate distribution.  The motivating four-node example has three correct processes and one Byzantine process. Two correct processes obtain the coin result s, while the adversary delays messages to the third until it can force estimate 1−s; the round can repeat (§II,Q7). This is a prior attack, not a new vulnerability discovered by the paper. Miller's 2018 issue credits Ethan MacBrough and explains that the actual coin instantiation lacks the independence assumed by the earlier ABA proof (Q48–49). The issue proposes an extra CONF phase before revealing coin shares (Q50). Its later implementation comments were read as allegations, without auditing the linked code (Q51).  The two repaired category C variants reportedly pass their obligations. Miller18 takes 11h46m47s and ABY22 takes 10h13m14s for all termination conditions, each exceeding 10^8 schemas; agreement takes 605s and 583s, respectively (Table II,Q45). The paper also describes 24-hour timeouts for the earlier technique on category C models, without a controlled cross-hardware speedup comparison (Q37).  Table IV changes milestone counts from 10 to 6 in five same-size ABY22-based automata. Calculated maximum schemas fall from 98,182,294 to 28,918 for CB0 and 7,479,057 to 3,592 for Inv2 (Q39,Q47). These are calculated search bounds, not measured runtime reductions or proof that the variants preserve protocol semantics. Table I supplies MMR14 transition guards and updates; Table III lists selected value 0 formulas and an ellipsis, rather than complete checker inputs. Figures 1–6 provide pseudocode, voting and MMR14 automata, and the binding refinement.  ## Source discrepancies that qualify interpretation (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Three points remain unresolved from the printed sources. They are recorded as static comparisons, not reproduced checker failures or counterexamples to a full theorem.  - Example 2 gives the MMR14 environment `n>5t` (Q9), while §II describes n>3t and §VI reports n=193,t=64. That witness satisfies 193 > 192 but not 193 > 320. The exact checked resilience condition cannot be recovered from the paper alone. - Proposition 5 reasons that no correct process has executed a coin-based rule, “in other words” the coin “is not yet tossed,” and therefore its result is independent of the binding bit (Q33). Figure 4 and Table I instead show rb with guard true, from initial coin location I2 to N0/N1, followed by unconditional rc/rd publishing its outcome (Q13,Q43–44). Under the initial-state and action definitions, a finite round 0 prefix can execute rb then rc before any correct-process transition. The adversary observes configuration histories (Q15,Q17). Thus the stated timing inference needs a further visibility or scheduling condition that is not enforced by these displayed rules. This prefix does not refute the full theorem or a benchmark protocol; it identifies an unsupported step in the supplied model-to-proof account. - Table III labels C1 with a universal implication excluding mixed final values along every path after a 0-valued final event (Q46). Body C1 is a positive-probability convergence condition, reduced through a per-adversary existential-path condition (Q27,Q35). The displayed statements differ. Separate model versions might explain a stronger checked obligation, but their concrete transformation is not supplied here, so the table cannot establish that correspondence.  The coin prefix, resilience arithmetic and quantifier comparison are in static-witnesses.json. All relevant formulas and table entries were checked against rendered PDF pages, rather than relying only on text extraction.  ## Relevance to the requested topic (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  This work directly addresses parameterized verification of randomized Byzantine consensus, especially the relation between common randomness, adaptive scheduling, binding and almost-sure termination. It supplies concrete verification-cost evidence and an example of using failed proof obligations to investigate a known attack. It also shows why a safety-preserving reordering theorem, a probabilistic termination argument and a checker result must retain their separate assumptions.  For evaluating LLM-written specifications or proofs, the reader-derived lesson is to check scheduler quantifiers, coin secrecy and independence, the exact resilience environment, source-to-formula correspondence, proof availability and what a counterexample actually disproves. The paper contains no LLM, Verus or verified Rust experiment. Its TLA proof-system reference concerns related HotStuff work, not this paper's verification method (Q40,Q42). HoneyBadger/Dumbo composition and cryptographic primitive verification remain future work (Q41).
    - limits: Parameterized canonical threshold automata under resilience environments; reliable eventual authenticated-sender delivery, no loss/duplication/modification; assumed strong fault-free common coin. Almost-sure termination only for fair round-rigid adversaries. CategoryA convergence differs from categoriesB/C decisions. Nonblocking and all fair single-round termination are prerequisites for reduction. Several central proofs omitted; printed resilience, coin-visibility argument and C1 formula correspondence unresolved.

    - what: ByMC models nodes that count messages against thresholds, abstracts the counts, and uses an SMT solver to check safety and liveness for every allowed number of processes and faults.     - title: Liveness Checking of the HotStuff Protocol Family
    - what: Extends Twins automated BFT testing with temperature-based and lasso-based liveness detection using partial/hot state abstractions, and compares them with bounded-time liveness checks on HotStuff, 2-Phase HotStuff, and Sync HotStuff. Defines partial states that retain liveness-relevant view/lock information and hot states used by temperature and cycle detection; lasso detection caches repeated hot states without controlled scheduling. Twins scenarios use two partitions, four honest processes and one Byzantine twin, with 1,000 unit tests for execution timing and 10,000 random scenarios per protocol evaluation. Baseline timeout checks report network-partition delays as violations; temperature/lasso distinguish persistent conflicting locks. Sync HotStuff’s early version exposes both safety and liveness faults.

- Finkbeiner Passing delay-dominance (2023)
    - title: Synthesizing Dominant Strategies for Liveness (Full Version)
    - what: Introduces delay-dominance, a strategy relation that compares unmatched progress failures against alternatives, to make best-effort synthesis compositional for many safety and liveness specifications. It defines a delay-dominance game, proves compositionality under an automaton bad-prefix criterion, and gives a universal co-Buchi automaton for synthesis. The paper formalizes the game where Spoiler chooses alternative-automaton branches first and Duplicator responds on the candidate strategy, proving self-dominance and that delay-dominant implies remorsefree dominant. It identifies existence of bad prefixes as the condition allowing blame of one component after composition, proves composition and winningness for realizable specifications, and constructs the recognizer by combining ACAs for phi and not-phi, Miyano-Hayashi conversion, and universal projection over alternative outputs. The resulting automaton has O(2^{|phi|^2}) states and bounded synthesis remains 2EXPTIME. Examples show immediate-send versus wait-for-peer strategies and mixed existential/universal ACA choices.
    - number: ACA size O(|phi|)
    - number: delay-dominance UCA size O(2^{|phi|^2})
    - number: synthesis in 2EXPTIME
    - number: parallel composition winning when phi realizable and bad-prefix criterion holds
    - limits: LTL specifications are represented by an ACA; delay-dominance depends on the chosen ACA structure, so a realizable formula may have no delay-dominant strategy. Composition requires the ACA to ensure bad prefixes; the authors state this is common for standard translations but leave parts marked TODO and give no implementation benchmark. Distributed architectures use Moore process strategies and standard bounded synthesis; complexity is worst-case 2EXPTIME.

- Geometric-logic LTL streams (2023)
    - title: Liveness Properties in Geometric Logic for Domain-Theoretic Streams
    - what: Defines LTL over the algebraic cpo of partially defined streams JStrAK and studies formula denotations through Scott topology, sobriety, frames and geometric logic. Negation-free LTL formulas translate inductively to geometric theories whose models are exactly the denotations; the paper applies the translation to prove a liveness specification for the non-productive stream filter function. The stream domain is DCPO[N,JAK] with a pointwise flat order, so finite-support streams are compact approximants and total streams are their directed suprema. The authors prove negation-free denotations are upward-closed and induce sober subspaces, while negation can destroy sobriety (e.g., J¬□aK excludes aω). They stratify negation-free LTL into G, Gδ and full fragments, define geometric-formula operators for next/until/weak-until, and prove model equivalence (Theorem 3). A frame/deduction treatment establishes spatiality for countable theories and gives complete deduction for Gδ formulas. For filter, ordinary fixpoint induction fails because the bottom function does not satisfy total-output liveness; finite-occurrence formulas ψn,k and output approximants φk yield Lemma 13 and prove that infinitely many predicate-satisfying input elements imply a total filtered stream.
    - number: Theorem 3: model equivalence for every negation-free LTL formula
    - number: Corollary 3: every negation-free denotation is a sober subspace
    - number: Corollary 5: countable geometric theories yield spatial frames
    - number: filter proof uses finite n,k approximants and Lemma 13, not experiments
    - limits: The semantic development assumes a set A (finite for the filter theorem), flat Bool and a Scott-continuous predicate p that is defined on every a∈A. Results concern denotational stream semantics, not an implemented language or distributed protocol. Negation-free formulas are the main tractable fragment; arbitrary negation and Fσ cases may lack a geometric-theory model presentation. Decidability and refinement-type automation are left open, and the filter proof handles a single recursively defined function/specification.

- Henzinger Mazzocchi Sarac quantitative safety liveness (2023)
    - title: Quantitative Safety and Liveness
    - what: Generalizes safety, liveness, the safety-progress hierarchy, and safety-liveness decomposition from Boolean properties to quantitative trace properties over complete lattices. It defines quantitative safety/liveness by finite-prefix rejection of threshold hypotheses, proves closure/decomposition theorems, and derives finite-state approximate monitors for properties that are approximately safe and co-safe. The paper introduces quantitative properties Φ:Σ^ω→D over complete lattices, quantitative safety via finite witnesses for wrong lower-bound hypotheses, and the safety closure Φ*(f)=inf_{s≺f} sup_g Φ(sg). It proves safety iff upper semicontinuity iff equality with safety closure, safety iff inf-property, co-safety iff sup-property, and characterizes lim-inf/lim-sup levels. New liveness and co-liveness definitions are shown to generalize Boolean liveness; every property decomposes as min(Φ*,Ψ) with Ψ live, and as the minimum of two live properties. Approximate α-safety/β-co-safety are characterized by closure gaps; for real-valued limit properties these imply suffix influence eventually falls below any δ>α+β, yielding a finite-state (δ,δ)-monitor. Examples include minimal/maximal/average response times and discounted safety.
    - number: complete-lattice value domain
    - number: safety/liveness decomposition for every property
    - number: approximation threshold δ>α+β
    - number: monitor with at most 2|Sδ| equivalence classes
    - limits: The core definitions assume a nontrivial complete lattice; finite-state monitoring Theorem 59 additionally assumes a limit property with finite real values, finite observation alphabet, and finite α/β error bounds. Quantitative liveness is intentionally stricter than prior multi-live or verdict-live notions, and the paper proves strict separations. The results are semantic and monitor-construction theorems rather than implementation benchmarks; applications to distributed systems require choosing an appropriate trace domain and quantitative metric. Limit-property converse results are left as conjectural/partial in places (e.g., the full converse of the countable-supremum characterization for lim-inf properties).

- PyLTA (2023)
    - title: PyLTA: A Verification Tool for Parameterized Distributed Algorithms
    - what: Introduces PyLTA, which model-checks layered threshold automata for all process and fault-parameter valuations without bounding state-space diameter or exchanged messages. It supports synchronous/asynchronous communication-closed protocols and LTL, using counter/predicate abstraction, Büchi automata, SMT realizability checks and interpolant-based refinement. The paper gives an LTA input language with repeating or lasso layers, threshold guards and parameter relations. Counter abstraction counts processes traversing states and loses identities; Phase King identity guards are nondeterministically abstracted. The Phase King example assumes 4t<n, introduces actual-fault parameter f≤t, and needs one refinement predicate.

    - what: The paper synthesizes reactive systems from Safety LTL extended with bounded next/eventually/until operators. On-the-fly pruning, timer-index reduction, and abstraction-based game solving keep generated games finite and manageable. - Cosmos DB TLA+ (2022)
    - title: Understanding Inconsistency in Azure Cosmos DB with TLA+
    - what: # Whole-paper read: Understanding Inconsistency in Azure Cosmos DB with TLA+ (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  A. Finn Hackett, Joshua Rowe and Markus Alexander Kuppe; arXiv2210.13661v1,24October2022,11pages. This is a client-facing behavioral model and industrial explanation case study. It reports390lines of core specification developed over2person-months,24non-type properties checked by bounded exploration/simulation, two documentation issues and an abstract explanation of an Azure outage. The service implementation is represented through expert feedback, without automated conformance checking. [Q0,Q1,Q11,Q19]  ## Model and assumptions  The four conceptual variables are an ordered write log, a universally replicated prefix readIndex, a durable majority-replicated prefix commitIndex, and a failover epoch. Their relationship is readIndex≤commitIndex≤Len(log). Replication nondeterministically increases either prefix; data loss truncates only the non-durable suffix and increases epoch. The log abstracts replica/region arrangements rather than enumerating them. The retrieved source additionally carries the configured write level as an unchanged state variable; the paper calls it a constant. [Q3,Q4,Q5,S1]  One write consistency level is configured for a deployment; individual reads may select an allowed weaker level. The model concerns individual single-key operations. Transactions and optimistic concurrency are outside its scope. Bounded staleness models operation counts, omitting the wall-clock bound. VersionBound limits unreplicated entries for all write levels; StalenessBound separately limits non-durable entries when bounded-staleness writes are configured. These abstractions should not be mistaken for a proof covering arbitrary implementation arrangements merely because the model does not explicitly contain servers. [Q3,Q5,Q14]  A write begins by appending its key/value and obtaining an epoch/checkpoint token. It can later succeed or fail. Failure need not delete the value: the request can have taken effect even if its response was lost. Weak reads can see the value while the write remains pending. Successful strong writes require durability. An epoch change invalidates all old session tokens in this model; authors acknowledge that real data loss need not invalidate every token. [Q6,Q7,Q9,Q18]  GeneralRead(key,index,allowDirty) returns the latest mapping at or before index, or a not-found marker if absent. If dirty reads are allowed, it also permits later matching entries. Strong reads use commitIndex without dirty values; bounded-staleness reads use commitIndex with dirty values; session reads use max(token.checkpoint,readIndex) with dirty values and require a current-epoch or null token. The client updates its token with the maximum prior/read checkpoint. Prefix/eventual reads both use readIndex with dirty values. This last equivalence concerns single reads/writes, not transactions. [Q8,Q9,Q10,Q14]  ## Validation and its limits  Authors report10liveness and14safety properties across4distinct levels, excluding basic typing, using exhaustive logs up to length6 and depth-first random traces up to100steps. Examples include durable committed prefixes, read-your-writes, monotonic reads, staleness bounds, token lifetime and eventual write completion. The paper gives no complete per-property run logs, state counts, runtimes or mapping of every property to each exploration bound. These are bounded model checks and sampled traces, not a parameterized proof or implementation test. [Q11]  The pinned artifact gives useful additional scope. MCCosmosDBProps uses two keys, three values, StalenessBound2,VersionBound3,Len(log)<3 and epoch<3. Its configuration labels several temporal checks “very very slow (1hr+)” and three “overloads TLC (out of memory), probably holds”. SimCosmosDBProps has a finite natural-number replacement0..100 and omits some costly properties. These comments are evidence about the retrieved snapshot, not proof that the paper's historical runs failed; neither do they substantiate completion of every stated check. The history specification assumes weak fairness of the disjunction WriteSuccess∨WriteFail; the simulation specification omits that fairness clause. Thus completion claims depend on the specification and bounds actually used. I did not establish an unbounded per-write progress theorem. [S2,S3,S4]  CosmosDBLinearizability maps strong read results to an atomic dictionary view. A commitIndex jump can correspond to multiple dictionary writes in one abstract step. The saved configuration asks for DictSpec under epoch<3 and Len(log)<4. This supports the paper's model-level refinement argument; I did not independently establish a full invocation/response-history linearizability theorem. The old-model refinement goes from the older model to this newer behavioral specification, so old behaviors are included in the new model. The claimed strictness additionally rests on the newer model admitting data loss and dirty reads omitted before. The retrieved mapping fixes two regions/two clients per region/one write region and constrains history length<5. I inspected the mapping, not its old-model dependency closure. [Q12,S5,S6]  The read hierarchy is checked while holding write consistency fixed and considering all session-token choices. Comparing differently configured write levels does not give the same inclusion: strong writes can admit more pending non-durable values than bounded-staleness writes. The four retrieved adjacent read-refinement modules use fixed write configuration and small finite state constraints; no unbounded theorem proof is attached to their THEOREM declarations. [Q13,S7]  ## Concrete cases and industrial results  The first documentation issue is a claim that prefix reads cannot observe A,C or B,A,C after sequential writes A,B,C. A,C is possible simply by interleaving reads around writes. For B,A, use five replicas: A reaches1,2,3;B reaches1,4,5;one read hits5 and another2 before replication catches up. Each write reached a local majority, yet the client seesBthenA. These are allowed service behaviors, not newly demonstrated implementation failures. Authors report the documentation was rewritten. [Q14]  The second issue concerns regional guarantees. Bounded-staleness operations in one region may appear strongly consistent until a region failover loses or exposes older data; they lack global durability. Authors report their argument was confirmed for individual operations, while regional documentation bullet points remained because transactions distinguish prefix and eventual behavior. The paper's section title about regions must be read with that explicit restriction. [Q14]  The outage affected thousands of subscriptions over28days and was noticed after26days. FrontDoor wrote task data, then asked Worker to read it. Session consistency was configured, but the processes used separate/unconfigured session tokens. Their cross-process interaction therefore had only eventual-read guarantees. A routing change exposed the existing semantic problem through latency. A PlusCal model reproduces a missing read before replication; forwarding FrontDoor's token to Worker removes the modeled error according to the authors. The original postmortem author confirmed the explanation made sense. This is an abstract causal explanation and proposed/rechecked model fix, not evidence that the fix was deployed or that every production trace was matched. [Q15,Q16,Q17]  Other cases show why the model has two-step writes: a weaker read can observe an incomplete strong write; a failed write can remain durable; and a bounded-staleness read under strong-write configuration lacks the separate bounded-staleness write throttle. Although the paper says strong consistency has no bound on in-progress requests, the model still has VersionBound on partially replicated entries; completed replication permits more pending requests. These are distinct quantities. Expert review caught an earlier incorrect atomic-write abstraction, which was rewritten and rechecked in2–3days. [Q18,Q19]  ## Printed text versus pinned source  The displayed WriteCanSucceed on paperp4 checks token validity and, for strong writes, token.epoch=epoch. It omits the checkpoint≤commitIndex condition that the immediately following prose says is required. The pinned CosmosDB.tla includes that durability conjunct. Thus the displayed formula and prose disagree; this does not establish that the checked source permits non-durable successful strong writes. [Q7,S1]  The outage text's expected formula is eventually workerValue="workerValue", but Listing1writes"taskValue". The source's temporal property uses eventually workerValue="taskValue" and its terminal-state property checks the same literal. I treat the printed literal as a presentation mismatch and preserve the intended missing-task-value scenario rather than presenting the printed formula as a valid test of that scenario. [Q16,S8]  These comparisons use a retrieved, pinned repository snapshot, not an asserted reconstruction of the exact2022experiment checkout. No author code or proof/checker was run. [S9]  ## Contribution and reading scope  For this database, the contribution is a reusable specification of a distributed dependency's visible behavior and a concrete demonstration that model correctness also depends on choosing faithful abstractions. Its relevance to LLM-written specifications is my inference: evaluation should test behavior the model must permit as well as properties it must forbid, record expert corrections, and distinguish bounded self-consistency from service conformance. The paper has no Verus, Rust or LLM experiment. [Q19,Q20]  Read all11pages,28reference entries, every displayed definition, Listing1,all4figures and TableI; inspected all11page renders across the preceding and current context. Read the complete core/property/read-wrapper/client/linearizability modules, both PlusCal outage modules, both model-check/simulation wrappers and configurations, four read-level refinement modules and their common-pattern constraints, the general-model mapping/configuration, and two repository READMEs.26static files acquired with hashes and Git blob identities. No old general-model dependency closure or broader implementation inspected. Official and inventory PDFs are byte-identical. No author execution/import, solver, compiler, checker or serialized scientific-results loading.
    - number: 2 person-month development
    - number: 10 liveness and 14 safety properties across 4 consistency levels
    - limits: single-keyindividualoperations;countnotwallclock;alltokensinvalidatedonepoch;boundedchecks;expertconformanceonly

- Holistic Verification of Blockchain Consensus (2022)
    - what: Parameterized model checking verifies binary Byzantine consensus associated with Red Belly Blockchain under n>3t and0≤f≤t. First verifies binary-value broadcast, then substitutes a smaller specification-based automaton into the outer consensus and checks safety and progress properties with ByMC. Agreement and validity do not require the added good-round fairness; termination does. The verified algorithm is the coordinator/timeout-free pseudocode variant under that fairness, rather than a proof of the deployed implementation or its partial-synchrony mechanism. Makes the decomposition concrete: global sent-message counters replace local receive counts; Byzantine effects lower enabling thresholds by f; four temporal broadcast properties support a replacement gadget; round communication closure reduces selected all-round LTL obligations to one-round checking. Manual meta-arguments connect per-superround invariants to global agreement/validity and good-round existence to eventual decision. Gives a fair-round definition, a nonterminating4-process trace, detailed automaton rules, explicit liveness preconditions and measured schema counts.
    - limits: Fixed n sequential processes communicate through reliable fully connected asynchronous point-to-point links; delays are finite but unbounded, steps interleave, each receive is atomic and sender identities distinguish quorum members. Up to t<n/3 are Byzantine; f≤t is the actual fault count. The threshold abstraction assumes faulty senders cannot impersonate arbitrarily many distinct processes. Communication closure is essential: each process uses only messages for its current round, discards earlier tags and buffers later tags. Monotone send counters and threshold guards admit the prior ByMC reduction. Guarantees do not automatically extend to arbitrary protocols with cross-round data dependencies, timing constraints or changing membership. Reliable communication is encoded by progress assumptions on enabled rules and source-location emptiness. This excludes scheduler executions that indefinitely withhold enabled productive actions. Added consensus fairness is different: some round r has every correct process first deliver r mod2 in that round’s binary-value broadcast. Neither fairness follows from asynchronous reliability alone. Algorithm1 is the DBFT variant without coordinator/timeouts. The second DBFT variant uses partial synchrony, but this paper replaces that mechanism with a good-round assumption. It does not prove a synchronizer, coordinator rotation, timeout correctness or that the deployed environment enforces the assumption. Threshold models describe correct processes and abstract Byzantine influence with f-offset guards; global send counters abstract possible local receipt histories. Parameterized checking ranges over symbolic parameters satisfying n>3t≥3f, rather than testing a list of network sizes. Soundness still depends on faithful manual abstraction and ByMC’s reductions/SMT checks. Inner broadcast verification establishes justification,obligation,uniformity and termination. Replacement must preserve all behaviors relevant to the outer consumer because contestants is shared and the outer algorithm can proceed before the broadcast finishes. The paper argues this and supplies encoded preconditions; it is not a machine-checked source-to-source refinement of production code. ByMC handles a restricted LTL fragment and one-round systems. Cross-superround agreement,initial validity and existential good-round termination require manually chosen intermediate formulas and mathematical composition arguments. The model checker does not directly discharge each final quantified formula as written. The proof covers binary agreement,validity and eventual decision for the displayed algorithm. It does not prove blockchain transaction semantics,multi-value reduction,ledger consistency across all block heights,network/cryptographic code,resource bounds or end-to-end behavior of the deployed Red Belly software. Processes continue participating after deciding so others can finish; only the first decision matters. Text states safe exit after two later rounds but does not supply a verified executable stopping implementation. A process that halts immediately on first decision would change progress assumptions.

- On the Correctness of Speculative Consensus (2022)
    - what: # Whole-paper read: On the Correctness of Speculative Consensus (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Jelle Hellings, Suyash Gupta, Sajjad Rahnama and Mohammad Sadoghi; arXiv2204.03552v1,7April2022,25pages. This is a detailed paper proof for Proof-of-Execution (PoE), a Byzantine state-machine replication protocol that speculatively executes before committing. Its main contribution is the connection between early client replies, preservation across view changes, eventual consensus and recovery of client results. The proof is mathematical prose and pseudocode, not a machine-checked development. [Q0–Q3,Q5–Q10]  ## System and required conditions  There are fixed replicas R, Byzantine replicas F, and clients C. The paper writes n=|R|,f=|F|,nf=n−f and assumes n>3f, authenticated communication and deterministic transaction execution. Byzantine replicas can coordinate and clients may all be malicious. Most exposition assumes publicly verifiable digital signatures; digest variants need cryptographic hash assumptions, and threshold variants need threshold signatures. This paper defines f from the fault set; it does not separately explain how deployments configure an upper bound used for quorum thresholds. [Q1,Q11–Q14]  Weak consensus means decisions never conflict, while termination and forcing new decisions require periods of reliable communication with some unknown bounded delay. The advertised asynchronous recovery therefore does not give unconditional progress during arbitrary loss or delay. Assumption4.6 bounds proposal size and the number W of prepared but uncommitted rounds. This makes a single finite message-delay estimate applicable to protocol messages. Timeout estimates must eventually grow sufficiently large. The displayed backoff argument also requires a positive initial estimate; multiplying zero cannot make it positive. [Q1,Q6,Q8]  ## Normal execution and recovery  The primary in view v has id=v mod n. A signed client request is proposed in a round. Each honest replica prepares only one proposal for that round/view and broadcasts Prepare. A certificate collects nf matching preparations. Replicas execute speculatively after all preceding transactions have executed and send Inform to the client. The client needs nf identical, view-specific Inform replies as a proof of execution. The familiar f+1 reply threshold for committed execution is insufficient here because speculative executions can be rolled back. Theorem4.1 establishes same-view uniqueness and normal-case service with a good primary, reliable communication and identical execution history. [Q2,Q3]  The printed Theorem4.1 contradiction proof defines T_i=S_i minus Byzantine replicas, but then treats S_i as if all its members were honest and as if those full sender sets were disjoint. Byzantine senders can vote for both proposals. The usual intersection argument applies to T_i, the honest subsets, instead. This is a notation defect in that displayed proof, not a demonstrated counterexample to same-view uniqueness. [Q3]  The four-replica failure examples distinguish omission by a faulty primary, a false accusation by a faulty backup, and network loss. Other replicas can observe the same messages in all three cases. A malicious primary can leave some honest replicas without a proposal while the client still receives a proof of execution using Byzantine replies. Recovery therefore must preserve what a client could already have observed, rather than treating an isolated replica's missing proposal as evidence that no execution occurred. [Q4]  Check-commit is decentralized. An honest replica broadcasts its prepared certificate only after execution, while still in the view, and after all previous rounds have committed. Receiving a valid certificate lets a missing replica prepare and execute. A commit certificate collects nf CheckCommit messages. Theorem4.4 relates a client proof to eventual preparation at every honest replica, relates one honest commit to eventual commits/client proof given the shared prefix, and derives that a commit certificate entails nf−f honest executions plus commitments for earlier rounds. The first two statements assume reliable communication and all honest replicas in the view. Out-of-order proposals are supported; the proved base still commits in order. [Q5]  View changes use three stages. A replica that detects failure rebroadcasts Failure. Receiving f+1 alerts induces an honest replica to join; nf alerts make it stop the old protocols and send ViewState to the next primary. State includes the latest commit certificate and every subsequently executed prepared certificate not rolled back. The next primary collects nf states and broadcasts NewView. Under reliable communication with delay δ, Lemma4.5 synchronizes entry into the proposal stage within2δ; Lemma4.7 delivers a good primary's new-view proposal within4δ of the first such entry. [Q6]  The new ledger retains the committed prefix through LC, then the latest-view prepared request in each round through LP. Missing committed history is queried; speculative requests outside the selected ledger are rolled back; the new primary must repropose selected uncommitted requests before new rounds. Theorem4.10 inducts over views to preserve any request executed by nf−f honest replicas, including every request with a possible client proof or commit certificate. Assumption4.8, uniqueness of the request named by commit certificates for a round, is a temporary proof hypothesis subsequently discharged by Corollary4.11. It is not an additional final assumption. [Q6,Q7]  Lemma4.12 supplies QueryCC/RespondCC recovery of missing committed history. Lemma4.13 and Theorem4.15 use increasing timeout estimates and eventual good primaries to establish resumption once communication is reliable. The worked δ=30ms example starts estimates7,3,5,1ms; three doublings yield56,24,40,8ms, and the four replicas need3,4,3,5 doublings respectively. Remark4.17 suggests a practical cap such as10seconds, but the theorem's unbounded-growth argument only carries to a cap when that cap exceeds actual reliable-network delay. [Q8]  Theorem4.18 combines preservation, recovery and client-request forwarding to prove weak consensus. Honest clients repeatedly send an unanswered request to all replicas; replicas forward it or trigger view changes if it is not served. Suggested defenses against malicious clients include per-client forwarding limits and treating another request by the same client as evidence of a timely proposal. These are stated heuristics, not a quantified load-admission/fairness analysis. Recoverable non-Byzantine crashes require certificates in persistent storage; Remark4.19's recovery claim depends on enough replicas recovering. [Q9]  Consensus alone does not guarantee a client obtains nf identical replies from one view. Example4.20 partitions honest replicas into A,B,C of sizes f,f,1 and alternates unreachable groups across three views. The same request becomes decided, but view-specific replies and commit certificates remain split, so no ordinary proof of execution forms. The repair is InformCC: after an honest replica commits, it can send the view-independent round/result to a retrying client. Now f+1 matching InformCC replies suffice because at least one is honest and committed execution is preserved. Theorem4.21 proves service when communication is reliable. Client request order is not automatically enforced; waiting for the first result or validating per-client counters supplies an extra application policy. [Q10]  ## Complexity, variants and evaluation  Figure1 compares seven protocols/variants; Figure12 gives13 message/certificate rows and three size variants. These are analytical accounting, not measured experiments. Base PoE takes two replica communication rounds before execution and three through check-commit; Linear-PoE takes three and five. The table's C parameter is request size, so expressions containing nC account for payload, not just the number of messages. Off-by-one terms are intentionally omitted in Figure1. Base prepared certificates have size O(nf+C); commit certificates O(nf²+C), because different replicas may attach different prepared certificates. ViewState and NewView additionally depend on W. [Q1,Q11]  Digests make most messages independent of request size but require explicit retrieval of missing request bodies and certificates. Replacing a certificate-carrying CheckCommit with a constant-size message requires f+1 matching senders before querying an honest holder. It does not eliminate the bounded-request-size assumption. Threshold signatures reduce certificates to constant size; by themselves they do not reduce all-to-all message counts. [Q12,Q13]  Linear-PoE sends preparations through the primary. Its decentralized commit replacement rotates aggregator q=round mod n, commits a digest of the last n prepared certificates, and uses a separate n:(f+1) threshold scheme for recovery certificates before n:nf commit certificates. This prevents a fixed faulty aggregator from permanently hiding committed history. The pseudocode waits for commitments through round−n and covers the preceding n−1 rounds with the resulting certificate. Failure detection still broadcasts quadratically. The authors' belief that such communication cannot be eliminated in their recovery setting is not an impossibility proof. The linear variant is explained by construction; it does not receive a separate full formal refinement proof here. [Q14]  The MAC-only variant is a proposed redesign of the two new-view stages by analogy with PBFT; the paper does not print that redesign. Out-of-order processing means proposing future rounds within W while earlier decisions finish. It does not remove the sequential execution dependence or the proved base's in-order commits. [Q15,Q16]  The n=31,C=10KiB,M=256B bandwidth example reports322KiB for the base primary and315KiB with linear aggregation, approximately2percent savings. Direct substitution gives322.5KiB and315.7258KiB, about2.10percent. The primary still transmits the full proposal to every replica. The accompanying “roughly-1/3-th increase in latency” is inconsistent with the stated increase from two to three communication rounds: relative to two, that is50percent. This is a reader arithmetic observation, not an execution measurement. [Q14]  Example5.6 assumes n=31,δ=15ms and1Gbit/s and prints22 sequential versus3028 out-of-order decisions/second. The22 figure follows1/(3δ)=22.22. The3028 figure is inconsistent with the printed bit/byte units: using10kB and256B gives about386.95 decisions/second; using10KiB gives378.51. The value3028.1 is obtained with10KiB if1Gbit/s is treated as10⁹bytes/s. Either consistent byte interpretation still gives a bandwidth-only upper estimate, excluding computation and other bottlenecks. The paper's two-orders-of-magnitude statement is therefore not supported by its stated inputs. [Q16]  Section6 compares PBFT, Zyzzyva, SBFT, HotStuff and MinBFT using communication rounds and simplified costs. It qualifies Zyzzyva/SBFT fast paths, assumes PBFT-like checkpoint costs for SBFT, and identifies trusted hardware/reliable communication for MinBFT. The HotStuff discussion and33 decisions/second example are this paper's account of a particular chained scheduling model, not an independently validated universal bound on HotStuff deployments. Its prediction that PoE outperforms PBFT “in all situations” exceeds what round counts alone establish: computation, rollback/recovery workload, implementations and resource bottlenecks are not measured here. [Q17–Q22]  The introduction/conclusion claim an “analytical and experimental” evaluation. This saved25-page version contains analytical comparisons and hypothetical numeric examples, with no experimental setup, implementation measurements, repeated trials or measured plots. Its earlier EDBT2021 paper is reference18; results from that separate paper are not imported into this record. No proof assistant, model checker, Verus, verified Rust, TLA+ or LLM-agent experiment is reported. [Q0,Q17–Q24]  ## Relevance and reading evidence  For distributed verification, this contributes detailed proof obligations for speculative client observations, view-change preservation, the distinction between agreement and delivered service, and communication/recovery tradeoffs. My inference for proof-agent evaluation is to check eventual-reliability and bounded-window conditions, quorum intersections, temporary proof hypotheses, persistent crash state and units in analytical evaluation separately. A successful local proof script would not by itself establish any of those model-to-implementation connections.  Read the complete25pages,40references,all15numbered figures including pseudocode and both tabular figures,all printed proofs/formulas/examples,and all25page renders. Official and inventory PDFs are byte-identical. No implementation or artifact source was linked as part of this paper's proof/evaluation and none was inspected here. This record does not audit the related ResilientDB implementation or preceding EDBT paper. No author execution/import, compiler/checker/prover/solver run or serialized scientific-result loading.
    - limits: n>3f/authentication/deterministicexecution/boundedproposal_window/eventualreliablecommunication/positivegrowingtimeouts/persistentcertificates/paperproofnotmechanized/noempiricalmeasurements/printedunitdiscrepancies

- Optimal-DPOR-Await (2022)
    - title: Awaiting for Godot: Stateless Model Checking that Avoids Executions where Nothing Happens
    - what: Introduces Partial Loop Purity Elimination (PLP), an assume-to-await transformation, and an await-aware Optimal-DPOR algorithm to eliminate executions whose loop iterations have no externally relevant effect. PLP statically detects pure loop iterations and inserts assumptions; suitable assumptions become awaits, while a weakened conflict relation lets Optimal-DPOR-AWAIT remain optimal. Theorems establish preservation of local-state reachability and assertion violations and one representative per equivalence class for terminating programs. Evaluation covers locks, queues, sorting networks, and SafeStack.
    - number: On sortnet(6), PLP+Await explores 1 complete execution in 0.08 s versus Nidhugg baseline 1+312 executions in 0.48 s
    - number: SafeStack-331 bug found in 8 minutes after 2+2,453,474 traces; fixed version exhaustively checked in 24 minutes after 5,772+8,521,721 traces
    - number: GenMC and baseline Nidhugg failed to find SafeStack-331 after more than 2,000 hours
    - limits: Correctness and optimality are stated for terminating programs; SMC requires loop bounds. PLP is static and can miss zero-net-effect loops or be limited by syntactic purity conditions. Experiments use C/C++ pthread programs on one Ryzen 5950X system, with one-hour benchmark and ten-hour SafeStack timeouts; fairness/livelock handling is limited.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- PTA reachability and liveness (2022)
    - title: Reachability and Liveness in Parametric Timed Automata
    - what: Maps decidability boundaries for parametric timed automata, introducing bounded integer-points PTAs and reset-PTAs for decidable reachability while proving many liveness and deadlock problems undecidable. The paper separates EF/AF reachability emptiness, cycle existence, deadlock existence, and EG persistence across bounded/open/closed L/U-PTAs and integer-point subclasses. It uses counter-machine reductions for undecidability and derives PSPACE-completeness for bounded closed L/U-PTA EF and cycle problems, identifying thin boundaries where closedness or boundedness changes decidability.
    - number: existence of an infinite run in an L/U-PTA is PSPACE-complete
    - number: deadlock-existence is undecidable for closed bounded L/U-PTAs
    - number: cycle-existence is PSPACE-complete for closed bounded L/U-PTAs and undecidable for general bounded PTAs
    - number: EF-emptiness is decidable/PSPACE-complete for bounded IP-PTAs
    - limits: Results are complexity-theoretic and depend on parameter domains, strict/non-strict guards, boundedness, and open versus closed parameter valuations. The undecidability constructions show that unrestricted PTAs cannot generally support algorithmic synthesis or liveness checking; no empirical implementation benchmark is claimed.

- SMR synchronizers and PBFT-light liveness (2022)
    - title: Liveness and Latency of Byzantine State-Machine Replication
    - what: Specifies an SMR synchronizer with application-controlled advance calls, supplies an O(n)-view-variable implementation tolerant of pre-GST message loss, and uses its interface to prove PBFT-light atomic-broadcast liveness and derive recovery/good-case latency bounds. The proceedings also describes extensions to periodic PBFT leader rotation and a HotStuff-like variant. Figure 1 separates Monotonicity, Validity, Bounded Entry, Startup and Progress. Figure 3 retains only each sender’s highest WISH and uses the (2f+1)st and (f+1)st order statistics plus periodic retransmission; it enters a view only when these agree. Bounded Entry holds eventually with d=2δ, provided no correct process requests a higher view during the entry interval. PBFT-light uses delivery and recovery timers, increasing both only on expiration. Lemma 6 shows that a correct process with recovery timeout >6δ and delivery timeout >4δ cannot initiate the first advance in a sufficiently late good view. Theorem 7 uses this to rule out executions where only a subset of correct processes increase timeouts indefinitely, then proves every valid broadcast by a correct process reaches all correct processes. Reliable DECISION retransmission also ensures dissemination of any already delivered value. Good-case delivery takes 4δ from a correct broadcaster, corresponding to 3δ from the leader. The analysis distinguishes unknown per-execution δ from a known conservative Δ used only for the latency bounds.
    - number: n=3f+1; quorums 2f+1; f+1 correct advance requests suffice for startup/progress
    - number: O(n) stored view variables; eventual entry skew d=2δ
    - number: good-view timer thresholds: dur_recovery>6δ; dur_delivery>4δ
    - number: Theorem 8 deadline: GST+ρ+max{ρ+δ,6Δ}+4Δ+max{ρ,δ}+7δ under its stated assumptions
    - limits: n=3f+1, at most f Byzantine processes, authenticated point-to-point communication, signatures and collision-resistant hashing. Partial synchrony: unknown GST and δ; before GST messages may be lost/delayed and clocks drift unboundedly; after GST correct-process delays are ≤δ and clocks track real time. Correct clients invoke advance at most once between new_view notifications. Correct broadcasts are valid and unique. PBFT-light uses signatures and omits checkpoints/watermarks; reliable DECISION broadcast requires retransmission omitted from pseudocode. Latency results additionally assume known Δ>δ, timeout caps 6Δ/4Δ, retransmission period ρ and reliable-broadcast latency ≤δ+ρ. Theorem 8 assumes sufficiently large timeouts already at GST and a correct leader(V); Theorem 9 assumes synchronous startup, correct leader(1), timeout bounds and the view-1 optimization. Full synchronizer proof, PBFT safety proof, portions of timer proof, latency proofs, periodic-leader and HotStuff protocol details, and textbook-bug details are explicitly deferred to arXiv:2202.06679, which was not read in this batch. Theoretical proof, not a machine-checked artifact or implementation performance experiment.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- TLA+ verification trifecta (2022)
    - title: Specification and Verification With the TLA+ Trifecta: TLC, Apalache, and TLAPS
    - what: Uses a TLA+ specification of asynchronous termination detection and Safra's ring algorithm to demonstrate complementary verification with TLC explicit-state checking, Apalache bounded/SMT and inductive-invariant checking, and TLAPS theorem proving, including refinement from the algorithm to a high-level state machine. The high-level module specifies active nodes, pending messages, termination detection, safety, liveness under weak fairness, and quiescence. Safra's algorithm adds white/black colors, message counters and a circulating token; termination requires an inactive node 0, no pending messages, white token/node, and zero accumulated counter. TLC finds 4,097 states for N=4,K=3 in the abstract model and 1.3 million (N=3,K=C=3,Q=9) or 219 million (N=4 with same bounds) in the algorithm model, exposing state explosion and a token-color bug. Apalache checks bounded prefixes, proves an inductive invariant for N=100 in 20 seconds, and checks quiescence for N=100 in 11 seconds. Refinement is checked for small N; TLAPS proves type, safety, liveness, and refinement for arbitrary instances, with an invariant proof taking about 230 lines and liveness/refinement about 245 lines. The conclusion recommends TLC first, Apalache for inductive invariants, TLC/random exploration for liveness, then TLAPS for higher confidence.
    - number: 4097 states for high-level TLC N=4,K=3
    - number: 262145 states for high-level N=6,K=3
    - number: 1.3 million states for Safra N=3,K=C=3,Q=9
    - number: 219 million states and about 50 min for Safra N=4
    - limits: The abstract model assumes a nonzero finite N, natural pending-message counters, asynchronous actions and weak fairness only for DetectTermination; stronger fairness is optional but unnecessary for the stated liveness. TLC requires finite bounds on pending messages/counters for exhaustive checking, so finite checks cannot establish arbitrary executions. Apalache handles bounded prefixes and inductive invariants but needs type annotations and human-discovered invariants; it cannot directly handle the fairness temporal property. TLAPS proof effort is manual and backend-dependent. The Safra case is a ring algorithm and does not evaluate throughput, deployment behavior, or other distributed topologies.

- Brief Announcement: On Strong Observational Refinement and Forward Simulation (2021)
    - what: Shows by example that ordinary forward simulation need not preserve hyperliveness or strong observational refinement when finite and infinite traces are allowed. Defines progressive forward simulation with a well-founded decrease on concrete steps mapped to an empty abstract sequence; states that it implies strong observational refinement. Full theorem proof is external. The counterexample composes a forward-simulated LL/SC implementation with fetch-and-add. A particular LL/SC implementation permits SC failure merely because another thread executed LL, without any intervening write. Alternating threads can therefore prevent both operations from returning. The formal scheduler requirement is nonempty enabled actions after every consistent finite trace, plus object determinism; it does not impose per-thread fairness. Definition 1 forbids infinite internal stuttering through a well-founded concrete-state order. Necessity of the progress condition is future work.
    - limits: Finite and infinite traces; programs composed with labelled transition-system objects; deterministic admissible schedulers. Specific implemented LL/SC semantics permit the counterexample; it does not assert every hardware LL/SC behaves this way. Full proof of Theorem 2 deferred to external reference [3]; no machine-checked artifact or empirical measurements in supplied brief. The motivating text says LL at F2 although pseudocode labels LL at F1; record interprets the operation rather than reproducing that line typo.
