- Failure-aware actor Synod progress (2021)
    - title: Verification of Eventual Consensus in Synod Using a Failure-Aware Actor Model
    - what: Introduces a predicate-fair failure-aware actor model (FAM) and mechanically verifies sufficient asynchronous conditions for eventual progress of the basic Synod protocol without a unique leader. The report formalizes proposers, acceptors, quorum predicates, message transitions and actor availability/failure in an asynchronous actor semantics. It states fairness axioms for send/receive, nonfaulty-agent assumptions, and P1/P2 proposal-number conditions preventing higher-numbered interruption. Lemmas show eventual promises and votes from a nonfaulty quorum; Theorem 1 derives that some proposal is eventually learned as chosen. Athena and SPASS mechanically check the proof sketches in roughly 6000 lines. The appendices include the actor-language operational rules and code correspondence discussion.

The sufficient condition CND assumes a nonfaulty proposer eventually ready to send a P1/P2 proposal to a quorum of nonfaulty acceptors. FAM assumes stable storage, uncorrupted but asynchronous messages, and fairness axioms; failures can be temporary or permanent, but progress requires the relevant actors eventually available. The proof does not use generic predicate fairness directly, instead relying on a system-specific uninterrupted-proposal property. Applicability to UAM timing requires additional bounds not proved here.

- Ghost Signals: Verifying Termination of Busy Waiting (2021)
    - what: Develops a modular Hoare/separation-logic approach to termination of busy-waiting programs under fair scheduling. Ghost signals represent conditions other threads must establish; linear obligations require setting them. A well-founded partial order forbids waiting on a signal at or above any obligation held by the waiter. Data-structure invariants tie signals to real conditions, allowing resource-local proof of waiting over arbitrary shared structures. §2 derives the logic through thread-safe physical signals, non-thread-safe signals guarded by mutexes, arbitrary heap cells, then erasure into ghost state. An augmented operational semantics makes a thread get stuck if it exits with outstanding obligations; fork transfers obligations rather than discarding them. A program-order graph tracks the path carrying each obligation. A minimal infinitely-waited signal would have an infinite obligation path that can wait only on finitely-waited lower signals, yielding contradiction under fairness. Points-to/signal ownership prevents races. Mutex acquisition creates a release obligation and also obeys the level order. §3 splits signal-ID allocation from initialization so a fixed finite collection of signal IDs can be named early without creating premature obligations. Loop iterations may select from a finite signal set, and outer productive loops use ordinary decreasing counters. §5 explains call/wait permissions and thread phases; permission consumption only in descendant phases prevents a self-fueling busy loop. §6 describes a possible reduction of termination to instrumentation safety for higher-order step-indexed logics.
    - number: bounded FIFO: 37 C lines, 160 proof-annotation lines, reported 435% annotation overhead, 0.08 s verification
    - number: CLH: 49 C lines, 343 annotation lines, 700% overhead, 0.1 s verification
    - number: example: FIFO capacity 10, 100 values, 200 signal IDs; levels pop(i)=102−i and push(i)=101−i, mutex level 0
    - limits: Termination relies on fair thread scheduling and a well-founded signal/mutex-level order; proof authors supply invariants, levels and ghost actions and must discharge every obligation. The proceedings presentation restricts busy loops to with-mutex-await syntax and ultimately to a finite fixed set of signals per loop; the external report generalizes looping/recursion. Physical machine memory models are not analyzed; claims use the presented language semantics. Higher-order assertions are not supported by this logic itself; Iris integration is proposed, not demonstrated as a mechanized embedding here. Abstract specifications for CLH require a suitable level universe supporting bounded infinite level sets. No automated signal/invariant/level inference or LLM experiment is reported. The server I/O-liveness result is a linked combined-logic artifact, not a full server proof included in these pages.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Justness (2021)
    - title: Justness: A Completeness Criterion for Capturing Liveness Properties
    - what: Defines justness as a completeness criterion for transition-system paths: once a non-blocking transition enabled by parallel components is not interfered with, some component must eventually act. It formalizes asymmetric concurrency for CCS, broadcast ABC/ABCd, and CCS with signals (CCSS). The paper gives an LTSC framework with blocking actions, concurrency relation, and B-just paths; defines dynamic and static synchron/component relations and proves they induce the same justness. It proves feasibility by constructing extensions of finite paths when countably many enabled non-blocking transitions (or equivalence classes) exist, relates justness to fairness, and proves agreement with coinductive definitions for CCS extensions and abstract paths. It also treats indicators/signals, infinitary choice, and gives inductive/coinductive characterizations.
    - number: Theorem 1 proves B-justness feasible under countably many enabled non-blocking transitions
    - number: Corollary 1 extends feasibility to countably many concurrency-equivalence classes
    - number: Theorem 2 shows dynamic and static justness coincide
    - number: Theorem 3 and Theorem 4 establish agreement with coinductive and abstract-path definitions
    - limits: Transition systems abstract distributed systems and assume atomic actions terminate. Blocking actions are explicitly identified; justness is weaker than fairness and cannot guarantee a task that is repeatedly postponed despite no interference. Feasibility theorem needs countably many enabled transitions/equivalence classes; the paper does not provide implementation or empirical evaluation, and future work is semantic equivalence with Petri/event-structure models.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `justness-2021`
- LI HLS Formal Model Checking (2021)
    - title: Verifying High-Level Latency-Insensitive Designs with Formal Model Checking
    - what: Automated RTL wrapper generation and bounded model-checking checks for invalid input consumption and deadlock in MatchLib latency-insensitive HLS designs. The invalid-input model instantiates reference and test DUTs with equal valid inputs but different invalid data and compares valid outputs. The deadlock model aggregates HLS global-stall signals; for nonblocking ports it synthesizes a stall signal when no relevant ready signal is asserted for a configured number of cycles. Checks operate on HLS-generated RTL because the bugs depend on cycle-accurate timing, while wrapper generation is fully automated.
    - number: Table I runtimes 5.99–359.3 s for abstracted cases; buggy initial versions are falsified and final fixes proven
    - number: five MatchLib components have invalid-input properties proven (0.10k–9.75k gates; 10.98–36,627 s)
    - number: NoCRouterArray.v1 deadlock falsified in 12.71 s; v2 proven to bound 450 in 16,915 s
    - number: OpticalFlow.v2 deadlock falsified in 1,237 s; OpticalFlow.v1 proven to bound 10,000 in 42,090 s
    - limits: Uses MatchLib valid/ready interfaces, HLS-generated RTL and Synopsys VC Formal 2018.10 bounded model checking. Deadlock proofs are exhaustive only for the modeled check or stated user bound; several application results are bounded or time out. The custom nonblocking stall threshold N must be known from HLS optimizations.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `li-hls-formal-model-checking-2021`
- Static Race Detection and Mutex Safety and Liveness for Go Programs (extended version) (2021)
    - what: The paper develops GoL, a process-calculus model for shared memory, locks, read-write locks, and later channels, with behavioural types that encode happens-before synchronization. Type soundness and progress connect processes to µ-calculus types; model checking these types detects data races, lock safety/liveness, and mixed channel/shared-memory errors. The migoinfer+ extractor and Godel2/mCRL2 backend form a static toolchain. Beyond the abstract, the authors define race and lock properties over GoL, prove subject reduction, process safety/data-race transfer, and conditional equivalence of type and process liveness for terminating, may-converging, and finite-branching programs. The channel extension models channel-as-lock and pointer-through-channel errors. Evaluation includes mutex/channel variants, producer-consumer, and five-philosopher programs with explicit false-positive caveats for approximated cyclic analyses.
    - number: 17 benchmark programs were checked on an 8-core Intel i7-7700K with 16 GB RAM
    - number: No-race and simple-race variants correctly separate safety/data-race outcomes in roughly 0.69–0.79 seconds
    - number: dine5 unsafe took 6,996 ms; dine5 deadlock 12,278 ms; dine5 fixed 8,998 ms
    - number: channel Dining Philosophers took approximately 185 minutes for the race version and 645 minutes for the fixed version
    - limits: The liveness correspondence requires restrictions such as termination, may-convergence, or finite branching; the implementation is sound for safety/data-race transfer but can report rare liveness (and possibly safety) false positives/negatives on approximated cyclic models. Extraction supports only a Go subset, has restrictions on embedded mutexes/structs and parameter passing, and does not scale to unbounded goroutine creation. mCRL2 state space grows rapidly with summands and action order.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `static-race-detection-and-2021`
- The CakeML Project’s Quest for Ever Stronger Correctness Theorems (2021)
    - what: Historical account of CakeML’s end-to-end verification strategy: proof-producing translation of logical functions to embedded ML programs, verified compilation to concrete bytes, and application-specific proofs, with extensions through a verified Silver CPU’s Verilog implementation. Describes improvements that strengthen transported correctness, especially impure code reasoning and space bounds needed for liveness. Explains why in-logic execution supplies a concrete compile(input)=machine_code theorem, enabling a final property statement without the compiler function; how applying synthesis and in-logic compilation to the compiler’s own logical definition yields a verified executable; why finite target memory limits the default behavior theorem; how a sound space-cost semantics removes early out-of-memory exits for specific programs; and how shared characteristic-formula infrastructure connects manual verification and monadic code synthesis. Separates completed compiler/hardware layers from 2021 prospective language/compiler projects.
    - limits: This 10-page invited overview summarizes previously published mechanizations; it contains no new complete formal proof, benchmark table, theorem inventory or fresh controlled experiment. Its references identify where to inspect the underlying statements. Counts or broad performance comparisons here are author reports, not independently reproduced results. The transfer argument relies on proved source properties, formal CakeML operational semantics, a correct compiler theorem and a proved concrete compilation result. In-logic compilation removes the compiler from the final statement; it does not eliminate trust in the logic, prover implementation or fidelity of source/machine/hardware models. Source lists, arrays and integers are unbounded; target memory is finite. The default behavior preservation explicitly permits early out-of-memory errors. Source liveness alone therefore does not ensure target liveness. The separate compiler-sound space-cost semantics supports tight bounds for concrete programs and the strengthened transfer. The first compiler’s front end includes parser soundness/completeness with respect to an SML syntax grammar, type-inference soundness (completeness added later), and no runtime errors for typed source programs. That typing theorem does not rule out target out-of-memory exits. The optimizing compiler uses eight intermediate languages and multiple passes per language. Functional big-step, clocked interpreter semantics permit a single simulation proof per phase to handle termination and nontermination; the paper says most, not all, simulation directions are forward. CakeML characteristic formulae are proved sound against CakeML operational semantics, including higher-order recursive functions, mutable state, exceptions and FFI I/O. A later extension handles diverging programs, and a simplified nontermination Hoare logic is proved sound and complete. Do not extend that completeness claim to every CF feature or program-verification automation. The Isabelle/HOL source generator is for the most part verified once for all; the HOL4 generator is for the most part proof-producing. Lem generates source-semantics definitions in both provers from a common specification. The overview does not present a verified cross-prover transport theorem or certify Lem itself. The Silver result composes a verified compiler target, machine-code filesystem and CPU correctness through the formal Verilog implementation. A later verified compiler maps a supported subset of generated Verilog to FPGA technology-mapped netlists. Physical fabrication, FPGA programming and the entire external synthesis toolchain are not established by this overview. Compiler projects using CakeML for a low-level imperative language, a Haskell-inspired language and a choreographic language are described as ongoing in 2021. Project-success explanations are explicitly the author’s beliefs, not empirical causal conclusions. No Rust, Verus, TLA+ or LLM-agent experiment is reported.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.4230/LIPIcs.ITP.2021.1)
    - record id: `the-cakeml-project-s-2021`
- What's Live (2021)
    - title: What's Live? Understanding Distributed Consensus
    - what: Extracts published liveness statements for consensus algorithms and formalizes them in TLA+. It compares what each one assumes and guarantees: 3 link assumptions, 7 server assumptions and 6 progress assertions. It also runs finite TLC experiments.
    - number: of 31 algorithms in Table 1, only 18 (58%) mention liveness and 11 (35%) give a liveness proof
    - number: TLC on up to 4 proposers and 4 acceptors in a few hours
    - number: 31 algorithms/variants are catalogued; 18 state liveness assertions, and only 11/31 (35%) provide some liveness proof.
    - number: TLA+ TLC illustrations cover up to 4 proposers and 4 acceptors for single-value Paxos; multi-value/larger cases become too expensive.
    - limits: It is an analytical comparison plus finite TLC runs, not new verified code. Appendix A uses a tick bound. Hours and lines of code are not stated.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- An Axiomatic Approach to Existence and Liveness for Differential Equations (2020)
    - what: # ODE liveness needs enough solution duration and a valid path through its domain (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Tan and Platzer develop deductive proofs that an ordinary differential equation (ODE) solution lasts long enough to reach a target without leaving its allowed domain. This record reads the complete 61-page arXiv:2004.14561v2 report, dated 9 August 2020, including all appendices. The contribution is a library of small refinement steps in differential dynamic logic (dL), a logic for discrete and continuous programs, plus derived existence/liveness rules and a KeYmaera X implementation. The authors derive corrected forms of several earlier liveness arguments; the earlier publications themselves were not independently checked for this record. [Evidence: Q0, Title, complete abstract and §1 scope, PDF pp.1–1; Q1, §1 complete surveyed corrections, contributions and trust/implementation claims, PDF pp.1–3; complete exact passages in quotes.json.]  Source (Q0, Title, complete abstract and §1 scope):  > An Axiomatic Approach to Existence and Liveness for Differential > Equations > Yong Kiam Tan André Platzer ∗ > Abstract > This article presents an axiomatic approach for deductive verification of existence and liveness for ordinary differ- > ential equations (ODEs) with differential dynamic logic (dL). The approach yields proofs that the solution of a given > ODE exists long enough to reach a given target region without leaving a given evolution domain. Numerous subtleties > complicate the generalization of discrete liveness verification techniques, such as loop variants, to the continuous set- > ting. For example, ODE solutions may blow up in finite time or their progress towards the goal may converge to zero. > These subtleties are handled in dL by successively refining ODE liveness properties using ODE invariance properties > which have a complete axiomatization. This approach is widely applicable: several liveness arguments from the > literature are surveyed and derived as special instances of axiomatic refinement in dL. These derivations also correct > several soundness errors in the surveyed literature, which further highlights the subtlety of ODE liveness reasoning > and the utility of an axiomatic approach. An important special case of this approach deduces (global) existence prop- > erties of ODEs, which are a fundamental part of every ODE liveness argument. Thus, all generalizations of existence > properties and their proofs immediately lead to corresponding generalizations of ODE liveness arguments. Overall, > the resulting library of common refinement steps enables both the sound development and justification of new ODE > existence and of liveness proof rules from dL axioms. These insights are put into practice through an implementation > of ODE liveness proofs in the KeYmaera X theorem prover for hybrid systems. > Keywords: differential equations, liveness, global existence, differential dynamic logic > 1 Introduction > Hybrid systems are mathematical models describing discrete and continuous dynamics, and interactions thereof. This > flexibility makes them natural models of cyber-physical systems (CPSs) which feature interactions between discrete > computational control and continuous real world physics [Alu15, Pla18]. Formal verification of hybrid systems is > of significant practical interest because the CPSs they model frequently operate in safety-critical settings. Verifying > properties of the differential equations describing the continuous dynamics present in hybrid system models is a key > aspect of any such endeavor. > This article focuses on deductive verification of existence and liveness1 > properties for ordinary differential equa- > tions (ODEs), i.e., the question whether an ODE solution exists for long enough to reach a given region without > leaving its domain of evolution. Such questions can be phrased naturally in differential dynamic logic (dL) [Pla10, > Pla12b, Pla17a, Pla18], a logic for deductive verification of hybrid systems whose relatively complete axiomatization > [Pla12a, Pla17a] lifts ODE verification results to hybrid systems, and whose theorem prover, KeYmaera X [FMQ+ > 15], > enables an implementation. > For discrete systems, methods for proving liveness are well-known: loop variants show that discrete loops eventu- > ally reach a desired goal [Har79], while temporal logic is used to specify and study liveness properties in concurrent > and infinitary settings [MP92, OL82]. However, the deduction of (continuous) ODE liveness properties is hampered > ∗Computer Science Department, Carnegie Mellon University, Pittsburgh, USA {yongkiat|aplatzer}@cs.cmu.edu > 1The form of ODE liveness considered in this article is in the sense of Owicki and Lamport [OL82] for concurrent programs within their (linear) > temporal logic. Liveness for ODEs has sometimes been called eventuality [PR07, SJ15] and reachability [TT10]. To minimize ambiguity, this > article refers to the property as liveness, with a precise formal definition in Section 2. Other advanced notions of liveness for ODEs are discussed > in Section 8. > arXiv:2004.14561v2 > [cs.LO] > 9 > Aug  The standing model uses autonomous polynomial vector fields over mathematical real states, with rational polynomial syntax and first-order real-arithmetic target/domain formulas. A fresh clock can represent time dependence. Polynomial fields have unique local solutions, but those solutions need not last forever. A diamond formula means that some finite solution segment reaches the target and satisfies the domain throughout, including its initial and final states; duration zero is allowed when the domain and target already hold. This is finite reachability along a unique continuous evolution. Applying it to distributed fairness, message delivery or infinitely recurring progress would require an additional model. The paper's completeness background concerns ODE invariants; it does not establish completeness for all ODE liveness. [Evidence: Q2, §2.1–2.2 syntax, standing ODE model and modal semantics, PDF pp.3–5; Q3, §2.3 Lie derivatives, base proof rules and constant-context restrictions, PDF pp.5–6; complete exact passages in quotes.json.]  Source (Q2, §2.1–2.2 syntax, standing ODE model and modal semantics):  > 2 Background: Differential Dynamic Logic > This section reviews the syntax and semantics of dL, focusing on its continuous fragment which has a complete > axiomatization for ODE invariants [PT20]. Full presentations of dL, including its discrete fragment, are available > elsewhere [Pla12b, Pla17a, Pla18]. > 2.1 Syntax > The grammar of dL terms is as follows, where x ∈ V is a variable and c ∈ Q is a rational constant. These  The central proof method refines a simple diamond property into the desired one, justifying each change with a box property that holds along relevant solutions. Ghost variables are fresh auxiliary ODE coordinates used only for proof. The new bounded differential ghost axiom permits nonlinear vector ghosts when their squared norm is bounded along augmented solutions; a derivative-growth version supplies another sufficient condition. These bounds prevent added coordinates from ending the solution prematurely. Appendix A proves the semantic duration comparison and derives the refinement axioms; Appendix B supplies syntactic rule derivations. Existing base-calculus results cited by the paper are distinguished from its new ghost and refinement arguments. Global existence is expressed by adding a clock and proving that every arbitrary finite clock threshold can be exceeded. The dependency-order rule builds the ODE in groups whose right-hand sides use only earlier groups and themselves. Each group needs either a squared-norm bound depending on earlier groups/time or a suitable linear bound on its squared-norm derivative. Derived cases include globally Lipschitz fields and affine dependency groups. Example 2, u′=u and v′=uv, has provably global solutions despite lacking a global Lipschitz constant for the whole vector field. The bounded-existence rule says a solution either reaches any requested time horizon or leaves a bounded region; invariance of that region then proves global existence. The combined rule allows affine groups alongside bounded nonlinear groups. [Evidence: Q7, §4–4.1 finite-time blow-up example, clock existence and Lemma5, PDF pp.10–10; Q9, §4.1 dependency-order groups and complete Corollary6, PDF pp.11–12; Q10, §4.2.1 global Lipschitz and affine dependency-order existence, PDF pp.12–13; Q11, §4.2.1 complete Example2 and dependency-order derivation, PDF pp.13–14; Q12, §4.2.2 complete bounded existence and combined Corollary10, PDF pp.14–15; complete exact passages in quotes.json.]  Source (Q7, §4–4.1 finite-time blow-up example, clock existence and Lemma5):  > 4 Finite-Time Blow Up and Global Existence > This section explains how global existence properties can be proved for a given ODE x0 > = f(x), subject to assumptions > Γ about the initial states for the ODE. The existence and uniqueness theorems for ODEs [Chi06, Wal98] guarantee that > polynomial ODEs like x0 > = f(x) always have a unique, right-maximal solution from any initial state, ϕ : [0, T) → S > for some 0 < T ≤ ∞. However, these theorems give no guarantees about the precise duration T. In particular, ODEs > can exhibit a technical phenomenon known as finite-time blow up of solutions [Chi06], where ϕ is only defined on a > bounded time interval [0, T) with T < ∞. Additionally, it is possible that such finite-time blow up phenomena only > happens for some initial conditions (and corresponding solutions) of the ODE. Moreover, these initial conditions (with > finite-time blow up) may not be relevant to the model of concern, especially when the dynamics of real world systems > are controlled to stay away from the blow up. For example, αn (2) exhibits finite blow up of solutions only outside the > red disk as shown in Fig. 1 and the blow up occurs well after its solutions have reached the target region, see Fig. 2. > As an additional example for this section, consider the following nonlinear ODE: > αb ≡ v0 > = −v2 > (7) > The solution to this ODE is v(t) = v0 > v0+t , where v0 6= 0 is the initial value of v at time t = 0 (if v0 = 0, then > v(t) = 0 for all t). If v0 < 0 initially, then this solution is only defined to the right for the finite time interval [0, −v0), > because the denominator v0 + t is 0 at t = −v0. On the other hand, for v0 ≥ 0, the existence interval to the right is > [0, ∞). Thus, αb exhibits finite-time blow up of solutions, but only for v0 < 0. > 4.1 Global Existence Proofs > The discussion above uses the mathematical solution v(t) of the ODE αb (7) as a function of time. Then, using a fresh > variable τ not in x, t, the following formula syntactically expresses that the ODE has a global solution because its > solutions can reach time τ, for any arbitrary τ: > ∀τ hx0 > = f(x), t0 > = 1i t > τ (8) > Proving formula (8) shows global existence of solutions for the ODE x0 > = f(x). The simplest instance of (8) is > for the ODE t0 > = 1 by itself without any ODE x0 > = f(x). In this case, the formula (8) is valid because t0 > = 1 is an > ODE with constant RHS and its solution exists for all time. The axiom TEx below expresses this fact and it is derived > directly from the solution axiom of dL [Pla17a]: > Lemma 5 (Time existence). The following axiom is derivable in dL. > TEx ∀τ ht0 > = 1it > τ > Proof in Appendix A.2. > Other instances of (8) can be proved using axioms BDGh·i,DDGh·i with appropriate assumptions about the initial > conditions for the additional ODEs x0 > = f(x). This is exemplified for the ODE αb next. > Example 1 (Velocity of particle with air resistance). The ODE αb can be viewed as a model of the velocity of a particle > that is slowing down due to air resistance. Of course, it does not make physical sense for the velocity of such a particle > to “blow up”. However, the solution of αb only exists globally if the particle starts with positive initial velocity v > 0, > 4For consistency, the ODE x0 = f(x) is assumed to not mention t even if this is not always strictly necessary.  Proposition 11 gives conditional completeness for global existence when a global solution is representable in the term language; a representable provable norm bound can also suffice. The report explicitly notes that its polynomial syntax excludes even the exponential solutions of Example 2, though extensions allow more functions. This result does not say that every global polynomial ODE has an automatically discoverable certificate, or that arbitrary liveness is decidable. The related-work discussion notes undecidability of global existence even for polynomial ODEs. For an unconstrained target, an atomic differential variant is a quantity whose derivative is bounded below by one fixed positive constant until the goal. Strict positivity alone can decay toward zero and fail to reach the goal. A constant positive rate also fails if another coordinate blows up before enough time passes. Corollary 12 therefore makes sufficient duration explicit or uses provably global solutions. Equational goals use continuity; higher-order variants use a positive higher Lie derivative to obtain polynomial-in-time progress. Staging-set rules keep the trajectory in a region until the goal and force exit: a bounded set bounds continuous terms, while a compact set lets a strictly positive derivative attain a positive minimum. The set-Lyapunov rule requires compact K and open target P, correcting a merely closed-K version reported in the survey. The first subtlety, shown in Fig. 3c, > is that ODE solutions must exist for sufficiently long for p or, more precisely its lower bound, to become non-negative. > This is usually left as a soundness-critical side condition in liveness proof rules [Pla10, SJ15], but any such side > condition is antithetical to approaches for minimizing the soundness-critical core in implementations [Pla17a] because > it requires checking the (semantic) condition that solutions exist for sufficient duration. The conclusion of rule dVΓ > < > formalizes this side condition as an assumption. In contrast, rule dV< requires provable global existence for the > ODEs (provable as in Section 4). In fact, as Fig. 3d shows, p may stay negative by asymptotically increasing > 18 Y. K. Tan and A. Platzer > towards a negative value as t approaches ∞.  The running linear spiral reaches a smaller circle and then an enclosing annular target by continuity. The nonlinear spiral u′=−v−u(1/4−u²−v²), v′=u−v(1/4−u²−v²) can blow up outside its small stable region, yet still reaches squared norm 2 from the unit circle using compact staging in 1≤u²+v²≤2. Thus global existence is one useful sufficient condition, not a necessary property of every successful finite reachability proof. For constrained variants, the initial target-false condition and open/closed domain requirements remain explicit. A closed annulus can permit reaching its outer boundary; excluding that boundary from the domain makes the same target impossible. The combined and compact-eventuality rules join higher derivatives, compact staging and domain refinement. The implementation offers an existential variant rule that lets arithmetic search find the positive derivative lower bound, with a manual-instantiation option to avoid that search. A semialgebraic variant rule handles disjunctions/conjunctions of polynomial inequalities through a progress formula and invariant reasoning, rather than adding a nonsmooth min/max function to the logic. Example 8 proves u′=−u eventually enters [−1,1]. Closed-domain refinement uses an interior-set obligation. Its practical strictification of nonstrict inequalities underapproximates that interior; Appendix B explicitly explains why it need not equal the interior, for example x≤x versus x<x. [Evidence: Q22, §7–7.1 implementation overview, existential and semialgebraic variants, PDF pp.24–26; Q23, §7.1 complete Example8, conjunction/disjunction progress and implementation assumptions, PDF pp.26–27; Q24, §7.1 complete Lemma26, Corollary27 and strictification explanation, PDF pp.27–27; Q34, AppendixB.4 complete Corollary27 proof and interior-underapproximation explanation, PDF pp.55–55; complete exact passages in quotes.json.]  Source (Q22, §7–7.1 implementation overview, existential and semialgebraic variants):  > 7 ODE Liveness Proofs in Practice > The preceding sections show how axiomatic refinement can be used to fruitfully navigate and understand the zoo of > ODE existence and liveness arguments from various applications (Table 1). The generality of the approach enables > the sound and foundational derivation of those arguments from a parsimonious basis of refinement steps. This section > provides a complementary study of how the refinement approach and its derived ODE existence and liveness proof > rules are best implemented in practice. There are two canonical approaches for such an implementation: > 1. Implement the foundational refinement steps and let users build their own arguments using those steps, e.g., by > following the derivations and proofs from Sections 4–6. > 2. Implement the zoo of proof rules from Sections 4–6 directly and let users pick from from those rules for their > particular ODE liveness applications. > The low-level flexibility of Approach 1 is also its drawback in practice because users need to tediously reconstruct > high-level ODE liveness arguments from basic refinements for each proof. Approach 2 provides users with those > high-level arguments but limits users to proof rules that have been implemented, which squanders the generality of the > refinement approach. Moreover, users would still need to navigate the redundancies and tradeoffs among the zoo of > proof rules to select one that is best-suited for their proof. To account for these drawbacks, this section advocates for > a middle ground between those two extremes: implementations should provide users with the basic refinement steps, > bundled with a set of carefully curated, high-level proof rules (Section 7.1) and associated proof support (Section 7.2) > that help users navigate the common cases in their liveness proofs. > These ideas are put into practice through an implementation of ODE existence and liveness proof rules in KeY- > maera X [FMQ+ > 15]. Proof rules and proof support are implemented as tactics in KeYmaera X [FMBP17], which > are not soundness-critical. Such an arrangement allows for the implementation of useful ODE liveness proof rules > and their associated proof support with KeYmaera X’s sound kernel as a safeguard against implementation errors or > mistakes in their derivations and side conditions. This core design decision underlying KeYmaera X is discussed > elsewhere [FMQ+ > 15, FMBP17, Pla17a]. All of the ODE liveness examples in this article have been formally proved > in KeYmaera X (Section 7.2.3). By leveraging existing infrastructure in KeYmaera X, the implementation can also be > used as part of liveness proofs for hybrid systems. It is used for the liveness proofs of a case study involving a robot > model driving along circular arcs in the plane [BTM+ > 19]. > The basic refinements steps from Section 3 and the proof rules in Sections 4–6 are mostly straightforward to > implement by following their respective proofs. Thus, Sections 7.1 and 7.2 focus on a select number of new proof > rules and proof support that are beneficial in the implementation. For the sake of completeness, syntactic derivations > of all liveness proof rules presented in these sections are given in Appendix B.4. > An Axiomatic Approach to Existence and Liveness for Differential Equations 25 > 7.1 Liveness Proof Rules > Atomic differential variants dV< is a useful primitive proof rule to implement in KeYmaera X because many ODE > liveness proof rules, e.g., dVM > = ,SP, derive from it. From a practical perspective though, rule dV< as presented in Corol- > lary 12 still requires users to provide a choice of the constant ε(), e.g., the proof in Example 4 uses ε() = 1 > 2 . The > following slight rephrasing of dV< enables a more automated implementation. > Corollary 24 (Existential atomic differential variants [Pla10]). The following proof rule (where < is either ≥ or >) > is derivable in dL, where ε is a fresh variable and ODE x0 > = f(x) has provable global solutions. > dV∃ > < > Γ ` ∃ε > 0 ∀x ¬(p < 0) → > . > p ≥ ε >  > Γ ` hx0 = f(x)ip < 0 > Proof Sketch (Proof in Appendix B.4). Rule dV∃ > < is derived from dV< as a corollary. > Just like rule dV<, rule dV∃ > < requires a positive lower bound ε > 0 on the derivative of p along solutions. The > difference is that the premise of rule dV∃ > < is rephrased to ask a purely arithmetical question about the existence of a > suitable choice for ε. This can be decided automatically to save user effort in identifying ε, but such automation comes > at added computational cost because the decision procedure must find a suitable instance of ε for the ∃ quantifier > (or decide that none exist) rather than simply check a user-provided instance. Thus, the implementation gives users > control over the desired degree of automation in their proof by giving them the option of either invoking an arithmetic > decision procedure R on the premise of dV∃ > < or manually instantiating the existential quantifier with a specific term for > ε. > Another useful variation of rule dV< is its semialgebraic generalization, i.e., where the goal region is described > by a formula P formed from conjunctions and disjunctions of (in)equalities. Rules dVM > = ,SP provide examples of such > a generalization, but they are indirect generalizations because users must still identify an underlying (atomic) differ- > ential variant p as input when applying either rule. In contrast, the new semialgebraic generalization of dV< below > directly examines the syntactic structure of the goal region described by formula P. Its implementation is enabled > by KeYmaera X’s ODE invariance proving capabilities which are, in turn, based on dL’s complete axiomatization for > ODE invariants [PT20]. > Corollary 25 (Semialgebraic differential variants). Let b be a fresh variable, and term ε() be constant for ODE > x0 > = f(x), t0 > = 1. Platzer > The intuition behind rule dV is similar to rule dV<: as long as the solution has not yet reached the goal P, it grows > towards P at “rate” ε(). Proof support obtains dependency groups by topologically sorting strongly connected components of the ODE variable-dependency graph. Affine groups can be handled automatically; nonlinear groups can need user-provided bounds, with an additional arithmetic method for an isolated univariate nonlinear group. Compatible invariant assumptions can strengthen box premises only when the antecedent ODE/domain permits at least the relevant trajectories. This is a controlled use of context: arbitrary initial-state assertions cannot be retained as if they were invariants. The canonical choice of such a dependency > order can be automatically produced by a tactic using a topological sort of the strongly connected components (SCCs)11 > of the dependency graph of the ODE. > More precisely, to prove global existence for an ODE x0 > = f(x), consider the dependency graph G where each > variable xi is a vertex and with a directed edge xi −→ xj if the RHS fj(x) for x0 > j depends on free variable xi. First, > compute the SCCs of G, and then topologically sort the SCCs. The groups of variables yi in dependency order can be > chosen according to the vertices in each SCC in topological order. On the other hand, the SCC y3 ≡ {x2, x6} has nonlinear dependencies on x2, x6, so users > are prompted to input a bounded set (or a bound on derivatives) over variables x2, x6 in order to prove global existence > for those variables. This continues similarly for the SCCs y2 (nonlinear dependency) and y1 (affine dependency) until > global existence is proved for the full ODE. This semi-automated proof support minimizes the manual effort required > of the user in proving global existence by focusing their attention on the automatically identified nonlinear parts of the > ODE that may cause finite-time blowup of solutions. > To drive global existence proof automation further, key special cases can be added to the method described above. > One such special case for univariate ODEs is shown below. > Remark 2 (Global existence for univariate ODEs). Consider the case where a variable group has just one variable and > no incoming dependencies, e.g., y2 ≡ {x3} in Fig. 4 or αb (7). Global existence for such univariate polynomials ODEs > is decidable [GBC08], even if the RHS is highly nonlinear, because all of its solutions either asymptotically approach > a root of the polynomial RHS or diverge to infinity. > This result is best illustrated through the dynamical systems view of ODEs shown in Fig. 5 for the ODE x0 > = > x4 > − 5x2 > + 4. This example ODE has global solutions from all initial states satisfying x ≤ r4 because the solution > from all such states are globally attracted to one of the fixed points. Conversely, for all other initial conditions (x > r4), > the ODE blows up in finite time because the RHS is quartic in x. > 11A strongly connected component of a directed graph is a maximal subset of vertices that are pairwise connected by paths. > An Axiomatic Approach to Existence and Liveness for Differential Equations 29 > x > f(x) > r1 r2 r3 r4 > Figure 5: The univariate ODE x0 > = x4 > − 5x2 > + 4 is can be illustrated by plotting its RHS f(x) = x4 > − 5x2 > + 4 > (vertical axis) against x (horizontal axis). Points on the horizontal axis evolve towards the right (red arrow) when > f(x) ≥ 0 and towards the left (blue arrow) when f(x) ≤ 0. The fixed points r1, r2, r3, r4 are roots of the polynomial > RHS where f(x) = 0. These fixed points either attract trajectories (like r1, r3), or repel them (like r2, r4). All points > on the horizontal axis evolve asymptotically towards exactly one fixed point or approach ∞. > More generally, for a nonlinear univariate polynomial ODE x0 > = f(x) and initial assumptions Γ, it suffices to > check validity of the following arithmetical sequent to decide global existence: > Γ ` ∃r f(r) = 0 ∧ (f(x) ≥ 0 ∧ r ≥ x > | {z } > a > ∨ f(x) ≤ 0 ∧ r ≤ x > | {z } > b > ) >  > The existentially quantified variable r corresponds to a fixed point (a root with f(r) = 0). Disjunct a checks > whether the solution approaches r from the left, e.g., the points between r2 and r3 in Fig. 5 approach r3 from the > left. An in-depth > discussion is available elsewhere [Pla18], but the idea is illustrated by the following derivation: > Γ ` [x0 > = f(x) & Q]C1 > . . . > Q ∧ C1 ∧ C2 ∧ · · · ∧ Cn ` P > dW > . > . > . > dC > Γ ` [x0 > = f(x) & Q ∧ C1 ∧ C2]P > dC > Γ ` [x0 > = f(x) & Q ∧ C1]P > dC > Γ ` [x0 > = f(x) & Q]P > The derivation uses a sequence of differential cut steps to progressively add the cuts C1, C2, . . . , Cn to the domain > constraint. A final dW step completes the proof when the postcondition P is already implied by the (now strengthened) > domain constraint. Intuitively, the differential cuts are akin to dynamical lemmas in this derivation. For example, by > proving the premise Γ ` [x0 > = f(x) & Q]C1, the cut C1 can now be assumed in the domain constraints of subsequent > steps. Just like the cut rule from sequent calculus, differential cuts dC allow safety proofs for ODEs to be staged > through a sequence of lemmas about those ODEs. > For proof modularity and maintainability, it is desirable to enable a similar staging for ODE liveness proofs. > Concretely, suppose that the formula [x0 > = f(x) & Q]C has been proved as a cut: > Γ ` [x0 > = f(x) & Q]C · · · > cut > Γ ` hx0 > = f(x) & QiP > The challenge is how to (soundly) use this lemma in subsequent derivation steps (shown as · · · ). Naı̈vely replacing > Q with Q ∧ C in the domain constraint of the succedent does not work. Platzer > The refinement-based approach to ODE liveness provides a natural answer: recall that each refinement step in the > chain (6) requires the user to prove an additional box modality formula. The insight is that, for these box modality > formulas, any relevant lemmas that have been proved can be soundly added to the domain constraint. For example, > suppose that rule Kh&i is used to continue the proof after the cut. The left premise of Kh&i can now be strengthened > to include C in its domain constraint: > Γ, [x0 > = f(x) & Q]C ` [x0 > = f(x) & Q ∧ ¬P ∧ C]¬G > dC > Γ, [x0 > = f(x) & Q]C ` [x0 > = f(x) & Q ∧ ¬P]¬G Γ, [x0 > = f(x) & Q]C ` hx0 > = f(x) & QiG > Kh&i > Γ, [x0 > = f(x) & Q]C ` hx0 > = f(x) & QiP > Users could manually track and apply lemmas using dC as shown above, but this becomes tedious in larger live- > ness proofs. The implementation instead provides users with tactics that automatically search the antecedents Γ for > compatible assumptions that can be used to strengthen the domain constraints. These tactics also use a form of ODE > unification when determining compatibility. More precisely, consider the sequent Γ ` [x0 > = f(x) & Q]P, which may > arise as a box refinement during a liveness proof. An antecedent formula [y0 > = g(y) & R]C in Γ is called a compatible > assumption for the succedent [x0 > = f(x) & Q]P if: > 1. The set of ODEs y0 > = g(y) is a subset of the set of ODEs x0 > = f(x). This is order-agnostic, e.g., the ODE > u0 > = v, v0 > = u is a subset of the ODE v0 > = u, u0 > = v, w0 > = u + v + w. > 2. The domain constraint Q implies domain constraint R, i.e., Q → R is valid. > Under these conditions, the ODE y0 > = g(y) & R permits more trajectories than the ODE x0 > = f(x) & Q. Thus, > if formula C is always true along solutions of the former ODE, then it also stays true along solutions of the latter. > Combining compatible assumptions with implementations of liveness proof rules yields turbo-charged versions of > those rules. For example, in rule dV∃ > <, instead of simply assuming the negation of the postcondition (¬(p < 0) → · · · ) > when determining the existence of suitable ε, all postconditions of compatible assumptions can be assumed, e.g., with > ¬(p < 0) ∧ C → · · · for postcondition C of a compatible assumption.  The authors report approximately 155 changed soundness-critical code lines and approximately 1,500 tactic lines. The tactic implementation is untrusted relative to the KeYmaera X kernel. They report formally proving the article's examples and external ODE cases, including liveness from a robot circular-arc model. This record did not run those proofs or examine the implementation's actual trusted base. A kernel-checked result remains an author-reported evaluation result here; the paper's printed derivations and the implemented proofs are separate evidence. K. Tan and A. Platzer > Table 1: Surveyed ODE liveness arguments with highlighting in blue for soundness-critical corrections identified in > this article. The applications (and corrections, if any) for each surveyed argument is briefly described here. The > referenced corollaries are corresponding derived proof rules with details of the corrections. > Application Without Domain Constraints With Domain Constraints > Hybrid systems verification [Pla10] OK (Cor. 12) if open/closed, initially false (Cor. 18) > Auto. ODE verification [PR05, PR07] [PR07, Rem. 3.6] is incorrect if conditions checked globally (Cor. 23) > Finding basin of attraction [RS10] if compact (Cor. 17) if compact (Cor. 20) > Staging set ODE liveness proofs [SJ15] OK (Cor. 15) OK (Cor. 21) > Switching logic synthesis [TT10] if global solutions (Cor. 13) if global solutions (Cor. 19) > by several difficulties: i) solutions of ODEs may converge towards a goal without ever reaching it, ii) solutions of > nonlinear ODEs may blow up in finite time leaving insufficient time for the desired goal to be reached, and iii) the goal > may be reachable but only by (illegally) leaving the evolution domain constraint. In contrast, invariance properties > for ODEs are better understood [Pla10, GP14, LZZ11] and have a complete dL axiomatization [PT20]. Motivated by > the aforementioned difficulties, this article presents dL axioms enabling systematic, step-by-step refinement of ODE > liveness properties, where each step is justified using an ODE invariance property. This refinement approach is a > powerful framework for understanding ODE liveness arguments because it brings the full deductive power of dL’s > ODE invariance proof rules to bear on liveness proofs. Using invariance (or safety) properties to deduce liveness is > a well-known proof technique for (discrete) concurrent systems [MP92, OL82]. This article shows that those ideas > work just as well in the continuous setting—as long as the aforementioned difficulties are appropriately handled. > To demonstrate the applicability of the approach, this article surveys several arguments from the literature and > derives them all as (corrected) dL proof rules, see Table 1. This logical presentation has two key benefits: > • The proof rules are syntactically derived from sound axioms of dL, which guarantees their correctness. Many > of the surveyed arguments contain subtle soundness errors (Table 1, middle and right). These errors do not > diminish the surveyed work. Rather, they emphasize the need for an axiomatic, uniform way of presenting and > analyzing ODE liveness arguments instead of relying on ad hoc approaches. > • The approach identifies common refinement steps that form a basis for the surveyed liveness arguments drawn > from various applications (Table 1, left). This library of building blocks enables sound development and jus- > tification of new ODE liveness proof rules, e.g., by generalizing individual refinement steps or by exploring > different combinations of those steps. Corollaries 14, 16, and 22 are examples of new ODE liveness proof rules > that can be derived and justified from the uniform approach that this article follows. > This article extends the authors’ earlier conference version [TP19]. The key new insight is that all of the aforemen- > tioned liveness arguments (Table 1) are based on reducing liveness properties of ODEs to assumptions about sufficient > existence duration for their solutions. In fact, many of those arguments become significantly simpler (and sound) when > the ODEs of concern are assumed to have global solutions, i.e., they do not blow up in finite time. It is reasonable > and commonplace to make such an assumption for the continuous dynamics in models of CPSs [Alu15, Section 6]. > After all, mechanical systems do not simply cease to exist after a short time! An example from control theory is Lya- > punov stability which guarantees global solutions near stable equilibria [Kha92, Definition 4.1][HC08, Theorem 3.1]. > Control systems are designed to always operate near stable equilibria and so always have global solutions. Logically > though, making an a priori assumption of global existence for ODEs means that the correctness of any subsequent > verification results for the ODEs and hybrid system models are conditional on an unproved existence duration hypoth- > esis. While global existence is known to hold for linear systems, even the simplest nonlinear ODEs (see Section 4) > fail to meet the hypothesis without further assumptions. This article therefore adopts the view that (global) existence > should be proved rather than assumed for the continuous dynamics in hybrid systems. > The new contributions of this article beyond the authors’ earlier conference version [TP19] are: > • Section 4 presents deductive dL proofs of global existence for ODE solutions. Together with the liveness proofs > of Sections 5 and 6, this enables unconditional proofs of ODE liveness properties entirely within the uniform > An Axiomatic Approach to Existence and Liveness for Differential Equations 3 > dL refinement framework without existence presuppositions. > • Section 7 shows how to apply the insights from Sections 4–6 in practice. This includes: i) the design of proof > rules that are practically useful and well-suited for implementation (Section 7.1) and ii) the design of proof > support to aid users in existence and liveness proofs (Section 7.2). > The practical insights of Section 7 are drawn from an implementation of ODE liveness proof rules in the KeY- > maera X theorem prover for hybrid systems [FMQ+ > 15]. The unconditional liveness proofs enabled by Sections 4–6 fit > particularly well with an implementation in KeYmaera X because axiomatic refinement closely mirrors KeYmaera X’s > design principles. KeYmaera X implements dL’s uniform substitution calculus [Pla17a] and it is designed to mini- > mize the soundness-critical code that has to be trusted in order to guarantee its verification results. On top of this > soundness-critical core, KeYmaera X’s non-soundness-critical tactics framework [FMBP17] adds support and au- > tomation for proofs. Liveness proofs are similarly based on a series of small refinement steps which are, in turn, > implemented as (untrusted) tactics based on a small basis of derived refinement axioms. More complicated liveness > arguments, such as those from Table 1 or from new user insights, are implemented by piecing those tactics together > using tactic combinators [FMBP17]. The implementation required minor changes to ≈155 lines of soundness-critical > code in KeYmaera X, while the remaining ≈1500 lines implement ODE existence and liveness proof rules as tactics. > These additions suffice to prove all of the examples in this article and in ODE models elsewhere [SJ15, BTM+ > 19] > (Section 7.2.3). > Throughout this article, core dL axioms underlying the refinement approach are presented as lemmas, which are > summarized and proved in Appendix A. Existence and liveness proof rules that are derived syntactically from those ax- > ioms, e.g., Table 1, are listed as corollaries and their derivations are given in Appendix B. Counterexamples explaining > the soundness errors in Table 1 are given in Appendix C.  Table 2 has 11 rows: six manual/automatic pairs for this article's examples, three external Sogokon/Jackson cases, and two robot properties. That is 17 proof configurations, rather than 17 independent application scenarios. Metrics are manual tactic steps, internal kernel steps, and execution seconds averaged over five runs on Ubuntu 18.04, a 2.70 GHz Intel Core i7-6820HQ and 16 GB memory. Example 8 drops from 28 manual tactic steps to one automated step, while time rises from 0.575 to 0.990 seconds and kernel steps from 1,747 to 2,571. Automation is slower in four of the six pairs; it is faster in Examples 2 and 4. This measures proof-execution cost and user tactic counts, not user-study time, LLM success or broad scalability. No independent reproduction is claimed. Table 2 provides a summary of statistics from these proofs: “Tactic Steps” counts the > number of (manual) user proof steps; “Kernel Steps” counts the number of internal steps taken by the soundness- > critical KeYmaera X kernel; and “Proof Time” measures the time taken (in seconds, averaged over 5 runs, rounded > to 3 decimal places) for the proof to execute in KeYmaera X. All experiments were run on an Ubuntu 18.04 laptop > with a 2.70 GHz Intel Core i7-6820HQ CPU and 16GB memory. None of the proofs have been optimized to favor > any specific metric. The specific timings and proof steps are naturally subject to change on different hardware and > as various aspects of the KeYmaera X theorem prover are improved. Nevertheless, the key takeaways from these > microbenchmarks remain broadly applicable. > (M)anual and (A)utomatic Proofs. The implementation provides users with powerful proof support but also ex- > poses low-level primitives for users who prefer more fine-grained control over (parts of) their proofs. Both types of > proofs are shown for this article’s examples in Table 2. Proofs that heavily exploit the proof support and automation are > more convenient for users and require fewer manual tactic invocations. This gap is most pronounced for Example 8, > where the 28 step manual proof requires just one automated dV step. > On the other hand, the automated proofs are slower than their manual counterparts on four out of six examples. > Most of this overhead arises when there is significant proof search in the automation. In particular, the automated > proof of Example 1 is significantly slower and requires almost six times more kernel steps compared to its manual > counterpart. This gap arises because the automated proof uses the decision procedure for univariate global existence > outlined in Remark 2 while the manual proof uses the direct argument in Example 1. However, the latter proof required > user insight about the physical system. This illustrates the need for a flexible implementation that lets users navigate > the convenience and efficiency tradeoff according to their needs and proof insights. > An Axiomatic Approach to Existence and Liveness for Differential Equations 31 > Table 2: Proof statistics for ODE existence and liveness properties proved using the implementation. Counterexample 9 adds an irrelevant-looking coordinate u′=u², u(0)=1, to v′=1, v(0)=0: u blows up at time 1, preventing the target v=2 despite constant progress of v. Counterexample 10 begins outside domain u≤1, making the diamond false even with target u≥0 initially true. Counterexample 11 demands u>1 while staying in u≤1. Counterexample 12 uses u′=0,v′=1 from (0,1), closed unit-disk domain and open-disk target; coincident boundaries make a surveyed boundary condition vacuous, and the solution exits immediately. Counterexample 13 uses a bounded open domain with the slice v=0 removed; the upward trajectory reaches the target only after crossing that forbidden slice. These are source-supplied examples against the specific earlier statements quoted in the report, not new independent checks of those original publications. K. Tan and A. Platzer > C.1 Finite-Time Blow Up > The soundness errors identified in Section 5 all arise because of incorrect handling of the fact that solutions may blow > up in finite time. This phenomenon is studied in detail in Section 4, and it is illustrated by αn (2), see Fig. 1, or αb (7), > see Example 1. The following is a counterexample for the original presentation of dV= (and dVM > = ,dV=&,dVM > = &) [TT10]. > Similar counterexamples can be constructed for [PR07, Remark 3.6] and for the original presentation of SLyap,SLyap& [RS10]. > Counterexample 9. Consider rule dV= without the restriction that the ODE has provable global solutions. This unre- > stricted rule, denoted dV=, is unsound as shown by the following derivation using it with ε()=1: > ∗ > R > v − 2 < 0 ` 1 ≥ 1 > dV=v − 2 ≤ 0 ` hu0 > = u2 > , v0 > = 1iv − 2 = 0 > The conclusion of this derivation is not valid. Consider the initial state ω with values ω(u) = 1 and ω(v) = 0. > The explicit solution of the ODE from ω is given by u(t) = 1 > 1−t , v(t) = t for t ∈ [0, 1). This solution does not > exist beyond the time interval [0, 1) because the u-coordinate asymptotically approaches ∞, i.e., blows up, as time > approaches t = 1. It is impossible to reach a state satisfying v − 2 = 0 from ω along this solution since at least 2 time > units are required. > This counterexample further illustrates the difficulty in handling nonlinear ODEs. Neither the precondition (v − > 2 ≤ 0) nor postcondition (v − 2 = 0) mention the variable u, and the ODEs u0 > = u2 > , v0 > = 1 do not depend > on variables v, u respectively, so it is tempting to disregard the variable u entirely. - Context-bounded multithreaded liveness (2020)
    - title: Context-Bounded Verification of Liveness Properties for Multithreaded Shared-Memory Programs
    - what: Proves decidability and complexity results for termination, fair non-termination and fair starvation of dynamically spawning recursive threads under a fixed context-switch bound, introducing VASS with balloons as the key intermediate model. The paper formalizes Dynamic Networks of Concurrent Pushdown Systems (DCPS), thread identities, scheduler swaps/resumptions and fairness. Ordinary termination reduces through downward closures to VASS non-termination, yielding the 2EXPSPACE result. Fairness is not downward-closure preserving, so VASS with balloons stores semi-linear multisets of future spawns; progressive runs and a shallow-run theorem reduce DCPS fair behavior to VASSB reachability, then to ordinary VASS reachability. A separate Ramsey/pumping and consistency construction reduces starvation to fair termination. The paper gives proof machinery rather than an implementation or empirical benchmark.
    - number: For K ≥ 1, K-bounded non-termination is 2EXPSPACE-complete
    - number: For every K, fair non-termination and fair starvation are decidable
    - number: Fair decision procedures are non-elementary, already for K=0 via VASS reachability
    - number: VASSB has decidable progressive runs and every progressive run has a shallow counterpart
    - limits: The bound K is fixed per decision instance and fairness means every thread ready infinitely often is eventually scheduled (with identity-equivalence subtleties handled by an angelic scheduler argument). Results concern the DCPS model: finite global state, pushdown stacks, shared global control and restricted scheduler rules. Fair problems are decidable but non-elementary; unbounded context switching remains outside the decidability claim.
    - how much was read: most of it read

Experiments cover software doping, mutual exclusion, dining cryptographers and symmetry properties, checking precise alternating formulas rather than universal approximations.

- Lively RaTT guarded-recursion liveness (2020)
    - title: Diamonds are not forever: Liveness in reactive programming with guarded recursion
    - what: Introduces Lively RaTT, a modal functional-reactive language combining guarded fixed points with an LTL-like until type constructor. A step-indexed Kripke logical relation proves productivity, causality, termination, liveness, fairness, and absence of implicit space leaks. Separates the LTL time-step modality from guarded recursion’s modality, defines events and fair streams, gives evaluation/step/reactive semantics, and proves metatheoretic properties through worlds, support, and logical relations. The system explains why unrestricted identification of the modalities breaks termination guarantees.
    - number: No runtime benchmark; guarantees are metatheoretic (§5)
    - number: Proves productivity, causality, termination, liveness, fairness and no implicit space leaks (Abstract)
    - number: Future extension to dependent/temporal inductive types (§7)
    - limits: The language is a typed functional/reactive calculus with guarded recursion and specific modal/temporal types; results are semantic and do not provide empirical performance or automatic model checking. Expressing termination and richer dependent specifications remains future work, and ordinary inductive/coinductive extensions require care.

- NetSMC: A Custom Symbolic Model Checker for Stateful Network Verification (2020)
    - what: NetSMC checks temporal policies on models of stateful network functions with persistent tables and one packet traversing the network at a time. It translates a restricted LTL language into CTL and represents sets of network states as existential first-order formulas. Backward preimages and a containment algorithm adapted from database query containment avoid general solver costs. A packet-order argument gives equivalence to interleaved executions for selected policies under an order-preservation condition. Benchmarks compare VMN, a Z3 containment variant, NuSMV and Cubicle; author-run exercises inject configuration mistakes into real network functions. Records exact NF/packet transition semantics, commutation and order-preservation conditions, restricted temporal operators and full appendix proofs, EFO preimage and containment construction, proof/implementation boundaries, printed listing/formula concerns, every Table2 timing and the narrow scopes of speedup, planted faults and model fidelity.
    - limits: §§4/8: A fixed topology and NF rule set; packets contain modeled header/application fields, not arbitrary payload processing. State is a collection of finite key-value maps; tests use specified finite domains, updates assign modeled expressions or increment/decrement by constants, expressions may choose nondeterministically from a domain. No multicast/broadcast, averages, generic computations, clocks/timeouts or direct network failures. Failures require separate enumerated scenarios. Integer/counter bounds and overflow semantics are not developed into an implementation-level finite-state proof. §§4.1/4.3/AppendixA: Exactly one matching NF rule; sequential commands are atomic at NF network-step granularity. A link preserves packet and tables. Drop/Exit permits an arbitrary next packet at arbitrary ingress, retaining all tables. Correct rules/topology/initial tables/model extraction are trusted; sequential packets do not mean tables reset between packets. Nondeterministic backend choice abstracts a concrete policy rather than verifies its implementation. §4.3/AppendixC: Every packet eventually leaves or drops; transient loops allowed, indefinite loops excluded. Unique immutable IDs distinguish packets. Interleaved model buffers unordered sets, not FIFO queues, and proof considers finite closed traces with no unfinished nonterminal packets. No general liveness/fairness or live controller-update verification. AppendixC/Definitions4–5/Lemma1: Non-conflict means swapping two same-NF processing steps preserves both output packets and final table valuation for every relevant initial valuation. A strict total packet order must be respected wherever packets conflict. Adjacent inversion swaps decrease an out-of-order index until packets can be serialized with identical per-packet located-packet traces. Conditions are assumptions on networks; no checker that establishes them for arbitrary input models is evaluated. §5/AppendicesB/D: Policies universally quantify outer variables; basic predicates inspect fields/location/tables. Permitted temporal forms are basic, F basic, within-packet eventual f basic, X(guard→formula), G(guard→formula), and within-packet g(guard→formula). f/g expand to restricted strong-until formulas terminated by Drop/Exit. General until, arbitrary quantifier alternation, generic temporal nesting/past properties excluded. A positive answer for arbitrary policies under serialization does not establish concurrent correctness. §5/AppendixD/Theorem7/Corollaries2,8: Conditional equivalence explicitly covers isolation, tag preservation and tag-based isolation on order-preserving networks. Other policies such as flow affinity/NAT consistency/service chaining are checked under the one-packet model and may miss interleaving bugs. Do not read Table1 sound/conditionally-complete labels as unconditional verification of all concurrent deployments. AppendixD qualified proof-scope concern: Theorem7 syntactically uses basic guards without explicitly excluding table predicates, although Lemma1 preserves only located-packet input/output traces, not every intermediate table valuation while a packet waits. Reordered unrelated processing can alter such valuations. The printed transfer argument therefore needs a restriction to predicates determined by those traces or an additional preservation proof; isolation/tag examples avoid this issue. This is a reading concern, not an executed counterexample against the tool. §6.2: EFO state formulas are disjunctions of conjunctive clauses with membership, inequality, location/field equality and table lookup. Variables are existential and outermost; denotation is a set of concrete packet/table valuations. This is a symbolic representation, not sampling of packet histories. §6.3/Algorithms1–2: Preimage unions NF-rule, link and new-packet predecessors. Commands processed in reverse order; tests conjoined. New-packet transition drops successor field/location constraints but preserves their variable/table relationships, restricts successor ingress and predecessor Drop/Exit. Table updates split equal-index overwritten entry versus an unequal index retaining old value. Helper expression translation F and link routine are omitted; precise implementation behavior is not reconstructed from pseudocode alone. §6.4/Algorithm3: Interpret clauses as conjunctive queries; homomorphism maps variables of candidate containing clause into contained clause. Inequalities require splitting equality patterns into atomic clauses; finite domains need emptiness checks (e.g., singleton variables forcing incompatible table values). Every nonempty atomic clause must map to some disjunct of target. This avoids a naive implication query to Z3, while still potentially expanding many clauses. §6.4/Theorem3 and AppendixE: Authors state exact True iff satisfaction under one-packet semantics. Appendices provide detailed semantics, trace reordering, policy transfer and structural LTL→CTL arguments (Theorem9/Lemmas10–12); no separate complete proof of Algorithm1/2 preimages, Algorithm3 domain splitting/emptiness, or Python refinement appears. Theorem3 is not a machine-checked verified implementation. Algorithm2/p188 printed-listing concerns: loop binds β_j on line14 but line15 passes β_i, and line21 recursive COMPUTECLS call omits expression-environment arguments present in its signature. Figure11/p196 sequence rule leaves c2 as a pending command without giving the corresponding continuation rule in the displayed semantics. Treat as incomplete/inconsistent presentation; no artifact tested. AppendixF/p200 printed-policy concerns: NAT consistency formula contains p without the displayed outer binder (only ∀i), despite closed-formula requirement. Flow affinity shown for S1 needs analogous instances for other servers. Conditional reachability uses G(loc=B→f(loc=A)) from the same arrival state at B, so without distinguishing request arrival from new reply injection it can demand that the current packet return to A; the prose speaks of packets sent afterwards. Qualified formula/specification boundary, not a measured tool failure. §7: Python prototype,20core2.8GHz/128GB server. CPU model/OS/Python/solver versions, source LOC, repeated-run statistics, confidence intervals and memory usage not reported. Alembic-generated NF models and manual POX translation used; authors assert high fidelity, but no formal extraction/refinement theorem or measured model-fidelity error rate is provided. §7.1/Figures6–8: Synthetic host/rule scaling and extended fattree/Ai3/Sprint topology isolation checks; flow-space sliced for both tools. Specific speedups depend on policy/model and baseline. VMN lacks service-chain policy support, so policy-complexity comparison uses only NetSMC/Z3. NuSMV fixed16-entry table and NetSMC without that size constraint are different encodings; Cubicle750× lacks detailed absolute timing/setup in text. §7.2: Red team authors2/3 inject secret deleted/modified rules; blue team author1 diagnoses with NetSMC, then rechecks fixed model. Four described real-NF exercises and nine modeled Table2 scenarios all succeed as reported; no production-discovered fault count, exhaustive fault set, detection rate, false-positive rate or independent user study. All benchmark/tool outcomes here are authors reports, not reproduced.

- Presburger stage graphs (2020)
    - title: Checking Qualitative Liveness Properties of Replicated Systems with Stochastic Scheduling.
    - what: Develops Presburger stage graphs for sound and complete verification of stable-termination/liveness properties of replicated systems with an unknown number of indistinguishable agents and stochastic scheduling. A stage graph is a finite DAG of inductive Presburger configuration sets with ranking certificates. The authors give a decidability/completeness result, an incomplete practical constructor using Z3, well-quasi-order and Petri-net techniques, and evaluate it on population protocols, leader election and mutual exclusion. The paper formalizes replicated systems as 1-conservative Petri/VAS-style multiset rewriting and assumes a scheduler whose transition probabilities depend only on the current configuration and are nonzero for every enabled transition. Proposition 2 characterizes fair-run progress by ranking certificates; Proposition 3 and Theorem 2 establish stage-graph soundness/completeness, while Theorem 3 reduces candidate checking to Presburger satisfiability. Algorithm 1 starts from a potential-reachability overapproximation, computes eventually dead transitions (linear ranking or layer functions), and splits stages when needed. Exact dead-state sets are computable via backward reachability but can be doubly exponential; the practical procedure is incomplete. Z3 experiments build mostly chains of at most three stages (five for approximate majority), verify infinite families of protocols, and produce independently checkable invariant/ranking certificates.
    - number: Z3 implementation on Intel Xeon E5-2630 v4 2.20GHz, 8GB RAM
    - number: timeout 1 hour; most stage graphs ≤3 stages, approximate-majority graphs 5 stages
    - number: fast-majority instances up to m=21,d=1: 466s; m=21,d=20: 3301s (timeouts shown)
    - number: flock-of-birds c=80: 1217s; threshold c=8: 1089s; remainder m=20: 1646s
    - limits: Systems are finite-state agent programs with fixed population size per run, clique-style rendezvous/multiway transitions, and stochastic scheduling satisfying the two stated probability assumptions. The complete decision problem has non-elementary complexity; the implemented constructor may fail or time out and uses existential Presburger formulas/linear certificates. Population-protocol families are checked instance by instance, not universally over the family parameter. The method addresses qualitative almost-sure stable liveness, not quantitative probabilities, arbitrary fairness notions, Byzantine faults, or Rust implementation details.

- An Axiomatic Approach to Liveness for Differential Equations (2019)
    - what: # Continuous reachability from invariants, with explicit duration and boundary conditions (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Quotation fidelity: the anchored passages below reproduce raw PDF extraction exactly, not faithful mathematical typography. Extraction prints the generic comparison symbol for either ≥ or > as <, and its dual as 4; it also drops overbars for topological closure and flattens derivatives/fractions. Use the separately labelled PDF-checked transcriptions and original page images for mathematics. The raw offsets and text remain unchanged.  Tan and Platzer present a deductive framework for finite-time reachability of ordinary differential equations (ODEs) in differential dynamic logic (dL), a logic for discrete and continuous programs. This record reads the complete 37-page arXiv:1904.07984v2 report, dated 10 July 2019, including Appendices A–C and all 31 references. The paper derives liveness rules from a small collection of refinement and existence axioms, and gives counterexamples to several earlier rules. These are paper proofs and examples. This version reports paper proofs and examples, with no implementation evaluation or proof microbenchmarks. [Evidence: Q0, Title, complete abstract and §1 motivation, PDF pp.1–2; Q1, §1 complete survey table, claimed corrections and contribution scope, PDF pp.2–3; Q14, §7 complete conclusion and acknowledgments, PDF pp.15–16; complete exact passages in quotes.json.]  Source (Q0, Title, complete abstract and §1 motivation):  > An Axiomatic Approach to Liveness > for Differential Equations > Yong Kiam Tan André Platzer ∗ > Abstract > This paper presents an approach for deductive liveness verification for ordinary differen- > tial equations (ODEs) with differential dynamic logic. Numerous subtleties complicate the > generalization of well-known discrete liveness verification techniques, such as loop variants, > to the continuous setting. For example, ODE solutions may blow up in finite time or their > progress towards the goal may converge to zero. Our approach handles these subtleties by > successively refining ODE liveness properties using ODE invariance properties which have a > well-understood deductive proof theory. This approach is widely applicable: we survey several > liveness arguments in the literature and derive them all as special instances of our axiomatic re- > finement approach. We also correct several soundness errors in the surveyed arguments, which > further highlights the subtlety of ODE liveness reasoning and the utility of our deductive ap- > proach. The library of common refinement steps identified through our approach enables both > the sound development and justification of new ODE liveness proof rules from our axioms. > Keywords: differential equations, liveness, differential dynamic logic > 1 Introduction > Hybrid systems are mathematical models describing discrete and continuous dynamics, and inter- > actions thereof [6]. This flexibility makes them natural models of cyber-physical systems (CPSs) > which feature interactions between discrete computational control and continuous real world physics > [2, 19]. Formal verification of hybrid systems is of significant practical interest because the CPSs > they model frequently operate in safety-critical settings. Verifying properties of the continuous > dynamics is a key aspect of any such endeavor. > This paper focuses on deductive liveness verification for continuous dynamics described by > ordinary differential equations (ODEs). We work with differential dynamic logic (dL) [16, 17, 19], > a logic for deductive verification of hybrid systems, which compositionally lifts our results to the > hybrid systems setting. Methods for proving liveness in the discrete setting are well-known: loop > variants show that discrete loops eventually reach a desired goal, while temporal logic is used to > specify and study liveness properties in concurrent and infinitary settings [12, 13]. In the contin- > uous setting, liveness for an ODE means that its solutions eventually enter a desired goal region > ∗ > Computer Science Department, Carnegie Mellon University, Pittsburgh, USA {yongkiat|aplatzer}@cs.cmu.edu > arXiv:1904.07984v2 > [cs.LO] > 10 > Jul > 2019 > Y. K. Tan and A. Platzer An Axiomatic Approach to Liveness for Differential Equations  The model uses autonomous polynomial ODEs over real states, rational-polynomial terms and first-order real-arithmetic domain/target formulas. Polynomial fields have unique right-maximal local solutions, whose maximum duration can be finite. A diamond property requires some finite solution segment to reach its target while satisfying the domain at every point, including both endpoints. Duration zero can succeed only when target and domain initially hold. Parameters remain constant; topological conditions concern sets over the evolving ODE variables with parameters fixed. This finite continuous reachability property is separate from concurrent-system fairness or infinitely recurring progress. Background completeness is for real-arithmetic ODE invariants, not all liveness. DR changes the domain when a box invariant justifies doing so. K<&> establishes that a known reachable region cannot be reached before the desired target. Existence axiom GEx supplies arbitrary clock duration for globally Lipschitz ODEs. BEx instead says that a solution reaches the requested clock threshold or exits a bounded region. Appendix B proves these existence axioms semantically using global solution existence or the unbounded norm near finite-time blow-up.
