The following axioms provide a means of formally establishing such an > initial liveness property:  Domain changes need more than an invariant restricted to the target's negation. COR requires an initially false target and matching open/open or closed/closed topology of target and new domain. SAR replaces the until-condition with the negation of target-and-domain together; its proof uses local progress into a semialgebraic set or its complement. Appendix B additionally handles closed targets or open domains without that general local-progress step. Appendix A explains decidable real-arithmetic topology checks and sufficient syntactic tests. Invariant rules generally discard initial-state assertions when reasoning along trajectories; constant-parameter assertions can remain. For soundness though, axiom COR has additional topological side conditions on > formulas P, Q so it can only be used when these conditions are met. Its proof crucially relies on Q being a formula of > real arithmetic so that the set it characterizes has tame topological behavior [3], see the proof > in Appendix B for more details.6  Corollary 5's explicit-duration differential variant requires a fixed positive derivative bound until the target and a premise ensuring enough evolution time. Its shorter rule assumes the entire ODE is globally Lipschitz. The equational variants, higher-Lie-derivative rule and ordinary staging rule likewise use global Lipschitz continuity in this version. Merely local Lipschitz continuity gives no global-duration guarantee. A derivative that stays positive but tends toward zero also need not yield finite reachability. Higher derivatives provide a polynomial-in-time lower bound, whose positive leading coefficient eventually forces progress. Bounded staging removes the global Lipschitz requirement: the trajectory stays in a bounded staging region until the target, and one fixed positive derivative forces it to exit. Compact staging needs only a strictly positive derivative because a continuous derivative attains a positive minimum there. The set-Lyapunov rule requires compact K and open target P; the authors explain that a surveyed merely closed-K statement omitted boundedness needed by its proof. These conditions distinguish sufficient finite-duration reasoning from assuming all trajectories last forever. Example 1 proves that the linear spiral u′=−v−u, v′=u−v reaches an annular target from the unit circle. For p=1/4−u²−v² the derivative is at least 1/2 before reaching squared radius 1/4, giving an analytic time threshold 3/2. Continuity then yields the circle and enclosing sup-norm annulus. The trajectory does not reach the origin in finite time; its shrinking rate tends to zero. Example 2 uses the nonlinear spiral u′=−v−u(1/4−u²−v²), v′=u−v(1/4−u²−v²). Compact staging in 1≤u²+v²≤2 and derivative at least 3/2 prove it reaches squared norm 2 before its later blow-up. The 2/3 threshold is an analytic bound, not a measured runtime. Example 3 strengthens the nonlinear reachability proof to stay in the closed annulus. A half-open annulus omitting its outer boundary cannot reach the same outer-boundary goal. The final combined rule joins compact staging, a strictly positive higher derivative and domain refinement. Counterexample 4 adds u′=u², u(0)=1, to v′=1, v(0)=0: blow-up at time 1 prevents v=2 despite constant progress of v. Counterexample 5 begins outside domain u≤1 although target u≥0 holds initially. Counterexample 6 requires u>1 while staying in u≤1. Counterexample 7 starts upward flow u′=0,v′=1 at (0,1), with closed unit-disk domain and open-disk target; coincident boundaries make a surveyed boundary condition vacuous, while the trajectory exits immediately. Counterexample 8 uses an open bounded disk with v=0 removed; upward trajectories from its lower part reach the upper target only after crossing the forbidden slice. - Characteristic Formulae for Liveness Properties of Non-Terminating CakeML Programs (2019)
    - what: Extends CakeML characteristic formulae and Hoare triples to prove total-correctness liveness properties of diverging programs. Divergent results carry lazy I/O traces; a clocked big-step semantics defines divergence as timeout at every clock and the supremum of finite-clock I/O prefixes. A transfinite-induction rule for repeat loops reduces proofs to terminating loop-body triples plus a limit-stream equation. A verified source transformation rewrites tail-recursive functions to repeat form. The framework is mechanized in HOL4 and integrated with CakeML. The semantics adds Div and FFIDiv results and POSTd predicates while preserving local separation reasoning. The repeat rule has base, successor and limit cases and a limited_parts side condition controlling all FFI resources. The transformation injects a runtime error in non-tail return branches so a transformed terminating function cannot be incorrectly treated as diverging; it is proved sound only for POSTd. Examples include silent pureLoop, conditional condLoop, infinite/finite catLoop over lazy input, cyclic pointerLoop, uncurried myRepeat, and a seL4 UAV filter. The filter proof shrinks from 1,971 lines of direct operational-semantics HOL to 1,479 lines with CF and yields an operational theorem; compiler liveness transfer remains conditional on no out-of-memory.
    - number: {'printed_pages': 19, 'filter_cf_hol_lines': 1479, 'filter_direct_hol_lines': 1971, 'filter_message_bound': 256, 'myrepeat_proof_lines': 8, 'cat_proof_approx_lines': 90}
    - limits: Divergence semantics is a supremum of I/O traces over all clock values; divergent runs have no final heap state. The repeat transformation currently handles single-argument tail-recursive, non-mutually-recursive functions; mutual recursion, curried/multiple arguments and recursion through store are future work. The induction principle requires limited_parts to bound possible FFI calls and user proofs of each terminating iteration and the infinite trace equation. The seL4 filter assumes infinite input, null termination and message length ≤256; the paper says the communication backend enforces these. Binary liveness after CakeML compilation is conditional on not running out of memory; no unconditional space bound is proved here. Formal results are HOL4/CakeML proofs, not Verus/Rust or TLA+ specifications and not LLM-generated proofs.

- IBFT 2.0 (2019)
    - title: IBFT 2.0: A Safe and Live Variation of the IBFT Blockchain Consensus Protocol for Eventually Synchronous Networks
    - what: Defines the IBFT 2.0 proof-of-authority BFT protocol with immediate finality, dynamic validator sets, and eventual-synchrony robustness. It gives pseudocode and a high-level lemma-based proof of optimal safety, persistence, weak liveness, and (with a fair-network probability assumption) liveness for up to floor((n−1)/3) Byzantine validators. The protocol removes IBFT locking and relies on prepared certificates, round-change certificates, and quorum commit seals. Height-tagged messages make lagging nodes request missing finalized blocks, addressing persistence. Safety derives from quorum intersections and induction over rounds (Lemmas 1–7); weak liveness derives from exponential round timers and eventual synchrony (Lemmas 8–17). Full liveness additionally assumes every sent message has positive probability of delivery within one-third of the initial timeout, then uses round-robin proposer selection and stable validator windows (Lemmas 18–23). The paper proposes ten concrete improvements, including fair proposer selection to remove the probabilistic assumption, two-phase fast path, quorum-triggered round advancement, hash certificates, reduced signatures, and aggregate signatures.
    - number: f(n)=floor((n−1)/3)
    - number: Quorum(n)=ceil(2n/3)
    - number: 4Δ synchrony window in Lemma 16
    - number: two-phase fast path claims 33% latency reduction
    - limits: Eventually synchronous gossip after GST with fixed delay Δ; cryptographic hash collision resistance and signature uniqueness/unforgeability; at most floor((n−1)/3) Byzantine validators per height; honest chains agree through h−1. Liveness (as distinct from weak liveness) depends on Fair Network Behaviour Assumption unless FairProposer is adopted. Formal specification and implementation details are explicitly future work; validator voting pseudocode is omitted.

- Ivy liveness-to-safety (2018)
    - title: Reducing liveness to safety in first-order logic
    - what: After a finite prefix of an execution, picks a finite projection of the state (the freeze point). Proves that no two later states can agree on that projection while all fairness obligations are met. This turns a progress proof into a safety proof about a finite bad pattern. Cases: ticket, alternating bit, TLB shootdown, Paxos, Multi-Paxos, Stoppable Paxos.
    - number: finding the invariants 'requires a sophisticated user'; conjecture counts for liveness are '2 - 4 times higher' than for safety (§7.2, pp.26:27-28)
    - number: six cases: ticket protocol, alternating bit, TLB shootdown, Paxos, Multi-Paxos, Stoppable Paxos
    - number: checking takes 3.7 to 219 seconds excluding invariant discovery
    - number: liveness needs two to four times as many invariant conjectures as safety (§7.2)
    - limits: The user still supplies the inductive invariants (facts preserved by every step). Humans applied the reduction by hand. Paxos assumes that the actions of a chosen leader or quorum are fair, that messages are eventually delivered, and that competing proposals eventually stop. Multi-Paxos and Stoppable Paxos cover one incarnation with fixed membership. The comparison counts invariants, not person-time.

- Symbolic liveness analysis (2018)
    - title: Symbolic Liveness Analysis of Real-World Software
    - what: Defines a generic liveness property for real-world programs—eventually consuming input or terminating—and implements symbolic lasso detection in KLEE to find violations. The paper formalizes transition systems with productive actions, deterministic computation except for input, and finite memory; it proves that any violating trace has a finite stem followed by a non-productive cycle. The implementation tracks per-path state fingerprints (register/memory fragments plus serialized path constraints), uses symbolic execution to cover input space, and reports concrete reproducing inputs. Evaluation covers GNU Coreutils, BusyBox and toybox, including interpreter behavior in sed, runaway counters, allocation-sensitive pointers and state explosion. Five previously unknown defects (three Coreutils, two BusyBox) were confirmed and fixed; all reported violations had concrete witnesses.
    - number: 354 tools evaluated
    - number: 5 previously unknown defects: 3 Coreutils and 2 BusyBox
    - number: BusyBox hush violation 71.73±5.00 s over 30 runs
    - number: toybox sed violation 4.99±0.25 min
    - limits: Target machines are deterministic apart from input, have finite memory, and input functions provide at least one bit; terminal states are modeled as productive self-loops. The method can miss cycles involving huge counters, repeated allocations whose addresses change, and equivalent symbolic states whose serialized names differ. It inherits symbolic-execution path explosion and repeated SMT solving; sed reports intentional/interpreter-related violations and yes intentionally loops forever.

- XRP Ledger Consensus Protocol analysis (2018)
    - title: Analysis of the XRP Ledger Consensus Protocol
    - what: Gives a formal network model and safety/liveness analysis of XRP Ledger Consensus Protocol (XRP LCP), a Byzantine agreement protocol operating with individually chosen trusted node lists (UNLs). Defines nodes, asymmetric UNLs, quorum qi=ceil(.8ni), tolerated faults ti, ledgers and atomic-broadcast properties. It decomposes XRP LCP into deliberation, validation and preferred-branch components and provides pseudocode. Lemmas and propositions derive overlap conditions for ledger agreement: validation propagation through honest-majority support yields Theorem 8’s pairwise overlap bound. A constructed 102-node example shows that even 99% overlap can get stuck; Lemma 10 and Theorem 11 establish progress for a single agreed UNL with leaf validators under bounded-delay/no-fault rounds.
    - number: Theorem 8 safety condition: O_i,j > n_j/2 + n_i − q_i + t_i,j for every pair
    - number: with 80% quorums and 20% faults, roughly >90% UNL overlap is required
    - number: Example 9 demonstrates no progress despite 99% overlap and zero Byzantine faults
    - number: Theorem 11 guarantees no stuck state for a single agreed UNL of size at least k with q=n−floor((n−1)/k) plus leaves
    - limits: Safety is intended under arbitrary message asynchrony but assumes authenticated reliable channels and computationally bounded adversaries. Liveness only assumes eventual civil network delivery and no faulty nodes; FLP rules out full-asynchrony progress. Early analysis uses Byzantine accountability, explicitly rejected as tenuous; the broad theorem treats arbitrary deliberation outcomes, making the overlap condition sufficient rather than necessary. Results concern protocol rules and parameterized trust topology, not a measured deployment.

- CCSroll safety and liveness (2016)
    - title: A Safety and Liveness Theory for Total Reversibility?
    - what: Defines CCSroll, a CCS extension with totally ordered keyed reductions and programmed rollback, and characterizes safety and liveness preorders using forward traces, rollback barbs and tree refusals. The syntax/semantics records memories and dependency histories so a rollback of key k deterministically returns all later reductions to the state before k. The authors prove rollback determinism and rollback/forward correspondence, then identify two semantic properties enabling forward-only reasoning. Observable trace inclusion is shown sound and complete for inverse may-testing safety; a rollback-aware tree-refusal model is sound and complete for should-testing liveness. Examples M1–M6 show safety conservatively extends CCS while rollback distinguishes systems that CCS cannot distinguish; the paper also gives compositional LTS, trace zipping/unzipping, and characteristic tests.
    - number: two semantic properties underpin forward models
    - number: Theorem 1 safety conservativity
    - number: Theorem 3 trace soundness/completeness
    - number: Theorem 4 refusal soundness/completeness
    - limits: CCSroll reductions are totally ordered, rollback is controlled by explicit rlh keys, and proofs rely on the two stated reachability properties, including the difficult liveness property. Liveness should-testing is justified under a fairness assumption that infinitely enabled outputs eventually happen. Results are semantic theorems, not mechanized proofs or implementation measurements; the authors leave applicability of the second property to other causal-reversibility calculi open. The theory concerns CCS-like processes and does not model data, failures or real distributed timing.

    - what: Extends binary session types with required-response annotations and a typing system for a process calculus with communication, internal/external branching, bounded iteration, and unbounded recursion. Defines response-aware labeled transition systems and responsive traces; proves strict language expressiveness over standard session types using a type requiring both labels infinitely often; gives subject reduction and liveness typing rules tracking accumulated selections, invariants, and pending obligations through recursion and conditionals. The main proof uses syntactic under-approximation A(P), decomposition of parallel traces, fairness/lock-freedom, and shows every requested response eventually appears. A shopping-cart/invoice process and an infinite-state data object illustrate checkout→invoice and write→read obligations.

- SLRP liveness synthesis (2016)
    - title: Liveness of Randomised Parameterised Systems under Arbitrary Schedulers
    - what: Presents a fully automatic CEGAR method for liveness of randomised parameterised systems under arbitrary schedulers by reducing probabilistic protocols to two-player reachability games and synthesising regular progress relations with L*-learning and SAT. The method ignores nonzero probability values, retaining transition topology, and represents configurations as words with length-preserving transducers. Regular advice bits consist of an inductive reachable over-approximation and a well-founded progress relation; VERIFY checks four conditions while SYNTHESISE searches deterministic automata via SAT and eliminates counterexamples. An incremental variant composes disjunctive advice, refines reachable/winning sets, learns inductive invariants, and exploits automorphisms such as ring rotation. Soundness follows from the advice conditions; a completeness claim is given under existence of regular advice, minimum-length selection and minimum automata.
    - number: Lehmann–Rabin requires incremental plus invariant and rotation symmetry: 10 minutes versus 48 minutes without invariant
    - number: Israeli–Jalfon takes 9.7 s with all optimisations; Herman 1.5 s monolithic
    - number: Szymanski monolithic takes 5.7 s while incremental takes 27 min
    - number: Lehmann–Rabin run uses 23-state reachable DFA, 25 progress pairs and 4,324 refinement iterations
    - limits: Each finite instance is treated as an MDP whose liveness depends only on transition topology, so actual nonzero probabilities are abstracted away; this proves almost-sure reachability only under the chosen scheduler/game interpretation. Systems are encoded by finite-state letter-to-letter transducers, excluding non-length-preserving/data-rich protocols; fairness is not handled and is future work. Results are proof-of-concept on finite benchmark encodings, with hard-coded invariant precision n=5 and substantial SAT/refinement cost for complex cases.

- T2 (2016)
    - title: T2: Temporal Property Verification
    - what: Presents the open-source T2 framework, translating C/LLVM or native integer transition systems into safety, termination and temporal-logic proofs for CTL, Fair-CTL, CTL* and LTL reductions. T2 front-end builds location/transition graphs and uses LLVM simplifications. Safety proving uses Impact, Z3 GPDR or Spacer via linear Horn clauses. Termination alternates safety checks with lexicographic ranking-function synthesis and counterexample refinement; preprocessing removes directly provable loops (about 80%). Nontermination searches recurrent sets. CTL uses bottom-up precondition synthesis; Fair-CTL uses prophecy variables to reduce fairness; CTL* uses explicit future-outcome variables and is incomplete. Machine integer overflow is handled by normalization in the C front-end.
    - number: Termination benchmark timeout 300 s: AProVE 641 terminating/393 nonterminating/188 fail, avg 49.1 s; T2-GPDR 627/442/153, avg 23.6 s; T2-GPDR-NoP 589/438/195, 31.4 s; Spacer-NoP 591/429/202, 33.5 s; Impact-NoP 529/452/241, 37.2 s. Preprocessing removes ~80% loops. CTL 56/56 examples solved; T2 average 2.7 s vs Q’ARMC 3.6 s (100 s timeout).

Heap, recursion and concurrency are future work. Fair-CTL is relatively complete only relative to safety/termination procedures; CTL* is incomplete. - Automatic Completion of Distributed Protocols with Symmetry. (2015)
    - what: # Automatic Completion of Distributed Protocols with Symmetry (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Rajeev Alur, Mukund Raghothaman, Christos Stergiou, Stavros Tripakis and Abhishek Udupa, arXiv:1505.04409v1. The arXiv comments identify this as the “Full version of paper presented at CAV 2015.” The paper gives a solver-and-model-checker loop that fills missing guards and state updates in finite distributed protocol sketches, requiring determinism, symmetry, deadlock freedom, safety and liveness under supplied fairness assumptions. Its empirical results cover Peterson mutual exclusion, a four-process self-stabilizing line, and five missing cache-coherence corner cases. Guaranteed correctness is explicitly limited to the finite synthesized instance (Q1,Q2,Q6,Q13,W1,W2).  ## Reading and evidence scope  Read all 21 pages, sections 1–6, all displayed definitions/formulas, nine figures, the complete five-row Table 1, and all 31 bibliography entries. There is no appendix, checklist, numbered theorem/proof or numbered pseudocode algorithm. Figure 2 is the synthesis workflow; §4 gives textual algorithms. Individually inspected page renders 3,8,11,13,14,15,16,17,18,19, covering every figure, the table, protocol diagrams and the weakest-precondition formula. This does not claim visual inspection of all pages. References were read as bibliography entries, not recursively followed.  Retained the inventory PDF, raw/layout extractions and official arXiv metadata. The paper says the custom model checker is planned for future public release; it supplies no implementation URL. No implementation/model files or historical logs were acquired or read. A web-search tool request for code returned an error without results; this is not evidence that no code exists. No author contacted, author code executed/imported, checker/solver rerun, or model/checkpoint/serialized experiment data loaded. Source versions and reported runs were not independently reproduced (Q8,W1).  ## Problem and modeling assumptions  The designer provides extended state-machine sketches, not just an English request or temporal goal. Each process has finite control locations, finite typed variables/channels, an initial valuation, known and unknown typed functions, guarded transitions and fairness sets. All type cardinalities are required finite, including process-identity symmetric types and array domains. A completed function is initially a finite interpretation table. The environment is another specified machine with no unknown functions (Q3,Q4,Q6).  Composition synchronizes matching output/input channels and sequences their updates so the receiver sees the transmitted value; independent internal actions interleave. Buffered unordered communication in the cache example is modeled within these finite-state machines, not assumed to be an unbounded reliable network. The formal product assumes disjoint process state variables and output ownership. Safety means no reachable error location. Liveness means no infinite fair accepting run of the supplied monitors; deadlock freedom is separate. Thus a finite deadlock cannot pass just because no infinite violating run exists (Q3,Q4).  Weak fairness applies to a set of transitions: either the whole set is disabled infinitely often or some member occurs infinitely often. Strong fairness requires that a set enabled infinitely often has a member taken infinitely often. Fairness sets are attached to output/internal transitions; input behavior depends on the environment/composition. These assumptions matter to the guarantees. The paper argues that using only safety in synthesis permits trivial solutions that disable progress (Q2,Q3).  Symmetry means behavior is invariant under renaming values of the designated types. Permutations act on array entries and indices, channel/process parameters and states. Unknown functions are constrained to commute with renaming, f(π(d))=π(f(d)). This is stronger than merely using the same source text while permitting special treatment of one numeric identity. Quantifiers are expanded over finite domains for the solver. The discussion mentions other symmetry notions as possible extensions; the implementation/evaluation described uses the stated finite symmetry treatment (Q5,Q7).  The designer can restrict signatures to exclude irrelevant variables, fix known read-only updates, and add constraints across functions. The formal completion requires deterministic individual machines, a symmetric product, satisfaction of initial constraints, and a deadlock-free/safe/live composition. Determinism allows simultaneous input transitions only on distinct input channels. Guarantees concern that chosen environment, monitor and fairness model, not every implementation of the informal protocol name (Q6,Q7).  ## Algorithm and what its checks establish  The synthesis loop asks Z3 for interpretations satisfying current constraints, instantiates the protocol, then model-checks it. An erroneous candidate yields further necessary conditions excluding its counterexample or repairing the reason it violates the property. A successful interpretation may subsequently be converted to a symbolic expression using an enumerative syntax-guided synthesis solver. A symbolic grammar/output is therefore a later step; the initial solver does not directly synthesize arbitrary program syntax (Q6,Q10,Q13).  The custom checker combines a symmetry-reduced annotated quotient graph with permutation tracking in a threaded graph, then searches accepting strongly connected components under fairness. Tracking the renaming matters because a compressed path can stand for multiple original paths and fairness obligations must be adjusted accordingly. The described procedure removes states enabling unmet strong-fairness sets and recomputes components until it finds a fair accepting cycle or rules one out. The authors build on cited algorithms and describe the search as sound and complete; this paper supplies no separate numbered soundness/completeness proof or implementation certification. Their historical limitations of Spin, Murphi, SMC and NuSMV are reports about their tool attempts, not a current feature comparison (Q8).  For a deadlock, the next candidate must block the witnessed path or enable some outgoing action at its end. For a safety trace it must block the error path. The paper compiles product transitions to guarded commands, coalesces assignments and computes weakest preconditions; a guarded-command obligation uses guard implication, so a disabled step can break the trace. For liveness, a stem/cycle witness must become infeasible, fail to close, or become unfair. The Peterson example shows why simply banning the waiting loop is too strong: enabling a continuously neglected critical transition can make that loop unfair under the given fairness assumption (Q9,Q10).  There is a printed indexing problem worth preserving without treating it as an implementation defect. §4.3 indexes a trace cmd1,…,cmdn but defines pre_i using cmd_(n−i−1). With n=1 and i=1 this refers to cmd_−1 rather than the sole command. The intended reverse traversal would require a corrected index/convention; no source code was available to determine what the implementation uses. The rendered page 11 confirms the printed formula. The liveness recurrence also reuses notation inconsistently around the named union of fairness sets. These are presentation limits on mechanically following the text, not reproduced failures of the tool (Q10,D1).  Search heuristics prefer each error path to exercise few unknown functions while choosing diverse paths that collectively cover many. Shorter unknown-function dependencies can give stronger pruning constraints. Candidate costs favor minimally permissive guards, unchanged updates, then fewer input dependencies; bounds relax when the solver cannot satisfy them. Such constraints are search guidance, not protocol requirements. They strongly affect runtime, and Dijkstra required a different guard heuristic. No controlled ablation quantifies each optimization's benefit (Q11,Q12).  ## Concrete cases  Peterson has two processes with my/other identity parameters, shared flag/turn variables, a critical-section safety condition and a starvation monitor. Figure 1 removes the waiting/critical guards; the experiment additionally leaves flag-update expressions unspecified and requires that at least one guard hold. Weak fairness of selected non-input transitions suffices for the stated monitor. The authors report an interpretation in under one second and expressions matching the known protocol. Table 1 reports three unknown functions; this count is retained as printed rather than inferred by counting the distinct update sites mentioned in prose (Q3,Q12,T1).  The self-stabilizing case is four processes in a line, each with Boolean x/up state. The two middle processes have two unknown guarded commands, each with two unknown updates; corresponding middle-process completions must be identical. A legitimate state has exactly one globally enabled guarded command. The specification requires reaching legitimate states from arbitrary starts, staying legitimate thereafter, and reachability among legitimate states. The successful expressions match Dijkstra's displayed rules. This is synthesis/verification for N=4; the general-N illustration is not itself a parameterized correctness proof (Q12).  German/MSI has a directory, symmetric caches and one environment per cache; permissions are invalid/shared/exclusive. Its variant adds direct cache-to-cache data/acknowledgment, silent shared eviction and coordinated exclusive eviction. These optimizations create interactions absent from the simpler base protocol. One explicit race has a directory still listing a silently evicted cache as a sharer: that cache has already requested exclusive ownership when it receives an invalidation for another requester, and must acknowledge the invalidation despite its transient state. Two related races vary the pending read/write request (Q12,Q14).  Two remaining missing behaviors arise when exclusive eviction/writeback races with another cache's request: the cache receives an unexpected invalidation, while the directory receives unexpected writeback. The synthesized behavior treats the invalidation like a writeback acknowledgment for the evicting cache; the directory accepts the latest data and forwards it to the requester without requiring an extra acknowledgment. Figures 8/9 mark the missing behaviors and recovery messages. The designer still specifies which message kinds each unknown state must handle; §5.4 says this assistance was crucial to scaling. This is completion of supplied protocol structure, not autonomous discovery of all states, messages or correctness requirements (Q14,Q15).  ## Measured results and limits  Table 1 reports five configurations. Peterson: 3 unknown functions, search space 2^36, 60 states, 14 iterations, 97 ms SMT and 130 ms total. Dijkstra: 6 functions, 2^192 possibilities, about 2,000 states, 30 iterations, 27 s SMT and 64 s total. German/MSI-2: 16 functions, about 2^4700 possibilities, about 20,000 symmetry-reduced states, 217 iterations, 31 s SMT and 298 s total. German/MSI-4: 28 functions, about 2^7614, about 20,000 reduced states, 419 iterations, 898 s SMT and 1,545 s total. German/MSI-5: 34 functions, about 2^9000, about 20,000 reduced states, 525 iterations, 2,261 s SMT and 3,410 s total. The exponents were checked in the page render because the extraction flattens superscripts (T1).  The suffix -n counts the missing transitions among five corner cases, not the number of caches. The paper does not state the exact cache-count/channel-capacity/value-domain configuration used for these rows, preventing a precise independent reconstruction from the paper alone. All runs use a Linux desktop with a 3.4 GHz Intel Core i7 and 8 GB memory. Multiple SMT seeds were run and the worst runtimes reported, but the number of seeds, dispersion and exact tool versions are absent. There is no broad benchmark population or matched competing-system comparison. Long cases spend much time solving heuristic constraints; German/MSI-5's SMT fraction is 2261/3410≈66.3%, a reader calculation, not a new measurement (Q15,T1,D2).  The authors say synthesized symbolic expressions were parametric and worked on larger instances, but explicitly qualify that interpretations are “only guaranteed to be correct for the finite instance of the protocol”. No sizes/results for the larger checks or cutoff/induction proof is supplied. Symmetry reduces a fixed instance's state graph; it does not by itself prove correctness for every process count. Nor do these finite-state experiments establish crash/Byzantine tolerance or machine-code correctness (Q13).  ## Contribution to the requested topics  This is directly relevant distributed-protocol synthesis with safety and liveness checking, predating LLM-based agents. Its distinctive combination is finite extended state sketches, symmetry constraints, fine transition-set fairness, and automatically generated counterexample constraints. Its related-work claims distinguish earlier scenario completion, safety-only concurrent sketching, and template-based self-stabilization; these comparisons are attributed to the authors, without independently reading those cited works (Q2).  For Verus/TLA+/LLM-agent work, the transfer is a reader proposal: preserve explicit environment/fairness assumptions, use deadlocks and fair cycles as separate feedback, constrain identity symmetry and keep programmer-supplied structure visible in evaluation. An SMT table, a readable symbolic expression, a finite checked instance and a general verified protocol are different outputs. The paper measures the first stages and fixed-instance checks; it contains no Verus/Rust development, TLA+ model, LLM agent or general parameterized proof.
    - limits: Allfinite types;specifiedenvironment/monitors/fairness;humanprovidedstates/messagekinds;fixedinstancecorrectness;unknowncachecapacities/version/seeds;noimplementationread/rerun;printedrecurrenceindexproblemnotcodefailure.

- Fairness Modulo Theory LTL checking (2015)
    - title: Fairness Modulo Theory: A New Approach to LTL Software Model Checking
    - what: Introduces Fairness Modulo Theory, reducing LTL software verification to absence of feasible fair paths in a Büchi program and prioritizing finite-prefix infeasibility proofs before expensive infinite-path ranking proofs. Ultimate LTLAutomizer implements the CEGAR loop with SMT trace abstraction and termination analysis. The paper defines Büchi programs/products and proves that P satisfies φ iff the product with A¬φ has no trace both fair and feasible. The algorithm maintains an automaton of known infeasible fair traces, checks stem/loop/concatenated finite prefixes, then invokes ranking synthesis only when needed; refineF and refineω generalize proofs. Implementation uses ACSL C parsing, LTL2BA, trace abstraction, ranking synthesis and Büchi automata operations. It reports benchmark behavior on Windows/PostgreSQL/Apache, RERS and coolant sets, including product-size and refinement observations.
    - number: most comparison benchmarks solved under five seconds
    - number: 20-minute timeout and 8 GB memory for authors' runs; prior Term/DP data used four-hour timeout
    - number: roughly 43% of RERS benchmarks verified without modifications
    - number: Büchi product blow-up no more than four times on reported sets
    - limits: Finite-prefix feasibility and termination analyses are generally undecidable and can time out, yielding unknown. The approach depends on Büchi automaton translation and SMT theories, and ranking synthesis remains necessary for traces with no infeasible finite prefix. RERS verification succeeded on roughly 43% without modifications; a product optimization bug caused most abnormal results, and one Windows benchmark had undefined variables. Results are tool/benchmark dependent.

- LTL distributed synthesis (2015)
    - title: Synthesis of Self-Stabilising and Byzantine-Resilient Distributed Systems
    - what: Synthesizes synchronous fault-tolerant distributed algorithms from LTL specifications, covering strong self-stabilization and Byzantine faults. Fixed-size bounded synthesis is complete for realizable specifications and optimizes implementation size and stabilization time. Defines universal transition systems, local transition systems and a bounded-synthesis SMT encoding. Byzantine synthesis uses an incremental CEGIS-like loop with error traces. The paper proves completeness for fixed network size, presents sufficient classes for arbitrary network-size correctness, and reproduces Dolev two-counting while extending to n-counting. Colorless specifications and majority-based abstraction support parameterized reasoning.
    - number: fixed-size synthesis complete for realizable specs
    - number: two classes with arbitrary-size correctness
    - number: n-counting extension beyond 2-counting
    - number: 64 GiB/8-core evaluation hardware
    - limits: Synchronous reactive processes with finite local state and a specified communication/failure model. Fixed-size completeness does not imply arbitrary-size correctness; the two arbitrary-size classes require stated structural hypotheses. Byzantine synthesis can be expensive; experiments use sequential solver runs and finite bounds. LTL specifications capture the modeled behavior, not physical network timing or implementation bugs.

- Parallel explicit generalized Büchi checking (2015)
    - title: Parallel Explicit Model Checking for Generalized Büchi Automata
    - what: Develops parallel Tarjan- and Dijkstra-style SCC emptiness checks for transition-based generalized Büchi automata. Threads swarm DFS traversals while sharing only stable SCC/dead-state structure through a lock-free union-find augmented with acceptance marks; a mixed portfolio combines both strategies. The generic canvas defines shared stop/union-find state and local DFS, LIVE, livenum, and pstack structures. Tarjan propagates lowlinks on backtracking; Dijkstra merges partial SCCs on closing edges and detects acceptance earlier. Proof sketches establish safety of shared information and acceptance detection, while complexity analysis identifies union-find contention in large SCCs. Experiments with Spot and DiVinE compare 1–12 threads on BEEM products, separating empty (satisfied) and nonempty (violated) formulas and analyzing topology-dependent scaling.
    - number: 2–12 threads
    - number: 10 benchmark models per plot group
    - number: union-find operations m versus n−1 per SCC
    - number: 2015 TACAS prototype
    - limits: The automaton is finite and transitions carry generalized acceptance marks; correctness relies on lock-free compare-and-swap union-find and stable SCC/DEAD facts. Local traversals may redo work when another thread marks a state DEAD. Speedups depend strongly on SCC shape and union-find contention; prototype absolute times lag sequential CNDFS, and one comparison precomputes products for LTSmin due to a bug. The approach is explicit-state and does not address symbolic or unbounded systems.

- SACO (2014)
    - title: SACO: Static Analyzer for Concurrent Objects.
    - what: Presents SACO, a static analyzer for actor/concurrent-object programs that infers termination, resource bounds and deadlock freedom. Points-to and may-happen-in-parallel analyses feed circular termination reasoning, cost bounds and dependency-graph deadlock checks. The monitor/object model permits one active task per object, cooperative await, asynchronous sends and blocking future get. Allocation-site sequences parameterized by k give object-sensitive points-to information; polynomial MHP composition prunes impossible interleavings. Termination assumes shared data is updated finitely often and proves that assumption through termination of updating loops. Resource bounds combine local ranking functions with counts of interfering updates; deadlock graphs are pruned using MHP labels. The vending-machine example derives a coin+·n loop bound and an acyclic dependency graph.
    - number: points-to sequence bound k
    - number: retrieveCoins resource bound coin+ * n
    - number: one active task per object
    - limits: Soundness depends on finite allocation-site abstraction, safe MHP over-approximation, and the circular finiteness assumptions used in termination proofs. get may deadlock; the dependency graph is a sufficient acyclicity test and may report false positives. The paper is a concept/tool report without a broad runtime benchmark table, and analyses apply to the cooperative concurrent-object model rather than arbitrary shared-memory threads.

- Towards Modeling and Model Checking Fault-Tolerant Distributed Algorithms. (2013)
    - what: Models threshold-guarded fault-tolerant distributed algorithms in extended Promela, replacing explicit message channels by sent/received message counters for efficient fixed-size SPIN verification. Separates receive/compute/send within one atomic process step. Correct processes broadcast each message type at most once; sender identities are abstracted into counts. Byzantine/symmetric process effects become bounded nondeterministic increments, while omission/crash processes are explicitly modeled with weaker fairness. Status variables encode control flow, parameter constraints encode resilience, and quantified atomic propositions are expanded for fixed process counts. Includes full broadcast encoding and appendix fragments for symmetric, omission, crash, folklore broadcast and condition-based consensus.
    - number: 8 GB encoding comparison; channels exhaust at n=6, counters at n=9

    - number: folklore n=6 property F:1690 s,1819 MB
    - number: Paxos-like target would need at least 14 processes to tolerate one fault, remains future work
    - limits: Assumes processes can distinguish senders; Byzantine senders cannot forge others’ messages. Asynchronous fairness requires each correct process to run infinitely and sent messages to eventually arrive. Counter encoding relies on uniform sender treatment, finitely many message types, all-to-all sends and at most one send per type/run. This paper verifies fixed parameter instances; parameterized proofs and Paxos-like algorithms are separate/future work. Small counterexamples check adequacy of the encoding but do not establish full refinement equivalence. Source is the 21-page SPIN 2013 submission including appendices, not the publisher final layout; graphs were not separately inspected.

- TLAPS (2012)
    - title: TLA+ Proof System (TLAPS) and its temporal support
    - what: # TLA+ Proofs (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Denis Cousineau, Damien Doligez, Leslie Lamport, Stephan Merz, Daniel Ricketts and Hernán Vanzetto, 2012. This record reads the 16-page extended article, arXiv:1208.5933v1, dated 29 August 2012. Its first-page footnote says a shorter version appears at FM 2012; reference [7] gives LNCS 7436, pp 147–154. The official TLAPS publications page links both a longer article and a Peterson model (Q1,Q3,Q30,Q37). The shorter proceedings paper was not separately read. The inventory's cs.LO label is not the PDF's classification: the PDF says cs.SE (Q1).  The article explains how to write, decompose, check and maintain TLA+ proofs through Peterson's two-process mutual exclusion algorithm. Its central result is an illustrated invariant proof, backed by reported TLC and theorem-prover checks. The most important qualification is explicit: the displayed final temporal QED is “proof omitted” (Figure 6, Q19), because the then-current TLAPS could not check temporal reasoning (Q11). The state-level proof obligations are reported checked; the complete temporal theorem is justified by standard induction rules in the explanation, rather than entirely checked by the presented tool. Liveness is outside the demonstrated verification.  ## Reading and evidence scope  Read all 16 PDF pages in text and rendered images, including sections 1–6, all six figures, every displayed formula/proof and all 24 bibliography entries on pp 14–16. There is no appendix, checklist, numbered equation, numerical plot or printed table. The six figures contain the PlusCal algorithm, its TLA+ translation, high-level proof, inductive invariant, preservation-proof outline and hierarchical proof. This is one mutual-exclusion theorem target shown at successive levels of detail, not six independently evaluated algorithms.  The local inventory PDF and freshly fetched official arXiv PDF have identical bytes. Read the official TLAPS publications page and two complete plaintext Peterson modules from the official tlaplus/tlapm repository, pinned to commit 901ae51ed5224f88d43a1a02969cd9be4ed1b48d, dated 2026-10-16. One module is the publications-page artifact path; its bytes match the separately fetched live official model. The second is under doc/fm2012. These are retained example artifacts, not an audit of the current TLAPS implementation. The repository tree was inventoried to locate them. Other examples, proof-manager/backend implementation, modern tutorials, the shorter article and recursively cited case-study papers were not read. The modern repository pin does not establish the exact executable versions used in 2012.  No author code, PlusCal translator, TLC, TLAPS, Isabelle, Zenon or SMT solver was executed or imported. No model, serialized dataset or checkpoint was loaded. The reader checks are symbolic reasoning, file comparisons and metadata/arithmetic checks. quotes.json contains exact passages with section/page, LF lines and character slices; figure-evidence.json preserves every figure listing. numeric_tables.json explicitly records zero tables and the limited quantitative claims. The extracted listings often render □ as 2 and a prime as 0; quotes retain those literal extraction bytes, while the analysis uses the symbols confirmed in the rendered PDF. source-scope.json, source-files.json and hashes.json document the reading boundary and bytes.  ## Specification and assumptions  TLA+ combines linear-time temporal logic, Zermelo–Fraenkel set theory with choice, and modules. The proof language adds hierarchical natural-deduction structure. The Toolbox is an Eclipse-based interface to both TLC model checking and TLAPS proof checking, so the same formulas can be tested and used in deductive proofs (Q2). These descriptions concern the paper's 2012 tools; they are not a current feature inventory.  The example has exactly two process identifiers, 0 and 1, with Not exchanging them. Shared variables are a Boolean-valued flag function, a turn value initially 0, and the generated program-counter function pc. Initially both flags are false and both counters are at a0. Each process repeatedly sets its flag, gives the other process the turn, checks the other's flag and turn through two separate labeled tests, executes the critical-section skip, then clears its flag (Q5–Q8).  Atomicity is specified by PlusCal labels: an action runs from one label to the next. Figure 2 exposes seven action formulas per process, and Next existentially selects one process's action. Reads/writes and control changes have exactly the interleaving semantics of these formulas. The example supplies no weak-memory, compiler-reordering, machine-instruction, crash, recovery or network-message model. Correctness of a compiled Rust or C implementation would need a separate correspondence argument. The critical section itself is represented by an atomic skip; the paper's property is that the two program counters never simultaneously equal cs (Q6–Q9).  The full specification is Spec = Init ∧ □[Next]_vars, with vars collecting flag, turn and pc. Brackets admit either a Next transition or a step leaving all listed variables unchanged. A behavior is an infinite state sequence. Stuttering is included to simplify refinement, not to assert progress (Q7,Q8,Q10).  There is no fairness clause in the displayed Spec. A reader witness repeats the initial state forever: Init holds, every step satisfies UNCHANGED vars, and neither process ever reaches cs. Thus that exact Spec does not imply eventual entry into a critical section. This is compatible with its mutual-exclusion safety property. The paper says liveness can be expressed and proved in TLA+, but does not give the extra progress assumptions or a liveness proof here (Q4; math-witness.json). It would be wrong to infer starvation freedom merely from the safety theorem.  ## What is proved, and the checking boundary  The target is Spec ⇒ □MutualExclusion. Its three state-level obligations are Init ⇒ Inv, Inv ∧ [Next]_vars ⇒ Inv′, and Inv ⇒ MutualExclusion. Figure 3 and §3.1 explain how propositional reasoning plus temporal induction and monotonicity yield the temporal theorem from these premises. They then state: “TLAPS does not yet handle temporal reasoning, so we omit the proof of the qed step” (Q11). Figure 6 calls itself the “complete hierarchical proof” while visibly retaining that omission (Q19). This is a disclosed tool limitation, not evidence that the mutual-exclusion theorem is false.  Inv is TypeOK ∧ I. TypeOK constrains pc to seven labels per process, turn to {0,1}, and flag to Boolean-valued functions on {0,1}. I says a process from a2 through a4 has its flag set. A process at cs or a4 excludes the other from both cs and a4; if the other is still in either waiting-test location, turn favors the process already in that region (Q12). The stronger control-state conditions explain why the simple target predicate is preserved even across the entry/exit protocol.  Two small reader checks clarify the formula. Init satisfies TypeOK, while all control-state antecedents in I are false at a0. If pc[0]=cs, I for process 0 excludes pc[1]=cs; if pc[0]≠cs, MutualExclusion already holds. These checks cover initialization and implication to the target. They do not replace the preservation proof or independently certify the tool. TypeOK describes 7²×2×2²=392 possible valuations; that is a domain-size calculation, not the paper's reported 58 reachable states (math-witness.json).  The paper first reports TLC checking execution errors and MutualExclusion over all 58 reachable states of this particular finite model. It also checks inductiveness by replacing Init with Inv, model checking Inv ∧ □[Next]_vars. Starting from every state satisfying the candidate invariant tests preservation beyond the original reachable-state set. For more complicated or infinite-state specifications, the authors recommend small-instance model checking as a way to find errors, and explicitly limit its ability to establish general inductiveness (Q9,Q12). A successful finite instance is not a universal proof for arbitrary process counts or data domains.  The illustrated checks also reveal the human work. A leaf proof citing only Init and Inv fails because TypeOK and I remain uninterpreted. Supplying their definitions lets TLAPS prove initialization. Expanding definitions for the entire preservation obligation still fails with the default provers and produces a 65-line obligation. CVC3 through the SMT backend proves it “in a few seconds” (Q13–Q15). No hardware, precise solver versions, timeout, repeat count or full timing log accompanies that statement.  To demonstrate hierarchy rather than rely on this easy SMT success, the authors deliberately decompose the preservation proof using Zenon and Isabelle. The SUFFICES step replaces [Next]_vars with Next only after separately proving the unchanged-variable case. It then splits Inv′ into TypeOK′ and I′. Zenon proves the SUFFICES reduction; Zenon fails on the stated TypeOK′ proof but Isabelle succeeds. Both fail on I′ without further decomposition (Q16–Q17).  The next level introduces an arbitrary process j, names its invariant subexpression as I!(j), and picks the process i that performs the Next action. Instead of seven action-by-action cases, two cases, i=j and i≠j, suffice for the displayed example. Explicit facts and definition expansions then discharge the leaves. A case step's own name denotes its case assumption, so citing that name within its proof is not circular citation of its conclusion. NEW-bound assumptions and the PICK membership fact are automatically available within scope; ordinary facts still need the stated context discipline (Q18–Q19).  ## Proof management, reuse and trust  The Proof Manager translates proof steps into sequents with a goal and context of symbols, assumptions and previously established facts. It tracks context changes and sends leaf obligations to backends. It records success, failure, cancellation and omission. The interface can check one step, theorem or file, show the exact failing obligation, navigate back to the producing proof, and hide irrelevant portions of the hierarchy (Q21–Q22).  “Proof obligations are independent of one another” describes the ability to check conditional obligations in any order (Q21). It does not make a theorem valid while required assumptions or earlier steps remain unproved. The distinction matters in this very example: the state-level work can be developed independently while the final temporal QED is omitted. Global completion requires all needed obligations and the correct bridge to the stated property.  Fingerprinting stores an obligation's status under a canonical representation. Bound variables become de Bruijn indices, so renaming them does not change the representation; unused symbols and hypotheses are removed from the context. This supports reuse after proof reorganization, moving a step or removing and later reinserting it. Status can also be queried without launching proofs (Q23). The paper describes the mechanism but gives no hash-collision theorem, implementation-level soundness proof or controlled cache benchmark. Correct reuse depends on the representation preserving all relevant logical dependencies. This record did not inspect the fingerprint implementation.  The backends described are Isabelle/TLA+, Zenon and SMT solvers. Default attempts use Zenon, then Isabelle; the SMT route translates obligations into SMT-LIB for CVC3, with native Yices/Z3 alternatives. Isabelle is the most trusted backend. Zenon can emit Isar proofs that Isabelle checks. The paper says certification of SMT proof traces is future work, so a successful SMT result should not be silently counted as Isabelle-certified evidence in this article (Q3,Q15,Q27).  The trusted components also include translations and critical Proof Manager transformations such as distributing prime through addition. Users can disable fingerprints and reprove obligations, which addresses cache reuse concerns without removing the other trust dependencies. The authors further warn that an incorrect target formula can make a proof meaningless and that incorrect assumed mathematical facts can invalidate an apparent result. TLC can test those exact assumed formulas on instances; that supplies error-finding evidence, not a proof of every assumed mathematical statement (Q27–Q28).  ## Evaluation and concrete larger cases  The evaluation is a worked proof tutorial plus development experience, not a randomized user study or comparative prover benchmark. Its concrete local evidence comprises the 58-state TLC result, the failed/repaired leaf proofs, the 65-line preservation obligation, backend-specific outcomes and the qualitative CVC3 timing. There are no statistical estimates, repeated trials, task success rates, hardware budgets or uniform comparison across proof assistants (Q9,Q13–Q19).  Larger uses are named: Byzantine Paxos, the Memoir security architecture, and Pastry lookup/join protocols. These are cited case studies, not complete proofs reproduced in this article (Q20). A cited 1100-line invariance proof has 100 non-leaf steps, illustrating the scale at which the hierarchy acts like nested lemmas (Q22). The claim that 100 conventional linear lemmas would be unreadable is the authors' judgment, not a measured readability result.  The strongest maintenance anecdote concerns Lamport's Byzantine Paxos proof. He initially spent several weeks over several months on its safety proof while debugging TLAPS. Later he discovered the algorithm lacked the desired liveness property, changed it, repaired small bugs found by TLC, and re-established safety in a day and a half, about 12 hours of actual work. About three quarters of the new proof obligations had already been proved (Q24). This shows reported reuse during a real revision and separately shows why safety does not imply liveness. It does not report a newly checked liveness proof, an exact obligation count, or an isolated speedup attributable solely to fingerprinting.  The related-work discussion compares declarative proofs and interface features with Mizar/Isar, reuse with KIV, set-theoretic/refinement approaches with Rodin/Event-B, and high-level specifications with VCC/Why source-code verification. These are architectural comparisons and historical author assessments, not present-day benchmark rankings (Q25). The conclusion also lists proof constructs not exercised in the Peterson example, including existential witnesses, local definitions and facts supplied implicitly to backends. Temporal reasoning and improved sequence support are planned work in the article (Q26,Q29).  ## Static artifact findings  Both pinned Peterson modules contain the full PlusCal comment, generated actions, candidate invariant, ISpec for inductiveness testing, and two variants of the same safety proof. One variant uses SMT for preservation; the other expands the hierarchy. Both end their top-level theorem with PROOF OMITTED (Q31–Q35). This independently preserves the article's disclosed checking boundary in a usable example artifact. The source exists; no dynamic proof certificate or successful replay is supplied by this read.  The artifact retains ProcSet and uses USE DEF ProcSet, matching the paper's footnote about a definition inlined in Figure 2. MutualExclusion uses negated conjunction, logically equivalent to the paper's disjunction. Source leaf proofs often cite Inv, Next and proc(i) directly where Figure 6 cites numbered steps; the explanatory text explicitly permits such fact references (Q7,Q17,Q32–Q35). The FM directory copy differs only by an unused NeverCS predicate and DEF versus DEFS spelling in one leaf proof (Q36 and retained file comparison). Neither difference establishes an algorithm change or runtime failure. The two files remain exact downloaded bytes.  ## Contribution to the requested research topics  This is directly relevant to formal verification of distributed systems and TLA+: it gives a concrete path from a labeled algorithm to a temporal specification, tested invariant, structured proof obligations, backend checks and maintained proof status. It makes both the specification assumptions and proof-completion boundary visible. Its larger-system anecdotes link those mechanisms to distributed protocols while leaving those cited proofs outside this article's detailed evidence.  It contains no Verus or verified-Rust development, LLM agent, generated-specification benchmark or automatic invariant-synthesis evaluation. Its transferable design ideas are therefore reader proposals: give proof agents explicit sequents and scoped context; use checker feedback to distinguish missing definitions from difficult obligations; split proofs without dropping stuttering or other cases; cache normalized obligations only with valid dependencies; and report omitted steps and certification routes alongside success counts. The article does not measure those proposals with LLMs. Its most useful evaluation lesson is to keep a correct formalization, state-level proof success, temporal completion, liveness, certificate checking and proof-reuse evidence separate.
    - number: Peterson mutual-exclusion example; proof obligations are split among back-end provers
    - limits: Exactly two processes and labeled atomic shared-state interleavings with arbitrary stuttering; no fairness/weakmemory/compiledimplementation correspondence. Reported state-level proof checks leave temporalQED explicitly omitted in2012tools. No liveness proof or fullTLAPSsoundness proof. Larger case studies cited; maintenance/timing evidence is anecdotal.2026modulepin does not identify2012executables.

    - title: Verifying Liveness Properties of ML Programs
    - what: Extends intersection-type model checking of higher-order recursion schemes from safety to Alternation Free Mu-Calculus/CTL, enabling automated liveness checking of higher-order functional programs. T HORS partially evaluates a HORS abstraction, heuristically proposes candidate intersection types, type-checks them, and solves a weak Buchi/parity game to establish acceptance. The paper explains a two-copy complement strategy for non-deterministic automata (termination on yes instances, possible divergence on no instances otherwise), and uses a CPS/resource-access translation for ML programs.

Fairness is imposed for unbounded file reads by excluding infinite reads. Worst-case complexity remains n-EXPTIME by function order, and no broad benchmark or automated front end is supplied.

- Robust GR(1) liveness robustness (2010)
    - title: Robustness in the Presence of Liveness
    - what: Defines robustness for liveness specifications by requiring a minimum number of system guarantees to remain true as environment assumptions fail. It reduces single and multiple counting requirements to Generalized Reactivity (GR) objectives and gives verification and synthesis algorithms for robust systems. The paper develops concrete robustness measures using dining philosophers, formalizes them as ordered sets of GR(1)-like implications, and shows that disjunctions of conjunctions of Büchi objectives can be exponentially simplified to generalized Büchi objectives. It proves DCB game solving co-NP-complete via a 3SAT reduction, then gives a counter-based construction reducing GR(1) games to one-pair Streett games. The construction preserves winning strategies, extends to GR(k), and yields verification and synthesis complexity bounds; synthesized strategies trade lower runtime for larger memory than Zielonka’s method.
    - number: GR(1) verification O(mn|Q|^2|δ|)
    - number: GR(1) synthesis O(|S||E|(mn)^2)
    - number: GR(k) synthesis O(|S|^k|E|(mn)^{k(k+1)}k!)
    - number: DCB game winning strategy problem co-NP-complete
    - limits: Specifications are finite products of deterministic Büchi automata over input/output signals and robustness is measured by counts of assumptions and guarantees, with a user-supplied total order. The model is reactive infinite-play games; liveness violations cannot be detected finitely, so safety guarantees cannot generally be sacrificed in response to a fairness-assumption violation. DCB-to-generalized-Büchi simplification is exponential and DCB game solving is co-NP-complete. The proposed GR(1) algorithm uses O(|S||E|(mn)^2) time but stores (m+1)(n+1) memory states (or n with an extra strategy computation); GR(k) incurs factorial/exponential factors. No implementation benchmark is reported beyond the worked examples and asymptotic analysis.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `robust-gr-1-liveness-robustness-2010`
- Termination Analysis with Compositional Transition Invariants (2010)
    - what: Introduces Compositional Termination Analysis (CTA), replacing repeated expensive whole-program safety checks in Binary Reachability Analysis with ranking of loop-free unwindings and a transitivity check on a disjunctively well-founded candidate transition invariant. Increasing unwinding depth and removing already-ranked transitions permits short terminating fragments to yield a global termination argument. §2 defines transition invariants as supersets of transitive reachable transition closure, and disjunctive well-foundedness as finite unions of well-founded relations. §3 observes a transitive disjunctively well-founded relation is well-founded. Lemmas 1/2 accumulate rankings of increasingly deep, not-yet-covered unwindings and allow skipped intermediate depths; the implementation uses powers of two. Algorithm 1 tracks an overapproximation X of reachable states. A failed ranking produces a candidate nontermination precondition C: reachable C establishes nontermination; unreachable C is removed from X. Thus reachability checking remains where ranking fails rather than in every refinement. §4 slices each loop independently, havocs other loops and assignments depending on five or more variables, and uses CBMC symbolic execution to discover paths in loop-free fragments instead of enumerating all of them.
    - number: 87 drivers ×2 configurations =174 driver benchmarks
    - number: matched nontrivial successful BRA baseline: 99 terminating and 45 nonterminating cases
    - number: 3 GHz Xeon; 2 GB memory limit; 2 h per driver
    - number: precise CTA: average speedup 52; up to 3 orders of magnitude; more than 3× analyzed benchmarks at equal precision under the limit
    - limits: Soundness depends on the rank subprocedure returning a disjunctively well-founded superset or a genuine nontermination precondition, and on valid reachability checks. Finite-state nontermination completeness requires a complete rank procedure, unlike the implemented incomplete engine. Per-loop slicing/havoc overapproximates behavior and can lose termination proofs; practical heuristics replace assignments depending on at least five variables. Evaluations compare identical abstracted slices, not untouched drivers. Baseline is selected from BRA-successfully-analyzed, nontrivial loops, so speedup figures are conditional on that subset. Fast variants skip path-precondition checking or all reachability checking, potentially reporting false nontermination outside these benchmarks; the paper reports no precision loss on its measured examples. No scheduler fairness or busy-waiting progress argument is supplied for concurrent programs.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Automatic Verification of Probabilistic Concurrent Finite-State Programs (1985)
    - what: Gives an automata-theoretic decision procedure for qualitative, probability-one satisfaction of linear temporal specifications by finite probabilistic concurrent programs. Converts formulas to omega automata and reduces probabilistic emptiness/universality to ordinary automata emptiness through fair-run reasoning. For deterministic Streett automata, constructs product automata whose acceptance enforces probabilistic fairness. Develops a generalized-subset determinization based on labeled state-set tuples; a singly exponential partial construction avoids full doubly exponential determinization for probabilistic Büchi checks. Extends pure Markov chains to concurrent Markov chains with probabilistic versus nondeterministic states and a designated fair-state set. A history-dependent scheduler induces a probability measure; correctness quantifies over every fair scheduler.
    - number: probability-one qualitative correctness
    - number: 12 scanned pages, printed 327–338
    - number: PSPACE-complete nondeterministic Büchi probabilistic emptiness/universality
    - number: Theorem 4.5: polynomial program-size, doubly exponential specification-size time
    - limits: Finite state space and fixed process count are essential; the method does not establish a parameterized result for arbitrary process counts. Only qualitative almost-sure satisfaction is checked, not numerical probability thresholds, bounded waiting or real-time response. Algorithms depend on nonzero transition support rather than exact positive probabilities. Fairness is state-based: repeatedly visited fair states must take every enabled outgoing choice infinitely often; a fair scheduler assigns fair runs probability one. Source includes proof sketches rather than mechanized proofs or implementation evidence. Abstract/Theorem 4.5 give polynomial program-size time and doubly exponential specification-size time; introduction/conclusion call the program dependence linear. This internal bound wording difference is preserved. Author-hosted scan matches title/author and FOCS 1985 printed pages/metadata; no DOI is printed in the scan.
    - how much was read: most of it read
